```text
 ----------             ------------------    -----------------    -------------
 example:               example:              example:             example:
 Harappan ivory         Mughal Sheesh         Mizo women weaving   a GI-registered
 dice exported;         Mahal glasswork,      the Puan; Rabari     craft sold to
 PGW glass beads        hukkas, ittardans;    Kothi grain rooms    an export or
 (1000 BC); bronze      Himroo for royal                           tourist market
 Dancing Girl           dress
 (c. 2500 BC)

 THE FATAL MOVE: using a Class A date as the age of a Class C practice.
 "Indian cotton weaving is five thousand years old" merges an
 archaeological material claim with a living practice claim, and has
 asserted continuity that no evidence in this session supports.
```

Each column can be evidenced; no column can be used to date another. The four-class split is the chronology control for the whole topic.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Craft is the one cultural topic where antiquity is constantly asserted and almost never dated. The reason is that the sources genuinely do carry deep chronologies - and they carry them for *materials and techniques*, which is not the same as carrying them for *present-day practices*. This lesson separates the two so that you can write "the technique is attested from period X; the present practice is recorded at centre Y; the two are connected by inference, not by evidence" - a sentence that is both honest and, in an examiner's eyes, sophisticated.

### 2. Class A: the excavated artefact, and what the record actually says

The handicrafts source gives several genuine archaeological chains, and they are worth holding exactly because they are the ones an examiner can test.

**Glass.** The first reference to glass making is in the **Mahabharata**. The first evidence appears in the **Painted Grey Ware culture of the Ganges Valley (1000 BC)**, where glass beads were discovered. The Vedic text **Shatapatha Brahmana** uses the term **kanch or kaca** for glass. Archaeological evidence of a **glass industry** has been found at **Brahmapuri and Kolhapur in Maharashtra**, operational between the **2nd century BC and the 2nd century AD**, producing **lenticular beads**. The Sanskrit text **Vyasayogacharita** refers to early spectacles. In the south, glass has been found at **Maski in Karnataka**, a **Chalcolithic** site in the Deccan, and glass remains have been found at **Ahar, Hastinapur, Ahichchhatra, Eran, Ujjain and Firozabad**.

Read what that chain does and does not establish. It establishes a *material* - glass - present in India across a long sequence, with one location described as an industry and one product, the lenticular bead, named. It does not establish that any present-day glass centre descends from any of those sites. The source separately lists the **current** facets of the industry - bangles, associated with Hyderabad's *churi ka jodas*; chandeliers at **Firozabad**; toys at **Saharanpur**; **Tikuli beads** at **Patna**. Firozabad appears in both lists, which is a genuine and striking overlap, and the honest sentence is that Firozabad appears among the sites with glass remains and is today a chandelier centre - not that Firozabad has made glass continuously for two thousand years, which is an inference the source does not license.

**Textiles.** Manufacturing of fine textile varieties can be traced back to the **Indus Valley period**, which had a well-developed textile industry. Excavations show **early cotton cultivation and textiles**, and details on fabrics are mentioned in the **Rigveda**. The art of **weaving and dyeing** was **perhaps** well-established at that time - and that hedge, *perhaps*, is part of the fact and must be carried. The evidence is **paintings, sculptures and inscriptions**. Archaeological findings at **Harappa and Chanhudaro** suggest the existence of **sericulture (silk farming)** and the use of **wild silk** during the **3rd millennium BC**. Evidence of textiles and embroidery has been found in the **Ajanta murals** and in **miniature paintings**.

Notice the evidentiary structure, because it is instructive: for the earliest period the evidence is often *indirect* - not surviving cloth, but depictions of cloth, impressions, and references to it. Cloth rots. That single physical fact explains why textile archaeology leans on painting, sculpture, inscription and residue, and why a confident statement about what a Harappan garment looked like is weaker than it sounds.

**Metal and toys.** The **bronze statue of a Dancing Girl from Mohenjo-daro**, dated to around **2500 BC**, is the source's anchor for bronze work. For toys, the **first evidence of toy carts** comes from the **Harappan civilisation** and continues into the early historic period: **copper, bronze and terracotta toy carts** have been found at Harappan sites including **Alamgirpur, Ambkheri, Kalibangan and Lothal**, and similar cart models at early historic sites including **Atranjikhera, Nagarjunakonda, Sambhar, Sonapur, Ujjain and Brahmpuri**. For ivory, Harappan **dice** were exported to **Turkmenistan, Afghanistan and the Persian Gulf**.

**Leather and tanning.** The art of **leather tanning** is recorded as having existed since **3000 BC**; the animals whose skins were first tanned are given as **tigers and deer**, with **camels** becoming the most popular later.

### 3. Class B: the court and classical craft

The second class is defined by a patron. Its evidence is different in kind: texts, inscriptions, patron records and surviving high-value objects.

- **Glass under the Mughals** flourished, seen in monuments such as the **Sheesh Mahal**, and in items such as **hukkas**, perfume boxes (**ittardans**) and **engraved glasses**.
- **Himroo** material was used for **royal dresses and shawls** in the courts of Muslim rulers during the medieval period; Himroo shawls are made at **Aurangabad, Maharashtra**.
- **Chamba Rumals** were embroidered under the **patronage of the rulers** of the region, and show **Pahari painting influence**, embroidered with **Krishna themes and nature**, and were **often given as wedding gifts**.
- The **Kani shawl** of Jammu and Kashmir is recorded as made of delicate **pashm** from the underbelly of wild Tibetan and related goats.
- **Nirmal** painting, furniture, toy and craft work in Telangana is recorded as having evolved under a named **dynasty**, with a precise process finished in **varnish**.
- The **ivory guild at Vidisha** appears in a **Sanchi inscription of the 2nd century BC** - a guild is not a court, but it is the same class of evidence: an institution attested in a document rather than a practice observed today.

The value of Class B for answer-writing is that it supplies **named patrons and named markets**, which are the raw material of the argument that craft aesthetics follow patronage. It also supplies the clearest cases of technique migration - **Tanchoi** silk arriving via trade, inspired by Chinese weaving, and being perfected at Varanasi.

### 4. Class C: the living folk and tribal tradition

This is the class most often mishandled, and the mishandling has a characteristic shape: the practice is described as timeless, the community as unchanging, and the motif as symbolic of something the writer has supplied.

What the evidence actually supports is narrower and more useful. The source records, in the present tense, that the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** tribes of Meghalaya are skilled weavers known for handwoven fabrics; that **eri silk is commonly produced** in that region; that the tribes weave **shawls, sarongs and mekhela chadors** using **both silk and cotton**. It records that in **Mizoram, women are primarily responsible for weaving** the **Puan**, which in Mizo means cloth decorated with exclusive needlework in the shape of a stripe or an arrow, worn by **both men and women**. It records that **each tribe of Nagaland has its unique patterns and motifs which reflect their cultural identity**, with vibrant colours, intricate designs and **natural dyes**. It records the **Gamit, Vasava and Dholiya Patel** communities of southern Gujarat using bamboo **Moshti** grain containers, the **Nayaka** community of Godhara and Panchmahal using the mud-and-dung-plastered **Porasi**, and the **Rabari** of Kutch using the **Kothi**, a room built with a large door for pouring grain in and a small outlet at the bottom for taking it out.

Every one of those is a statement about a **current practice by named people**, which is exactly what Class C evidence can carry. Three disciplines follow, and they are non-negotiable in this session:

1. **Do not date a Class C practice from a Class A material.** That weaving, dyeing and silk use are attested in the third and second millennia BC says nothing about how long any particular community has woven any particular garment.
2. **Do not freeze the community.** A living tradition changes - in fibre, in dye, in colour range, in product mix, in who is allowed to make it and in whom it is sold to. Recording change is accuracy, not disrespect. The source itself notes natural dyes in one state and vibrant colours in another without implying that either is permanent.
3. **Do not invent meaning.** Where the source says a motif reflects cultural identity, write that. Where it says nothing, write nothing. A stripe or an arrow on a Puan is recorded as a form of needlework, not as a coded message, and supplying the code is fabrication.

### 5. Class D: contemporary design, and why it belongs in the topic at all

The fourth class is the craft object as it is made and sold now - in a cooperative, for an exporter, for a designer's collection, for a tourist counter, for an online listing. It has no evidentiary value for history whatsoever, and it is nonetheless central to the topic, for two reasons. First, it is where the **livelihood** half of the source's own definition actually lives. Second, it is where the **status** vocabulary operates: a registration, a promotional campaign, a fair, a pavilion and a certification are all Class D facts. An answer that discusses safeguarding without discussing the contemporary market has discussed the heritage half of the definition and dropped the livelihood half.

### 6. The six clocks, and how to label a date

Whenever a year or a period appears in this topic, name the noun it belongs to before using it.

| Clock | What the date fixes | Example from this session |
|---|---|---|
| **Material clock** | when a material is attested | glass beads in Painted Grey Ware, 1000 BC; sericulture at Harappa and Chanhudaro, 3rd millennium BC |
| **Technique clock** | when an operation is attested | a glass industry at Brahmapuri and Kolhapur, 2nd century BC to 2nd century AD; leather tanning from 3000 BC |
| **Institution clock** | when an organisation is attested | the ivory guild at Vidisha, Sanchi inscription, 2nd century BC |
| **Patronage clock** | when a patron sustained the craft | Mughal glassware; Himroo for medieval royal dress |
| **Practice clock** | when and where the craft is recorded as practised now | Mizo women weaving the Puan; Firozabad chandeliers; Saharanpur toys |
| **Status clock** | when an authority recorded something about it | the GI Act of 1999, in force 2003; Darjeeling Tea's registration as India's first GI in 2004-05 |

The rule for writing: **state the number, then state its noun.** "The GI Act came into force in 2003" is safe. "Bidri is a thousand years old" is not, because no clock has been named and no source in this session supplies the figure.

### 7. Comparative treatment: why the four-class split beats a simple ancient-to-modern timeline

A timeline implies succession - that Class A became Class B, which became Class C, which is now becoming Class D. The evidence does not support that, and three counter-cases show why.

- **Household craft never stopped.** The Gujarat storage containers are a living household technology, not a survival from an earlier stage. Class C runs parallel to Class B, not after it.
- **Court craft did not simply die into folk craft.** Himroo, made for medieval royal dress, is recorded as still being made at Aurangabad. The patron changed; the craft did not descend a ladder.
- **Contemporary design does not replace anything.** A registered, exhibited, exported craft is the same craft in a new market, and its Class D facts sit alongside its Class C practice rather than superseding it.

So the four classes are **four kinds of evidence available at the same moment**, not four historical periods. That is precisely why they can be confused, and precisely why keeping them apart is worth marks.

### Examination use

The four-class split is the **chronology defence** for the whole topic and the thing that lets you answer an "antiquity and continuity" demand without either exaggerating or being evasive. The safe formulation, usable almost verbatim: *"The material and the technique are attested from [named archaeological or textual evidence]; the practice is recorded today at [named centre or community]; the connection between them is an inference of continuity rather than a documented lineage."* The **six-clock table** is the direct defence against the classic statement-based item that attaches a real date to the wrong noun. The glass sequence is the single most examinable antiquity chain in this topic and should be retained with all five of its anchors - Mahabharata, Painted Grey Ware, Shatapatha Brahmana, Brahmapuri and Kolhapur, and the southern and northern find-spots.

### Traps introduced here

- Using an archaeological date as the age of a living practice.
- Dropping the hedge: the source says weaving and dyeing were *perhaps* well established in the Indus period, and the hedge is load-bearing.
- Treating a find-spot as an ancestor of a modern centre because the place name is the same.
- Assuming that a court craft "became" a folk craft when the court disappeared.
- Describing a tribal tradition in the past tense, which quietly asserts that it has ended.
- Supplying meanings for motifs that no source records.

### Revision notes

1. Four age-classes: **excavated artefact, court or classical craft, living folk and tribal tradition, contemporary design.** Four evidence types, four proof limits.
2. **Glass chain:** first reference in the **Mahabharata**; first evidence in the **Painted Grey Ware culture of the Ganges Valley, 1000 BC** (glass beads); **Shatapatha Brahmana** term **kanch/kaca**; industry at **Brahmapuri and Kolhapur, Maharashtra, 2nd century BC-2nd century AD**, making **lenticular beads**; **Vyasayogacharita** on early spectacles; **Maski (Karnataka)**, a Chalcolithic Deccan site; remains at **Ahar, Hastinapur, Ahichchhatra, Eran, Ujjain, Firozabad**.
3. **Current glass facets:** bangles (Hyderabad's *churi ka jodas*), chandeliers (**Firozabad**), toys (**Saharanpur**), **Tikuli beads** (**Patna**).
4. **Textile antiquity:** Indus Valley fine textiles; early cotton cultivation; fabrics mentioned in the **Rigveda**; weaving and dyeing **perhaps** well-established; **sericulture and wild silk at Harappa and Chanhudaro, 3rd millennium BC**; textile and embroidery evidence in **Ajanta murals** and **miniature paintings**.
5. **Bronze anchor:** the **Dancing Girl from Mohenjo-daro**, around **2500 BC**. **Toy anchor:** Harappan **toy carts** in copper, bronze and terracotta at **Alamgirpur, Ambkheri, Kalibangan, Lothal**; early historic models at **Atranjikhera, Nagarjunakonda, Sambhar, Sonapur, Ujjain, Brahmpuri**.
6. **Ivory anchor:** Harappan **dice** exported to **Turkmenistan, Afghanistan, the Persian Gulf**; **ivory guild at Vidisha**, Sanchi inscription, **2nd century BC**; comb from **Taxila**.
7. **Leather anchor:** tanning from **3000 BC**; first **tigers and deer**, later **camels**.
8. **Court class:** Mughal glass (**Sheesh Mahal, hukkas, ittardans, engraved glasses**); **Himroo** for medieval royal dress, made at **Aurangabad**; **Chamba Rumal** under royal patronage, Pahari influence, Krishna themes, wedding gifts; **Kani** shawl from **pashm**.
9. **Living class:** Meghalaya's **Garo, Khasi, Jaintia** weavers and **eri silk**; Mizoram's women weavers and the **Puan**; Nagaland's tribe-specific motifs and **natural dyes**; Gujarat's **Moshti, Porasi, Kothi** with named communities.
10. **Six clocks:** material, technique, institution, patronage, practice, status. State the number, then state its noun.
11. The four classes are **concurrent kinds of evidence**, not successive periods. Household craft, court craft and market craft coexist.
12. Cloth decays, so early textile evidence is largely **indirect** - depiction, impression, reference - which is why confident statements about ancient garments are weaker than they look.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Antiquity of material is not continuity of practice." Discuss this proposition with reference to Indian handicrafts. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Indian craft sources supply genuine deep chronologies, but they are chronologies of **materials and techniques**, not of living workshops.

Glass illustrates both halves. The material is attested from the Painted Grey Ware culture of the Ganges Valley around 1000 BC, named as *kanch* in the Shatapatha Brahmana, and worked as an industry at Brahmapuri and Kolhapur between the second century BC and the second century AD. Firozabad appears among sites with glass remains and is today a chandelier centre. The two facts are separately true; a lineage joining them is not documented.

The same discipline applies to cloth. Sericulture and wild silk are suggested at Harappa and Chanhudaro in the third millennium BC; weaving and dyeing were *perhaps* well established. Neither statement dates any present weaving community.

The defensible formulation names the evidence for the material, names the centre for the practice, and marks the link between them as inference.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = one fully dated material chain reproduced accurately 4 + one explicit statement of what it does not prove 3 + the safe formulation applied to a second material 3.** An answer that asserts unbroken continuity forfeits the middle three marks however well the chain is recalled.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two statements are put to you. (i) "The bronze Dancing Girl of Mohenjo-daro shows that Dhokra casting has been practised continuously for 4,500 years." (ii) "Himroo is a dead court craft of the medieval Deccan." Explain what is wrong with each, and give a defensible version of both.

**Model answer:** Statement (i) commits the **Class A to Class C** error. The Dancing Girl, dated to around 2500 BC, is an excavated artefact, and what it evidences is that bronze - an alloy of copper and tin, the earliest non-ferrous metals used - was being cast with skill in the Harappan world. It evidences nothing about any named living casting community, and "continuously" asserts a lineage for which no documentary chain is offered. A defensible version: bronze casting is attested in India from around 2500 BC by the Mohenjo-daro figure, and non-ferrous lost-wax casting is today a living practice associated with artisan communities in central and eastern India, including the registered Bastar tradition; the two facts are separate, and the second is not dated by the first. Statement (ii) commits the opposite error, the **Class B to past-tense** slide. Himroo is recorded as material used for royal dresses and shawls in medieval Muslim courts, *and* as shawls that remain very popular and are made at Aurangabad in Maharashtra. A patron disappearing does not kill a craft; it changes who buys it. A defensible version: Himroo originated as a court fabric for medieval royal dress and continues to be produced at Aurangabad for a present-day market. Both corrections use the same move - identify which class of evidence the claim rests on, and refuse to let it speak for another class.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking that the four classes are four periods arranged in sequence, so that a craft "passes" from archaeology into court patronage into folk practice into the modern market. They are four kinds of evidence available simultaneously. A single craft can hold facts in all four columns at once, and each fact must be used only for what its own column can prove.

## Lesson 4 - Fibre before fabric: what spinning actually does, and the fibres India works

Progress: 4/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the ginning-spinning-weaving sequence, cotton, the silks including muga and eri, wool and pashm, the khadi record, and why the fibre decides the fabric's behaviour

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the cloth-handicrafts opening on Indus textiles and sericulture, the North-East section's statement of the traditional weaving sequence and its fibre records, the Kani shawl pashm entry, and the khadi box in full
CA search: "Not applicable - materials and process lesson; the session's single current record is held for Lesson 18"
CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: from fleece, boll or cocoon to a yarn that can hold tension

```text
  RAW FIBRE                     WHY IT MATTERS
  ---------                     --------------
  COTTON  (seed hair)           short staple; absorbent; takes mordant dyes
    |                           well; the ground cloth of most Indian
    |                           printing and painting traditions
    |
  SILK    (filament from a      long filament; strong; lustrous; takes dye
    |      cocoon)              with brilliance; the fibre of brocade,
    |                           patola, and most court textiles
    |      mulberry  ---- cultivated on mulberry leaf
    |      muga      ---- Assam, described as GOLDEN
    |      eri       ---- commonly produced in Meghalaya
    |      wild silk ---- suggested at Harappa and Chanhudaro,
    |                     3rd millennium BC
    |
  WOOL    (fleece)              warmth by trapped air; felts; takes
    |                           strong colour; the fibre of shawls,
    |                           namda felt and knotted carpets
    |      pashm    ---- delicate, from the underbelly of wild
    |                    Tibetan and related goats: the Kani shawl fibre
    |
  BAST / LEAF FIBRE             stiff, strong; baskets, mats, cordage,
                                and natural-fibre craft

              |
              v
  [1] GINNING     separate the usable fibre from seed, husk or waste
              |
              v
  [2] SPINNING    draw out and TWIST loose fibre into a continuous
              |   yarn. Twist is the whole point: it converts a heap of
              |   short fibres into something that can be pulled tight
              |   without pulling apart.
              |
              v
  [3] WEAVING     interlace a tensioned WARP with a crossing WEFT

  NO TWIST -> NO TENSION -> NO WARP -> NO CLOTH.
  Which is why the two stages nobody names are the two stages
  without which the named stage cannot happen.
```

The source states the sequence in exactly this order - **ginning, spinning and weaving** - as the traditional techniques used across the North-East. Everything in the next five lessons is a variation on what you do to the yarn before, during or after step three.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Most candidates enter this topic at the level of fabric names and never go below them. That is why the fabric names then behave like unrelated facts to be memorised. Go one level down - to the fibre and the twist - and the fabric names start to explain themselves: why Kalamkari is done on cotton and not on silk; why brocade is a silk technique; why a shawl is wool and a *rumal* is not; why felt exists at all. The fibre is the reason the technique is possible.

### 2. Cotton, and why it is the ground of the printing traditions

Cotton is a **seed hair** - short fibres attached to the seed of the plant, which is why **ginning** exists as a separate operation. The source's record is that excavations show **early cotton cultivation and textiles** in the Indus Valley, that the Indus Valley had a **well-developed textile industry**, and that details on fabrics are mentioned in the **Rigveda**.

Two properties of cotton decide its craft uses, and both are visible in later lessons:

- **It is absorbent and takes mordant dyeing well.** A mordant is a substance that fixes a dye to the fibre, and the same dye with different mordants yields different colours. This is why the great Indian *painted and printed* traditions - hand-painted cloth with vegetable dyes, block-printed cloth - are cotton traditions. The source records Kalamkari as **hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours**, and records **Ajrakh printing** of Gujarat as a **block-printed textile**.
- **It is cool and washable**, which is why it is the everyday cloth and therefore the cloth of household, village and mass-market production, as against the court fabrics that are largely silk.

Cotton is also the fibre of the two things the source treats as composite: **Moirang Phee** of Manipur, a silk fabric made from a **combination of silk and cotton**; and the **Maheswari** sarees of Madhya Pradesh, which use a **mixture of cotton and silk yarns**, are embellished with **golden zari**, and are famous for **reversible borders that can be worn on either side**. A composite yarn is a deliberate engineering decision - silk's lustre and strength with cotton's cost and comfort - and naming it is worth a mark.

### 3. Silk, and the four silks that must be kept apart

Silk is a **continuous filament** unwound from a cocoon, which is why it is strong, lustrous and long. The source's antiquity claim is careful: archaeological findings at **Harappa and Chanhudaro** *suggest* the existence of **sericulture - silk farming -** and the use of **wild silk** during the **3rd millennium BC**. Carry the verb: *suggest*.

The distinctions that are actually examined:

| Silk | Where the source places it | Identifying fact | The error it catches |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Mulberry silk** | the general cultivated silk | reared on mulberry leaf | the default; not itself discriminating |
| **Muga** | **Assam** | described as the **golden** Muga silk; used for sarees, **mekhela chadors** and other garments | Muga is Assam and is golden; it is not eri |
| **Eri** | **Meghalaya** - "eri silk is **commonly produced** in this region" | produced where the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** tribes are skilled weavers, who weave **shawls, sarongs and mekhela chadors** from **both silk and cotton** | eri must not be attributed exclusively to Assam |
| **Wild silk** | Harappa and Chanhudaro, 3rd millennium BC | an archaeological suggestion, not a living product name | do not use an archaeological silk as a modern brand |

One further discipline sits on top of that table and is worth stating separately because it is a designed trap: the **mekhela chador is recorded for both Assam and Meghalaya**. It therefore **cannot identify a state on its own**. The identifying facts are the *fibre* and the *community*: golden muga for Assam; eri with the Garo, Khasi and Jaintia weavers for Meghalaya.

Silk's craft consequences run through the rest of this session. Because the filament is strong and takes dye brilliantly, it is the fibre of **resist-dyed yarn** traditions where the yarn must survive tying, dyeing and re-tying before it is ever woven - the **Patan Patola**, described as rich handloom **silk** sarees; **Pochampalli**, described as **silk and cotton** sarees with an ikat style of dyeing. Because it is lustrous, it is the fibre of **brocade** - Banaras brocade, generally on silk sarees, and the **Tanchoi** brocades of Varanasi.

### 4. Wool and pashm, and the fibre that makes felt possible

Wool's crafted property is that its fibres have a scaled surface, which is why wool can be **felted** - matted into a fabric without any weaving at all - and why woollen textiles trap air and insulate. The source's named wool traditions are gathered in Lesson 9, but the fibre record belongs here:

- The **Kani shawl** of Jammu and Kashmir is made of delicate **pashm**, from the **underbelly of wild Tibetan** and related goats. The word to hold is *underbelly*: the fineness of the fibre is a function of where on the animal it grows, and the shawl's value follows from the fibre's fineness.
- **Pashm** is therefore a fibre whose value is set before any craft operation begins: the fleece's fineness, not the weaver's skill alone, establishes what the finished shawl can be.
- **Thigma**, or **wool tie and dye**, is recorded for **Ladakh**, crafted mainly in the Nubra area, with the name derived from the word for the tying operation and the part being **tied tightly** before dyeing. Wool taking a resist is the point: the same logic as cotton or silk resist, executed in a fibre that behaves differently.
- **Namda felted rugs** are recorded for Gujarat in the floor-covering list. Felt is the proof that wool does not need a loom.

### 5. Khadi: a fibre record that is also a political record

The source treats khadi in a box of its own, and every element of it is examinable.

- The word **Khadi** is derived from **Khaddar**, a term used for the **hand spun cotton fabric** of **India and Bangladesh**.
- Khadi is famous for its **rugged texture**; it **keeps the body warm in winters and cool in summers**.
- In **1925**, **after the Non-Cooperation Movement**, the **All India Spinners' Association** was established for the **development, production and selling** of khadi.
- During the independence movement, **Mahatma Gandhi promoted khadi** as a symbol of **self-reliance, self-sufficiency and resistance against British textiles**.
- The **Khadi and Village Industries Commission (KVIC)**, established in **1956**, now promotes it as a mark of **identity, empowerment and transformation**.

Why does this belong in a fibre lesson rather than a history lesson? Because khadi is defined by a **process stage**, not by a design. It is hand-spun cotton fabric. Everything political about it follows from the fact that the spinning - the invisible second stage of the sequence - was deliberately made visible and made the centre of a movement. Khadi is the one case in Indian craft history where the uncounted stage was counted, and counted on purpose. That makes it the best available bridge between this session's value-chain argument and the wider history the modern-history owner holds. The boundary is clean: the political history of the Non-Cooperation Movement and of swadeshi belongs to that owner; the fabric definition, the 1925 association, the Gandhian symbolism as recorded, and the 1956 Commission belong here as craft facts.

### 6. Comparative treatment: what each fibre lets a craft do

| Fibre | Physical behaviour | Techniques it makes possible | Techniques it resists |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cotton** | short staple, absorbent, matt | mordant dyeing, hand painting, block printing, white-on-white embroidery on fine grounds, everyday weaving | high-lustre brocade; very fine warp-resist patterning is harder than in silk |
| **Silk** | long filament, strong, lustrous | supplementary-weft brocade, yarn-resist ikat including double ikat, fine figured weaves, zari-ground embroidery | felting; low-cost mass household use |
| **Wool / pashm** | scaled, insulating, felts | felt, knotted and pile carpets, tapestry-woven shawl work, wool tie-and-dye | fine transparent grounds; sharp printed detail |
| **Bast and leaf fibre** | stiff, strong, coarse | baskets, mats, cordage, natural-fibre craft objects, storage containers | drape and fine dyeing |

Read across the last column. It explains something a name-based approach cannot: why traditions cluster by fibre rather than by state. Painted and printed cloth clusters where cotton is grown and dyed. Brocade and double ikat cluster where silk is available and affordable to buy in quantity. Shawls, felts and carpets cluster where wool is available and warmth is needed. Geography enters through the fibre, not through the border.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** If fibre determines technique, the emphasis on communities and regions is misplaced; the map of Indian textiles is really a map of agriculture and climate.

**Reply.** Fibre sets the **possibility**; it does not set the **practice**. Cotton is grown across much of India and the painted-cloth tradition is not. Silk is available in many places and double ikat is recorded at Patan. What fibre explains is why a technique is *feasible* in a region; what community, patronage and market explain is why it is *performed* there and why it took the specific form it did. The composite fabrics make the point sharply: **Moirang Phee** and the **Maheswari** saree both combine silk and cotton, and the combination is a design decision about cost, comfort and lustre, not a consequence of what grows nearby. Fibre is a constraint, and a constraint is not a cause.

### Examination use

Three uses. First, the **three-stage sequence** - ginning, spinning, weaving - is quotable and is the correct opening for any question on handloom or on textile process. Second, the **four-silk table** carries the topic's most trap-prone distinction, and the sentence to hold verbatim is that **eri silk is commonly produced in Meghalaya, where the Garo, Khasi and Jaintia tribes are skilled weavers**, while **muga is the golden silk of Assam**, and the **mekhela chador cannot identify a state by itself**. Third, the **khadi record** supplies a fully dated, non-controversial block - 1925, the All India Spinners' Association, and 1956 for KVIC - that can close any answer on craft, self-reliance or artisan organisation without straying into the modern-history owner's argument.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating "silk" as one thing. Mulberry, muga, eri and archaeologically suggested wild silk are four different records.
- Attributing eri silk exclusively to Assam, or muga to Meghalaya.
- Using the mekhela chador as a state identifier when the source records it for two states.
- Dropping the hedge on Harappan sericulture: the finding *suggests* the existence of silk farming.
- Calling khadi a design or a style. It is defined by being **hand spun**.
- Writing that a fibre "originated" somewhere when the source only records where it is produced or used.

### Revision notes

1. The traditional sequence, in the source's own words: **ginning, spinning and weaving**.
2. Twist is what spinning adds; without twist there is no tension-bearing warp and no cloth. The two unnamed stages make the named stage possible.
3. **Cotton:** early cultivation and textiles in the Indus Valley; fabrics mentioned in the **Rigveda**; absorbent and mordant-friendly, hence the ground of painted and printed traditions.
4. **Sericulture and wild silk** are *suggested* at **Harappa and Chanhudaro**, **3rd millennium BC**.
5. **Muga = Assam, golden**, used for sarees, mekhela chadors and other garments.
6. **Eri = commonly produced in Meghalaya**, where the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** tribes are skilled weavers producing **shawls, sarongs and mekhela chadors** from **both silk and cotton**.
7. The **mekhela chador is woven in both Assam and Meghalaya** and therefore identifies no state by itself.
8. **Moirang Phee** (Manipur) is a **silk-and-cotton** fabric; the **Phanek** is a wraparound skirt worn by Manipuri women, usually in vibrant colours with traditional motifs.
9. **Maheswari** sarees (Madhya Pradesh) use a **cotton-and-silk mixture**, **golden zari**, and **reversible borders** wearable on either side.
10. **Pashm** for the **Kani shawl** comes from the **underbelly of wild Tibetan** and related goats; the fleece's fineness, set before any craft operation, establishes what the shawl can be.
11. **Thigma** is **wool tie and dye** from **Ladakh**, mainly from the Nubra area, the name taken from the tying operation.
12. **Khadi** is from **Khaddar**, the **hand spun cotton fabric** of India and Bangladesh; rugged texture; warm in winter, cool in summer; **All India Spinners' Association, 1925**, after the Non-Cooperation Movement; Gandhian symbol of **self-reliance, self-sufficiency and resistance against British textiles**; **KVIC established 1956**, promoting it as a mark of **identity, empowerment and transformation**.
13. Fibre sets possibility; community, patronage and market set practice. Composite fabrics prove that the combination is a design choice.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how the choice of fibre shapes the technical possibilities of an Indian textile tradition, using named examples. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Fibre sets what a textile tradition can physically do.

**Cotton** is a short, absorbent seed fibre that accepts mordant dyeing, so it grounds the painted and printed traditions - hand painting with vegetable dyes, and block printing. It is also the everyday cloth of household production.

**Silk** is a long, strong, lustrous filament. It survives repeated tying and dyeing before weaving, which is why the yarn-resist traditions are silk traditions, and it carries metallic supplementary weft, which is why brocade is a silk technique.

**Wool** has a scaled fibre that felts and insulates, yielding felted rugs needing no loom, knotted carpets and shawls; the finest work uses pashm from the underbelly of wild Tibetan goats.

Composite fabrics - the silk-and-cotton Moirang Phee, the Maheswari saree - show that fibre constrains but does not dictate: combination is a design decision.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = one correct physical property per fibre, not merely a name 3 + one named Indian tradition tied to each property 4 + the composite-fabric qualification showing fibre constrains rather than determines 3.** Naming fibres without stating a physical property scores at most three.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it technically unsurprising that hand-painted and block-printed cloth traditions developed on cotton grounds while brocade and double-ikat traditions developed on silk? Answer from fibre properties, and then state the limit of the explanation.

**Model answer:** The explanation is in three steps. **Cotton is a short, absorbent seed fibre**, so it takes mordant dyeing readily - and mordant dyeing is what makes painted and printed cloth possible, because the artisan applies a mordant where colour is wanted and the dye then fixes only there. An absorbent matt ground also shows a printed or drawn line crisply. So hand painting with vegetable dyes and block printing are cotton techniques for material reasons, not merely regional ones. **Silk is a long, continuous, strong and lustrous filament**, and that has two consequences. It survives the mechanical punishment of yarn-resist work - being bound, dyed, unbound and rebound before it ever reaches the loom - which is what the resist-dyed sarees of Patan and Pochampally require; and it displays the lustre of metallic supplementary weft, which is what makes brocade worth the two to eight weeks a Banaras saree takes. The **limit** of the explanation is that fibre establishes feasibility, not practice. Cotton grows very widely and painted cloth does not; silk is available in many regions and double ikat is recorded at Patan. Why a feasible technique is actually performed in a given place is a question about community, patronage, credit and market, and the composite fabrics - Moirang Phee and the Maheswari saree, both deliberately mixing silk and cotton - show that producers cross fibre boundaries whenever design or cost makes it worthwhile.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that a textile map is a map of agriculture. Fibre availability explains where a technique *could* exist; it never explains where it *does*. Treating the fibre as the cause produces the error of assuming that any cotton-growing region must have a printing tradition, and that any silk region must have a brocade tradition - neither of which the record supports.

## Lesson 5 - The loom: how pattern gets built into cloth, and the weaves that are constantly confused

Progress: 5/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: warp and weft, plain ground, supplementary weft and brocade, Jamdani, Tanchoi, Mashru, the named regional saree and weaving traditions, and the weave-versus-surface firewall
