Book context: Queried - the cloth-handicrafts passages on Tanchoi and Jamdani, the regional saree table, the varieties-of-weaving table, the Banaras brocade description with its production time, and the Maheswari and Himroo entries
CA search: "Not applicable - technique lesson"
CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the loom, and the three places a pattern can come from

```text
   THE LOOM, SEEN FROM ABOVE

   warp threads, held under tension, running away from the weaver
   || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || ||
   || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || ||
   ===========================================================  <- weft passes
   || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || ||     across
   || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || ||
   || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || || ||

   WHERE CAN A PATTERN COME FROM?

   (A) FROM THE INTERLACING ITSELF
       plain weave: one over, one under -> no pattern, just cloth
       satin-type float: warp floats over several wefts -> sheen
         -> MASHRU, a handwoven MIX of silk and cotton: the lustrous
            face and the different back are the point of the structure

   (B) FROM AN EXTRA WEFT ADDED ONLY WHERE THE PATTERN IS
       "supplementary weft": a second weft, often metallic, carried
       across only in the patterned zones
         -> BANARAS BROCADE: silver and golden zari, floral motifs,
            generally on silk sarees, TWO TO EIGHT WEEKS per saree
         -> TANCHOI: came via trade, inspired by Chinese weaving,
            perfected at Varanasi, resembling miniature paintings
         -> JAMDANI: muslin woven with OPAQUE designs on a
            TRANSPARENT base -- the contrast IS the technique

   (C) FROM YARN THAT WAS ALREADY COLOURED BEFORE WEAVING
       -> the whole resist family: Lesson 6

   AND, CRITICALLY, NOT FROM:
   (D) ANYTHING ADDED AFTER THE CLOTH LEAVES THE LOOM
       needle work, painting, printing, appliqued patches
       -> Lessons 7 and 8. This is the firewall.
```

Three sources of pattern on the loom, one source of pattern off it. Almost every textile identification item in this topic is built on the boundary between (B) and (D).

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Banarasi" and "Chikankari" are both Uttar Pradesh textiles with metallic or white thread and floral motifs, and both are famous. A candidate who has stored them as *places* cannot tell them apart when the question describes the process instead of naming the product. The distinction is not regional and not aesthetic: one is made **on** the loom and the other **after** the loom. Once that is fixed, a whole family of look-alikes separates cleanly, and the separation is permanent because it is physical.

### 2. Warp, weft and the ground

The **warp** is the set of yarns stretched under tension along the length of the cloth; the **weft** crosses it. Plain weave - one over, one under - produces cloth and nothing else. Everything decorative is a departure from that: either a different interlacing order, or an extra thread, or a yarn that was coloured before it arrived.

That is why the *fineness* of a ground matters so much in Indian weaving. A very fine, low-density cotton ground is nearly transparent, and a pattern built on it in a heavier or denser thread reads as opaque against a transparent field. That contrast is precisely what the source records for **Jamdani of Bengal**: **weaving muslin with opaque designs on a transparent base**. Jamdani is therefore not a motif vocabulary or a regional style; it is a *relationship between two densities* on the same cloth.

### 3. Supplementary weft: the brocade family

A **supplementary weft** is an extra weft yarn carried across the warp only where the design requires it, floating over the ground and being caught into it. Because the extra yarn is usually metallic or of contrasting lustre, the effect is a raised, gleaming figure on a plainer field - which is why the family is called brocade, and why it is a silk family.

The source's brocade records:

- **Banaras Brocade** is famous for **silver and golden zari work** carried out on sarees, with **floral motifs**, **generally silk** sarees, and it takes **two to eight weeks to produce one**.
- **Tanchoi Brocades**, at **Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh**, are described as a kind of Banarasi saree whose technique involves a single or more weft; and separately, **Tanchoi silk** is recorded as having come **via trade**, inspired by **Chinese weaving**, **now perfected in Varanasi as brocade resembling miniature paintings**.
- **Jamdani of Bengal**, as above, and the separately recorded **Awadh Jamdani** at **Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh**, described as light and transparent.

Two cautions attach. First, **Banaras Brocade and Chikankari are both Lucknow-and-Varanasi-region famous, and both are worked with fine thread on fine cloth, but the first is a weave and the second is embroidery** - the single most tested weave-versus-surface pair in the topic. Second, **Jamdani appears in the record under more than one regional name**: Jamdani of Bengal, and Awadh Jamdani placed at Varanasi. Treat "Jamdani" as a **technique family** - opaque supplementary figuring on a transparent muslin ground - with named regional expressions, rather than as the property of one place.

### 4. The regional saree and weaving traditions, as the source records them

| Tradition | Where the source places it | What the source says about the technique or product |
|---|---|---|
| **Pochampalli** | **Bhoodan Pochampally, Telangana** | **silk and cotton** sarees with an **ikat style of dyeing**; the record also notes an association with Air India |
| **Patan Patola** | **Patan, Gujarat** | **rich handloom silk sarees** |
| **Baluchari** | **Murshidabad, West Bengal** | these **depict ancient stories**, with threads extensively used |
| **Tanchoi Brocades** | **Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh** | a kind of Banarasi saree; the technique involves a single or more weft |
| **Maheswari saree** | **Madhya Pradesh** | **cotton and silk yarn mixture**, **golden zari**, famous for **reversible borders** wearable on either side |
| **Himroo** | **Aurangabad, Maharashtra** | material used for **royal dresses and shawls** in medieval Muslim courts; the shawls remain very popular |
| **Pata weaving** | **Bastar, Chhattisgarh** | sarees of this kind |
| **Mashru weaving** | **Gujarat** | a **handwoven mix** of fibres |
| **Bohra cap weaving** | **Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh** | a crochet-based construction worked outward from the core, with floral patterning formed in the process |
| **Patku weaving** | **Gujarat** | a **tie-dyed textile** |
| **Moirang Phee** | **Manipur** | a **silk fabric made from a combination of silk and cotton** |
| **Phanek** | **Manipur** | a **wraparound skirt** worn by Manipuri women, usually woven in **vibrant colours with traditional motifs** |
| **Khes** | **Panipat, Haryana** | **woven in a double** structure, in a **chequered design** |
| **Puan** | **Mizoram** | cloth decorated with **exclusive needlework in the shape of a stripe or an arrow**, worn by **both men and women**; **women are primarily responsible** for weaving it |

Two entries in that table deliberately break the "weave" heading, and they are teaching you something. **Patku** is listed by the source among weaving varieties but is described as a **tie-dyed textile**, which means the yarn or cloth carries a resist; and the **Puan** is a woven cloth that the source identifies by its **needlework**. Real traditions combine operations. The firewall between weave and surface is a tool for *identifying the discriminating step*, not a claim that a single cloth can only ever have one operation performed on it.

**One further Jamdani-family centre, added for completeness.** The **Uppada Jamdani** saree is associated with **Uppada, in coastal Andhra Pradesh**, and belongs to the Jamdani technique family described above - fine figured work built into a light ground. It is the third recorded regional expression of that family alongside **Jamdani of Bengal** and **Awadh Jamdani** at Varanasi, and it is the clearest demonstration that "Jamdani" names a **technique** with several homes. This session records the centre and the family placement and does not assert a registration year, a fibre specification or a motif vocabulary for it, because the substantive record used here does not supply them.

### 5. Mashru, and what a structure can be *for*

**Mashru** is recorded as a **handwoven mix** made in **Gujarat**. The structural family it belongs to - a warp-faced satin-type weave in which one fibre dominates the face and another the back - is worth understanding because it is the clearest case in Indian weaving of a **structure designed for a purpose other than appearance**. A cloth whose face and back are different fibres behaves differently against the skin than against the outer world. That is a functional design decision expressed purely through interlacing order, with no dyeing, printing or stitching involved at all. Wherever a question asks how technique and use interact, Mashru is the most economical example available.

### 6. Comparative treatment: five ways to make a figured cloth, and how to tell them apart in one sentence

| Family | Where the pattern is decided | The one-sentence test | Named examples here |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Structural weave** | in the interlacing order | the pattern disappears if you change only the order of over-and-under | Mashru; the double-woven chequered Khes |
| **Supplementary weft** | on the loom, by an extra thread | an extra thread floats on the surface and is caught into the ground | Banaras Brocade; Tanchoi; Jamdani |
| **Yarn-resist (Lesson 6)** | before the loom, on the yarn | the colour is already in the yarn before weaving begins; edges look slightly feathered | Patan Patola; Pochampalli |
| **Surface application (Lesson 7)** | after the loom, on the cloth | the colour or pigment sits on the finished cloth | Kalamkari; Ajrakh; batik |
| **Surface stitching (Lesson 8)** | after the loom, with a needle | thread is added to existing cloth by needle | Chikankari; Kashida; Phulkari; Kantha |

Why do they differ? Because each family solves a different problem. Structural weaves solve a problem of *function*. Supplementary weft solves the problem of putting an expensive material only where it shows. Yarn-resist solves the problem of colour that cannot be washed off a surface, since it is inside the yarn. Surface application solves the problem of pictorial freedom, because a brush or a block can draw what a loom cannot. Surface stitching solves the problem of adding relief and texture. Learn the problem each family solves and you will never need to memorise which is which.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** The weave-versus-surface firewall is too rigid. The Puan is identified by needlework yet listed as weaving; Patku is listed as weaving yet described as tie-dyed; Banaras brocade is described as zari **embroidery** work in one place and as a woven brocade in another. If the sources themselves blur the categories, the firewall is an artefact of this session, not of the craft.

**Reply.** The blurring in the sources is real and must be reported rather than tidied away - and the Banaras case is recorded here as an open discrepancy for exactly that reason. But the firewall survives, because it is not a claim that each cloth belongs to one family. It is a claim about **which step you must name to identify the tradition**. For Banaras brocade the discriminating step is that metallic weft is introduced **during weaving**; for Chikankari the discriminating step is that white thread is worked **into finished cloth with a needle**. Even where a cloth carries several operations - woven, then resist-dyed, then embroidered - the identification question is always "which step makes this tradition what it is?". The firewall is a diagnostic instrument, and diagnostic instruments are allowed to be sharper than the world they are applied to, provided you say so.

### Examination use

Four uses. The **weave-versus-surface firewall** is the single highest-yield distinction in the textile half of this topic and resolves the Banarasi-Chikankari family instantly. The **regional table** is the raw material for any matching item, and should be rehearsed as name-plus-region-plus-process, never as name-plus-state. The **Jamdani definition** - opaque designs on a transparent base - is quotable and is the kind of phrase that converts a vague identification into a precise one. The **two-to-eight-week figure** for a Banaras brocade saree is the session's best single piece of evidence for any answer about why hand production cannot simply be scaled, and it should be deployed in livelihood answers rather than in identification answers.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling Banaras brocade an embroidery, or Chikankari a weave.
- Treating Jamdani as belonging to one region when the record places it in Bengal and at Varanasi.
- Assuming that anything with metallic thread is zari *embroidery*; zari can be woven in as supplementary weft or stitched on afterwards, and the two are different traditions.
- Forgetting that Pochampalli is recorded as **silk and cotton**, not silk alone.
- Reducing Mashru to "a mixed fabric" without noting that the structure puts different fibres on the two faces.
- Treating the weave-versus-surface split as a claim that a cloth can carry only one operation.

### Revision notes

1. **Warp** runs the length under tension; **weft** crosses it. Plain weave is one over, one under.
2. Pattern can come from **(a) the interlacing order**, **(b) a supplementary weft**, **(c) pre-coloured yarn**, or **(d) anything added after the loom**. Identification turns on which of these is the discriminating step.
3. **Jamdani** = weaving **muslin with opaque designs on a transparent base**; recorded for **Bengal**, as **Awadh Jamdani** at **Varanasi**, and as **Uppada Jamdani** at **Uppada, Andhra Pradesh** - a technique family with several homes.
4. **Banaras Brocade** = **silver and golden zari**, **floral motifs**, generally **silk** sarees, **two to eight weeks** per saree.
5. **Tanchoi** came **via trade**, inspired by **Chinese weaving**, **perfected at Varanasi**, **resembling miniature paintings**; the Tanchoi brocade entry notes a single or more weft.
6. **Pochampalli** = **Bhoodan Pochampally, Telangana**, **silk and cotton** sarees, **ikat style of dyeing**, with a recorded Air India association.
7. **Patan Patola** = **Patan, Gujarat**, **rich handloom silk sarees**.
8. **Baluchari** = **Murshidabad, West Bengal**, sarees that **depict ancient stories**.
9. **Maheswari** = Madhya Pradesh, **cotton-and-silk mixture**, **golden zari**, **reversible borders**.
10. **Himroo** = **Aurangabad, Maharashtra**; medieval **royal dress and shawls**; still popular.
11. **Pata** = Bastar, Chhattisgarh. **Mashru** = Gujarat, handwoven mix. **Bohra cap** = Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh, crochet from the core outwards. **Patku** = Gujarat, tie-dyed.
12. **Khes** = **Panipat, Haryana**, double-woven, **chequered**.
13. **Moirang Phee** and **Phanek** = Manipur; **Puan** = Mizoram, needlework stripe or arrow, worn by both sexes, woven primarily by women.
14. Five figuring families and the problem each solves: function (structure), economy of precious material (supplementary weft), wash-fast colour (yarn resist), pictorial freedom (surface application), relief and texture (stitching).

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Distinguish between woven and surface-decorated Indian textile traditions, and explain why the distinction, rather than regional attribution, should organise an answer on Indian textiles. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

A woven tradition decides its pattern **on the loom**. Supplementary-weft brocade carries an extra, often metallic, weft across the ground only where the design falls: Banaras brocade uses silver and golden zari with floral motifs on generally silk sarees, taking two to eight weeks each; Jamdani weaves opaque designs on a transparent muslin base.

A surface-decorated tradition decides its pattern **after the loom**. Chikankari works white thread into finished cotton, mulmul, polyester or voile; Kalamkari hand-paints cloth with vegetable dyes; Ajrakh is block-printed; Pipli applique sews cut fabric onto a ground.

Regional attribution cannot organise this, because the same region carries both: Varanasi holds brocade, Lucknow holds embroidery, and both are Uttar Pradesh. Technique attribution also survives the examiner's favourite move, which is to describe a process without naming the product.

The correct structure is therefore technique first, region second, community third.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = the loom-versus-post-loom criterion stated as a physical test 3 + two named woven and two named surface traditions with their processes 4 + the demonstration that one state holds both, which is what defeats regional organisation 3.** Listing textiles by state, however many, cannot exceed four.

### Concept check

**Question:** An item describes a textile as "a fine muslin in which dense, opaque figures appear to float on a nearly transparent field, the figuring built up by an extra weft introduced as the cloth is woven". Name the technique family, state the discriminating step, and explain why the description alone cannot fix a single state.

**Model answer:** The family is **Jamdani** - the source's definition is weaving muslin with **opaque designs on a transparent base**, and that contrast between two densities on the same cloth is the tradition's identity. The **discriminating step** is that the figuring is **supplementary weft introduced during weaving**: an extra weft is carried across only where the design falls, floats over the ground and is caught into it, which is why the figure reads as dense while the untouched ground stays sheer. That single step separates Jamdani from every surface-decorated look-alike - a white-on-white embroidery such as Chikankari also produces pale figures on fine cloth, but it does so with a needle on finished fabric, and a printed or painted motif sits on the surface rather than being locked into the structure. The description **cannot fix a state** because the record places the technique in more than one place: Jamdani is recorded for **Bengal**, and **Awadh Jamdani** is recorded at **Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh**. Treat the word as naming a technique family with regional expressions; to fix a region you would need a further fact - the centre, the community, or the specific registered description - not merely the structural description given.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a textile name as automatically a place name. Several of the most examinable terms in this topic - Jamdani, ikat, brocade, batik, applique - name **operations**, and operations travel. The names that do fix a place do so because a place word is inside them: Patan Patola, Bhoodan Pochampally, Awadh Jamdani, Banaras brocade. Learn which kind of word you are holding before you commit it to a matching answer.

## Lesson 6 - Resist: why tying, binding and waxing produce three different families

Progress: 6/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the logic of resist, bandhani and bandhej, laharia, ikat and its centres, Patola and Pochampalli, Telia Rumal, Thigma, batik, and the yarn-resist versus cloth-resist distinction

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the tie-and-dye section in full, the ikat and laharia passages, the regional saree entries for Patan Patola and Pochampalli, the Telia Rumal and Patku entries, the Thigma entry, and the batik passage with its origin statement
CA search: "Not applicable - technique lesson"
CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one idea, two places to apply it

```text
   THE IDEA: stop the dye from reaching part of the material.
   Whatever the dye cannot reach keeps the earlier colour.
   Repeat with a new colour and a new set of blocked areas,
   and an image builds up in reverse.

   APPLY IT TO THE FINISHED CLOTH            APPLY IT TO THE YARN
   ("cloth resist")                          ("yarn resist")
   -------------------------------           ----------------------------
   pinch, tie, bind, fold, or wax            bind sections of the yarn
   the woven fabric, then dye                BEFORE weaving, dye, unbind,
                                             rebind, dye again, then weave
   -------------------------------           ----------------------------
   BANDHANI / BANDHEJ                        IKAT ("resist dyeing" method)
     repeated knotting and dyeing              major centres: TELANGANA,
     Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra                ODISHA, GUJARAT, ANDHRA
     Pradesh, Tamil Nadu                       PRADESH
   LAHARIA                                   -> warp only, weft only, or BOTH
     ripple or wave-like patterns              BOTH = DOUBLE IKAT = PATOLA
     Jaipur and Jodhpur                        (Patan, Gujarat)
   BATIK                                     -> POCHAMPALLI: silk and cotton
     wax resist; one end permeated               sarees, ikat style of dyeing
     with molten wax, then dyed in             -> TELIA RUMAL: historic home
     cold wax; multicoloured sarees               CHIRALA, ANDHRA PRADESH;
     and dupattas; MP and West Bengal             the REGISTERED indication is
     ORIGINATED IN INDONESIA                      PUTTAPAKA TELIA RUMAL,
   THIGMA (Ladakh)                                PUTTAPAKA, NALGONDA,
     WOOL tie and dye; the part is                TELANGANA (App. 599;
     tied tightly before dyeing                   no year asserted)

   THE VISUAL GIVEAWAY: yarn-resist patterns have slightly BLURRED or
   FEATHERED edges, because the dyed yarn can never be re-aligned
   perfectly on the loom. Cloth-resist patterns have crisp, often
   circular or dotted, marks where the ties were.
```

One physical idea, applied at two different moments, producing two families that look superficially similar and are technically unrelated.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Bandhani, laharia, batik, ikat and Patola are usually stored as five unrelated words. They are one idea applied five ways, and the ordering principle is *when* the resist is applied. Once you ask "was the resist on the yarn or on the cloth?", the five separate instantly, and the follow-up question - "on one set of yarns or both?" - separates the two hardest members of the family, Pochampalli and Patola.

### 2. Cloth resist: bandhani, laharia and batik

**Bandhani or Bandhej** is what the source calls the **Tie and Dye** technique in English. It is **largely practised in Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu**, and it produces **vibrant designs through repeated knotting and dyeing**. Two words are doing the work: *repeated* and *knotting*. The cloth is pinched into small points, tied, dyed; the ties are moved or added and it is dyed again in a second colour. The resulting field of small round reserved spots is the family's signature.

**Laharia** is recorded as a **special kind of tie and dye method** that leads to **ripples or wave-like patterns** in the fabric, **practised in Jaipur and Jodhpur**. The difference from bandhani is a difference of *geometry*: instead of pinching points, the cloth is rolled or folded diagonally and bound along its length, so the reserved areas form diagonal stripes rather than dots.

**Batik** uses a different resist material altogether - **wax**. The source's description is specific: **one end of the fabric is permeated with molten wax and then dyed in cold wax to produce multicoloured batik sarees and dupattas**. It is **most popular in Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal**, and - an examinable point that candidates routinely get backwards - **batik art originated in Indonesia**.

**Thigma**, the **wool tie and dye** of **Ladakh**, belongs to this family too. It is crafted mainly in the **Nubra** area, its name is derived from the word for the tying operation, and the part to be reserved is **tied tightly**. It is the family's proof that resist is fibre-independent: the same idea works on wool, where it must contend with a fibre that behaves quite differently from cotton or silk.

### 3. Yarn resist: the ikat family

**Ikat** is described by the source as another tie-and-dye method, **also known as the 'resist dyeing' method**, with **major centres in Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh**. Four states named in one line - which is by itself the refutation of any one-state claim about ikat.

The technical point that the phrase "resist dyeing" leaves implicit is the one worth holding: in ikat the resist is applied to the **yarn**, before weaving. Sections of yarn are tightly bound so that dye cannot penetrate; the yarn is dyed, unbound, rebound elsewhere, dyed again; and only then is it mounted on the loom and woven. The pattern therefore exists in the threads before the cloth exists at all, and the weaver's job is to align hundreds of pre-dyed yarns so that the intended image appears. Perfect alignment is impossible, which is why genuine ikat has the soft, feathered edge that is its most reliable visual tell.

Three degrees of the technique matter:

| Degree | What is resist-dyed | Consequence | Named case in the record |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Single ikat (warp)** | only the warp yarns | pattern reads along the length; easier to control | - |
| **Single ikat (weft)** | only the weft yarns | pattern reads across; alignment adjusted while weaving | - |
| **Double ikat** | **both warp and weft** | both sets must be dyed to match and then aligned to each other; the most demanding form | **Patola**, of **Patan, Gujarat** - recorded as **rich handloom silk sarees** |

**Pochampalli**, at **Bhoodan Pochampally, Telangana**, is recorded as **silk and cotton sarees with an ikat style of dyeing**. Note what the record does and does not say: it places the tradition and names the dyeing style; it does not state that Pochampalli is single or double ikat. The safe formulation is therefore that Pochampalli belongs to the ikat family at a named Telangana centre, and that **Patola of Patan is the recorded double-ikat case**. Writing a confident single-or-double claim about Pochampalli goes beyond what this session can evidence.

**Telia Rumal**, a **resist-dyed textile** whose name the source glosses as meaning **oily** - a reference to the oil treatment the yarn undergoes as part of the preparation - is the family's third named member, and it requires one careful separation.

- **The historic association.** The core record places the tradition at **Chirala, in Andhra Pradesh**. That is a statement about where the cloth has historically been made.
- **The registered indication.** What the register actually protects is **Puttapaka Telia Rumal**, under **application number 599**, for **Puttapaka village in Nalgonda district, Telangana**. That is a statement about a protected name and its territory.

**Do not attach the registration to Chirala.** The two are different claims about different places, and merging them produces exactly the error Lesson 17 is built to prevent - reading a registration as though it were a statement about a craft's origin. The honest formulation is that the Telia Rumal tradition is historically associated with Chirala in Andhra Pradesh, while the registered geographical indication is *Puttapaka Telia Rumal* of Puttapaka, Telangana. **No registration year is asserted here**, because reports of the certificate's date do not agree and no dated registry extract was obtained; the discrepancy is recorded in the ledger. The entry is worth keeping for three reasons: it names a **town** rather than a state, its name records a **process stage** rather than a place or a motif, and it is this session's clearest case of a craft whose historic home and whose registered territory are not the same.

Finally, **Paagadu Bandhu**, also called **Chitki**, is recorded as a tie-and-dye tradition of **Andhra Pradesh** in which bindings are applied to the fabric as part of the dye process - a reminder that the same state can hold both cloth-resist and yarn-resist traditions, and that "Andhra Pradesh tie and dye" is not a single thing.

### 4. Why the two families look alike and behave differently

| | Cloth resist (bandhani, laharia, batik, Thigma) | Yarn resist (ikat, Patola, Pochampalli, Telia Rumal) |
|---|---|---|
| **When applied** | after weaving | before weaving |
| **What is bound** | the fabric | the yarn |
| **Edge quality** | crisp; dots, rings, diagonal bands | soft, feathered, slightly stepped |
| **Correcting a mistake** | possible in later dye rounds | effectively impossible once the yarn is on the loom |
| **Skill concentrated in** | the tier and the dyer | the dyer *and* the weaver, who must align the pattern |
| **Failure mode** | colour bleeding under a loose tie | misalignment, which destroys the image |
| **Can it be imitated by printing?** | yes, and printed imitations are common | yes on the surface, but the reverse face will not match |

That last row is the one with analytical value. In a yarn-resist cloth the pattern goes **through** the fabric, because the colour is inside the thread; in a printed imitation it sits on one face. So the reverse side of the cloth is the honest test - and this is a rare case where a craft's authenticity can be checked by a physical property rather than by a label. It is also the cleanest illustration of what a Geographical Indication does and does not do: the tag protects the *name*, while the fabric itself carries the evidence.

### 5. Comparative treatment: resist against the other figuring families

Resist solves a problem that neither weaving nor surface application solves well - **colour that cannot be removed**, because it is inside the yarn or has penetrated the cloth. A printed motif can abrade; an embroidered thread can be pulled; a supplementary weft can be snagged. Resist-dyed colour has nowhere to go. That durability explains why resist traditions concentrate in garments meant for hard, repeated use and for ceremonial wear expected to last - and it explains why the technique survives despite being the most laborious way to put a pattern on cloth.

It also explains the family's economics. Yarn-resist work requires the entire pattern to be planned before a single pick is woven; an error at the binding stage is discovered only when the cloth appears. The labour is therefore front-loaded and irrecoverable, which is why double-ikat silk is among the most expensive handwoven goods in India and why "scaling it up" is a contradiction in terms.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** The source itself calls ikat a "tie and dye" method and groups it with bandhani. Insisting on a yarn-versus-cloth distinction imposes a technical taxonomy the source does not use.

**Reply.** The source's grouping is correct at the level of *principle* - all of these are resist techniques, and saying so is not an error. The distinction added here is at the level of *application*, and it is forced on us by the source's own descriptions rather than imported from outside: bandhani is described as **knotting and dyeing** producing designs **in the fabric**, while ikat is described as a **resist dyeing** method whose centres produce sarees with an **ikat style of dyeing** applied to yarn that is then woven, and Patola is recorded as resist-dyed in **both warp and weft**. A warp and a weft only exist as yarn. So the distinction is latent in the record, and making it explicit is what allows a candidate to answer a process-description item rather than only a name-matching item.

### Examination use

The highest-yield sentences here are: **bandhani produces vibrant designs through repeated knotting and dyeing and is largely practised in Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu**; **laharia produces ripples or wave-like patterns and is practised in Jaipur and Jodhpur**; **ikat is a resist-dyeing method with major centres in Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh**; **Patola of Patan, Gujarat, is resist-dyed in both warp and weft**; and **batik originated in Indonesia and is most popular in Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal**. The four-state ikat line is the single most useful weapon against one-state exclusivity anywhere in this topic. For a Mains answer, the front-loaded, irrecoverable labour of yarn resist is the best available illustration of why craft value and craft unscalability are the same property.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating ikat and bandhani as the same operation because both are called tie and dye.
- Asserting that Pochampalli is double ikat; the record names the ikat style of dyeing, not the degree.
- Claiming that Patola is made at Pochampally or Kanchipuram; the record places Patan Patola at Patan, Gujarat.
- Forgetting that batik originated in Indonesia, or that its Indian popularity is recorded for Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal.
- Assuming resist is a cotton-and-silk technique only; Thigma is wool.
- Treating a single state as the home of ikat when four are named in one sentence.

### Revision notes

1. Resist = preventing dye from reaching part of the material. Applied to **cloth** or to **yarn**; that is the family split.
2. **Bandhani / Bandhej** = tie and dye; **repeated knotting and dyeing**; **Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu**.
3. **Laharia** = ripples or **wave-like patterns**; **Jaipur and Jodhpur**.
4. **Ikat** = **resist dyeing** applied to yarn before weaving; major centres **Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh**.
5. **Double ikat** = both warp and weft resist-dyed; the recorded case is **Patola**, **Patan, Gujarat**, rich handloom **silk** sarees.
6. **Pochampalli** = **Bhoodan Pochampally, Telangana**; **silk and cotton** sarees, **ikat style of dyeing**; the degree of ikat is **not** stated in the record.
7. **Telia Rumal** = a resist-dyed textile, name glossed as **oily**; historically associated with **Chirala, Andhra Pradesh**. The **registered** indication is **Puttapaka Telia Rumal**, application **no. 599**, **Puttapaka village, Nalgonda district, Telangana**. **No registration year is asserted**; the registration is **not** attached to Chirala.
8. **Paagadu Bandhu (Chitki)** = Andhra Pradesh tie and dye with bindings applied to the fabric.
9. **Batik** = **wax** resist; one end permeated with **molten wax**, then dyed in **cold wax**; **multicoloured sarees and dupattas**; **Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal**; **originated in Indonesia**.
10. **Thigma** = **wool tie and dye**, **Ladakh**, mainly the **Nubra** area; the part is **tied tightly**.
11. **Patku** (Gujarat) is listed among weaves but described as a **tie-dyed textile** - operations combine in real cloth.
12. The physical tell: yarn resist gives **feathered edges and a matching reverse face**; cloth resist gives **crisp dots, rings or diagonal bands**; printing matches on one face only.
13. Yarn-resist labour is **front-loaded and irrecoverable**, which is why the cloth is costly and the technique unscalable.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Resist-dyeing traditions illustrate why craft value and craft unscalability are the same property." Examine with reference to Indian tie-and-dye and ikat traditions. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Resist-dyeing prevents dye from reaching part of a material, so that the protected area keeps its earlier colour. Indian traditions apply this idea at two moments, and the difference explains the economics.

**Cloth resist** is applied after weaving. Bandhani produces vibrant designs through repeated knotting and dyeing across Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu; laharia yields ripple-like patterns at Jaipur and Jodhpur; batik uses molten wax, is popular in Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal, and originated in Indonesia; Thigma applies the same logic to Ladakhi wool. Here an error is partly recoverable, because later dye rounds can absorb it.

**Yarn resist** is applied before weaving. In ikat - practised at centres in Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh - yarn is bound, dyed, rebound and dyed again, and only then woven. In the double form, recorded for Patola of Patan, both warp and weft are dyed and must then be aligned to each other on the loom.

The consequences are economic. The entire design must be resolved before a single pick is woven; labour is front-loaded; an error is discovered only when the cloth appears and cannot be corrected. Precisely this irrecoverability is what buyers pay for, and it is also what makes volume production impossible without abandoning the technique.

The qualification matters: the record names Pochampalli's ikat style of dyeing without specifying its degree, and the claim above rests on Patola, which is documented as double.

**Scoring note:** **15 marks = the yarn-versus-cloth resist distinction made physically, not verbally 4 + at least four named traditions correctly placed with their processes 5 + the front-loaded-irrecoverable-labour argument stated as an economic mechanism 4 + the explicit qualification about what the record does not state 2.** An answer asserting that Pochampalli is double ikat loses the final two marks even if otherwise complete.

### Concept check

**Question:** You are shown two lengths of cloth. Both carry a pattern of soft-edged geometric figures. On one, the pattern appears identically on both faces; on the other, the reverse is pale and blurred. What can you infer, what can you not infer, and which Indian traditions are candidates in each case?

**Model answer:** The cloth whose pattern appears **identically on both faces** is consistent with **yarn resist**: the colour is inside the thread, so it necessarily runs through the fabric, and the soft edges are the signature of yarn that could not be perfectly re-aligned on the loom. Candidates are the ikat family - centres recorded in Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh - including Pochampalli at Bhoodan Pochampally, Telia Rumal at Chirala, and, if the pattern is precise in both directions, the double-ikat Patola of Patan, Gujarat. The cloth whose **reverse is pale** is not yarn-resist; the colour has been applied to one face, which is consistent with printing, painting or a surface imitation of a resist look. What you **cannot** infer from either observation is equally important. You cannot infer the state: ikat is recorded across four states, so a two-faced soft-edged pattern places the cloth in a family, not in a place. You cannot infer the degree of ikat without seeing whether the pattern resolves in both the warp and the weft directions. You cannot infer authenticity of origin at all, because origin is a legal and factual question about who made the cloth and where, which no visual property answers - that is precisely the gap a registered origin-linked name is designed to address, and precisely why the registration protects a name rather than certifying a fabric.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a physical test can establish provenance. The two-faced test distinguishes yarn-resist construction from surface imitation, which is a claim about **technique**. It says nothing about which region, which community or which registered producer made the cloth. Confusing a technique test with an origin test is the same error as confusing a quality mark with a Geographical Indication.

## Lesson 7 - Pattern put onto cloth: painted, printed and the mordant question

Progress: 7/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Kalamkari as hand painting with vegetable dyes, block printing and Ajrakh, the mordant-and-resist logic, the boundary with the painting topic, and the named printed and painted registered traditions

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Kalamkari passage, the Ajrakh printing entry, the batik passage as the wax-resist neighbour, the Telia Rumal and Chamba Rumal entries that sit either side of this family, and the boundary statement routing painting styles to the painting chapter

CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four ways to put colour on a finished cloth

```text
   THE CLOTH IS ALREADY WOVEN. NOW WHAT?

   (1) DRAW IT           (2) STAMP IT          (3) BLOCK THE DYE   (4) STITCH IT
       by hand               with a carved         with wax           with a needle
                             wooden block                             (Lesson 8)
   -----------------     ------------------    ----------------    --------------
   KALAMKARI             AJRAKH PRINTING       BATIK               CHIKANKARI,
   hand painting on      Gujarat;              molten wax at one   KASHIDA,
   fabrics using         a block-printed       end, then dyed in   PHULKARI,
   VEGETABLE DYES        textile               cold wax;           KANTHA...
   with deep colours                           multicoloured
   commonly practised                          sarees, dupattas
   in ANDHRA PRADESH                           MP and West Bengal
                                               ORIGIN: INDONESIA

   UNDER ALL OF (1) AND (2) SITS THE MORDANT IDEA:

     plain cotton
         |
         +-- apply MORDANT where colour is wanted  (a metal salt that
         |   binds dye to fibre)                    grips both fibre and dye
         |
         +-- immerse the WHOLE cloth in one dye bath
         |
         v
     colour appears ONLY where the mordant was placed,
     and DIFFERENT mordants under the SAME dye give DIFFERENT colours

   So the artisan is not painting colour. The artisan is painting
   INSTRUCTIONS, and the dye bath executes them. That is why these are
   cotton traditions, and why the drawing tool matters less than the
   chemistry.
```

The mordant idea is the single most useful thing in this lesson, because it explains why the same dye plant yields several colours, why the process runs through repeated washing and dyeing rather than one application, and why cotton rather than silk is the natural ground.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Kalamkari" is usually filed next to Madhubani and Warli as an Indian folk *painting*. That filing loses the examinable content. Kalamkari's identity is that it is a **textile process**: the support is cloth, the colourant is a vegetable dye system, and the work is done on a ground prepared to receive mordants. Filing it as painting also produces the boundary error in the opposite direction - importing pigment-on-paper traditions into a textile answer, where they do not belong.

### 2. Kalamkari, stated exactly

The source's record is short and should be held verbatim: among processes from ancient times that are still in use is **Kalamkari, which utilises the art of hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours; this is commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh**.

Three elements, each of which discriminates:

- **Hand painting**, not printing. The pattern is drawn, which is why Kalamkari carries narrative and figural content that a repeat block cannot produce.
- **On fabrics**, which is what makes it a textile craft rather than a painting style.
- **Vegetable dyes with deep colours**, which places it inside the mordant-and-dye-bath system rather than the pigment-and-binder system of painting on paper or wall.

The topic's own record adds a fourth element as an analytical caution: the wider craft record describes Kalamkari as hand-drawn **or block-printed** cotton worked with **mordant and resist** processes at centres in **Andhra Pradesh and Telangana**. The two accounts are not in conflict - one records the core hand-painted identity, the other records that the tradition spans a hand-drawn and a block-printed practice. The safe formulation is to lead with the source's definition, and to note that the tradition includes a block-printed branch. **This session does not name the individual centre-styles within Kalamkari**, because the held sources do not print them, and it does not supply them from elsewhere. That gap is declared rather than filled.

### 3. Block printing, and Ajrakh

Block printing stamps a carved wooden block, charged with mordant or dye, repeatedly across the cloth. Its defining properties are the opposite of hand painting's: a **repeat** unit, a **registration** problem - each impression must align with its neighbours - and the possibility of building a complex design through several blocks used in sequence, one per colour or per mordant.

The source records **Ajrakh printing** for **Gujarat**, describing it as a **block-printed textile**. It is the tradition to name when a question asks for a printed - as against a painted or a woven - Indian cloth. Wax-resisted batik sits immediately beside this family, and is a useful reminder that the categories in this lesson are analytical conveniences that real workshops cross.

### 4. The mordant, explained once and used forever

A **mordant** is a substance - typically a metal salt - that forms a bridge between a dye molecule and a fibre. Without it, most plant dyes wash out of cotton. With it, they lock.

The craft consequence is counter-intuitive and worth stating slowly. The artisan does not apply the colour. The artisan applies the **mordant**, in the shape of the design; then the whole cloth goes into a single dye bath; and the dye takes only where the mordant is. Because different mordants bind the same dye differently, one dye bath can yield more than one colour on the same cloth, depending on which mordant was painted or printed where. Repeat the cycle with a second mordant pattern and a second bath, wash between stages, and a multi-coloured, wash-fast cloth is built up.

Four things follow, and all four are examinable:

1. **Cotton is the natural ground**, because it is absorbent and takes mordants well.
2. **The process is long and involves repeated washing**, which is why these traditions historically sit near water.
3. **The design must be planned in reverse**, in terms of which areas receive which treatment rather than which areas will look which colour.
4. **Colour is inside the fibre, not on top of it**, which is why a mordant-dyed cloth survives washing in a way a surface pigment does not - and why the comparison with a merely printed pigment imitation is not snobbery but a statement about durability.

### 5. The boundary with the painting topic, stated so it is never crossed

This is one of the two places where this session touches another cultural topic closely, and the rule is simple.

| Question | Owner | Why |
|---|---|---|
| The composition, iconography and stylistic development of folk and tribal **painting** traditions | the painting topic | they are painting styles, and their support is usually paper, wall or floor |
| A **painted cloth** as a textile process - ground preparation, mordant, dye, wash | **this session** | the operation is a textile-finishing operation |
| Pigment technology on wall and paper - binders, grounds, plaster | the painting topic | different chemistry, different support |
| A **printed cloth** - block, repeat, registration, mordant | **this session** | a textile-finishing operation |
| A **mask** or an object that happens to be painted | **this session**, as a made object | it is a craft object; its painted decoration is part of its making |

The practical instruction: if the question names a *cloth*, the process is yours; if it names a *style*, route it out and say so in one clause.

### 6. Comparative treatment: painted, printed and resisted cloth

| | Hand-painted (Kalamkari) | Block-printed (Ajrakh) | Wax-resisted (batik) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Tool** | drawing instrument | carved wooden block | molten wax applicator |
| **Unit of design** | the free-drawn figure | the repeat | the waxed area |
| **What is applied** | mordant or dye, by hand | mordant or dye, by block | wax, which blocks dye |
| **Colour logic** | positive - draw where colour is wanted | positive - stamp where colour is wanted | negative - cover where colour is *not* wanted |
| **Narrative capacity** | high; figural and story-carrying | limited by the repeat | limited; produces characteristic crackle and pooling |
| **Speed** | slow | faster; the reason printing exists | moderate |
| **Where recorded here** | commonly practised in **Andhra Pradesh** | **Gujarat** | **Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal**; originated in **Indonesia** |

Why do they differ? Because each answers a different question about how much control the maker wants over each square inch, and at what cost. Hand painting buys narrative freedom with time. Printing buys speed with repetition. Wax resist buys a distinctive negative-image aesthetic with the loss of fine positive detail. There is no hierarchy among them; there is a trade-off, and naming the trade-off is what turns a description into analysis.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** The distinction between painting and printing is a distinction of tool, not of tradition. Kalamkari is described in one place as hand painting and in another as spanning hand-drawn and block-printed work. If the same named tradition contains both, the category is not doing any work.

**Reply.** The category does real work, but at the level of the **individual cloth**, not the tradition name. For any given piece, either the figures were drawn or they were stamped, and the difference is visible, consequential and identifiable: a drawn line varies in width and never repeats exactly; a stamped motif repeats precisely and shows edge registration. A tradition that contains both branches is a tradition whose workshops made different trade-offs for different markets - narrative pieces by hand, yardage by block. So the honest formulation is that **Kalamkari names a mordant-dyed cotton tradition of Andhra Pradesh and Telangana whose core recorded identity is hand painting with vegetable dyes and which also includes block-printed work**. That sentence carries the source, carries the wider record, carries the technical distinction, and asserts nothing further.

### Examination use

The verbatim line to hold is that **Kalamkari utilises the art of hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours and is commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh** - it is the form in which this tradition has actually appeared in reproduced objective material. **Ajrakh printing of Gujarat** is the printed counterpart and should be paired with it. The **mordant mechanism** is the highest-value explanatory idea in the lesson and converts an identification answer into a process answer: *the artisan paints the mordant, the dye bath supplies the colour, and different mordants under one dye give different colours*. For boundary questions, the rule "cloth is mine, style is theirs" should be written into the answer as a single clause rather than left implicit.

### Traps introduced here

- Filing Kalamkari as a painting style rather than as a textile process.
- Saying Kalamkari is printed, or that it is only painted; the record supports a hand-painted core identity within a tradition that includes block-printed work.
- Attributing batik to India; it originated in Indonesia and is recorded as most popular in Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal.
- Treating painted and printed cloth as "dyed cloth" without distinguishing mordant application from immersion.
- Importing paper-and-wall painting traditions into a textile answer.
- Naming specific Kalamkari centre-styles that the held sources do not print.

### Revision notes

1. **Kalamkari** = **hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours**, **commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh**; the wider craft record adds hand-drawn **or block-printed** cotton with **mordant and resist** processes at Andhra Pradesh and Telangana centres.
2. **Ajrakh printing** = **Gujarat**, a **block-printed textile**.
3. **Batik** = wax resist; **molten wax**, then **cold wax** dyeing; **multicoloured sarees and dupattas**; **Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal**; **originated in Indonesia**.
4. A **mordant** bridges dye and fibre. The artisan applies the **mordant** in the shape of the design; the **dye bath** supplies the colour; **different mordants under one dye give different colours**.
5. Consequences of mordant dyeing: cotton ground; long process with repeated washing; design planned as treatments rather than colours; colour inside the fibre and therefore wash-fast.
6. **Painting versus printing:** drawn lines vary and never repeat; stamped motifs repeat exactly and show registration edges.
7. **Positive versus negative logic:** painting and printing put something where colour *is* wanted; wax resist puts something where colour is *not* wanted.
8. **Boundary:** a painted or printed **cloth** is a textile process and belongs here; a painting **style** belongs to the painting topic.
9. Trade-offs: hand painting buys narrative freedom with time; printing buys speed with repetition; wax resist buys a negative-image aesthetic at the cost of fine positive detail.
10. Declared limit: this session does not name individual Kalamkari centre-styles, because the held sources do not print them.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain the mordant-and-dye-bath mechanism underlying Indian painted and printed cotton traditions, and show how it shapes the character of those traditions. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

In mordant dyeing the artisan does not apply colour. A mordant - a substance that binds a dye to a fibre - is painted or block-printed onto the cloth in the shape of the design; the whole cloth is then immersed in a dye bath, and the dye takes only where the mordant lies. Because different mordants bind the same dye differently, a single bath can yield several colours.

Four consequences follow. The ground is **cotton**, because cotton is absorbent and mordant-friendly. The process is **long and water-intensive**, involving repeated washing between stages. The design must be planned as a **sequence of treatments** rather than as a picture. And the colour ends up **inside the fibre**, making the cloth wash-fast.

This is why Kalamkari - hand painting on fabrics with vegetable dyes, commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh - and block-printed Ajrakh of Gujarat are cotton traditions rather than silk ones.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = the mordant mechanism explained as instruction-then-execution rather than as "a fixing agent" 4 + the four structural consequences 4 + two correctly placed named traditions used as illustration, not as a list 2.** Describing Kalamkari as a painting style rather than a textile process costs the final two marks.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Kalamkari and Madhubani are two great Indian painting traditions; both use natural colours and depict mythological scenes." Identify what is defensible, what is misplaced, and how the sentence should be rebuilt for a craft-and-textiles answer.

**Model answer:** What is **defensible** is thin: both traditions do use natural colourants, and both carry narrative content. What is **misplaced** is the classification, and the classification is what the marks attach to. **Kalamkari is a textile process** - the source defines it as hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours, commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh, and the wider record places it within mordant-and-resist processes on cotton at Andhra Pradesh and Telangana centres. Its support is cloth, its chemistry is mordant-and-dye-bath, and its colour ends up inside the fibre. **Madhubani belongs to the painting topic**, whose support, pigment system and stylistic questions are different in kind; importing it into a textiles answer both loses marks here and trespasses on another owner. Grouping the two as "painting traditions" also conceals the technical content that an examiner can test - namely that in Kalamkari the artisan applies a mordant in the shape of the design and the dye bath supplies the colour. A rebuilt sentence for this topic would read: Kalamkari is a mordant-dyed cotton textile tradition of Andhra Pradesh, whose recorded core identity is hand painting with vegetable dyes and which also includes block-printed work; its narrative capacity comes from being drawn rather than stamped, and its durability from colour bound inside the fibre.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking that "uses natural colours and shows mythological scenes" is a classification. It is a description that fits dozens of unrelated traditions across different supports, chemistries and owners. Classification in this topic runs on **support and operation** - what the thing is made of, and what was physically done to it - and never on subject matter.

### Visual: the identification grid - four questions that separate every tradition here

```text
               AN EMBROIDERED CLOTH ARRIVES
                          |
   Q1. WHAT IS THE THREAD?
       white cotton ------------------> CHIKANKARI (Lucknow)
       silk, floss, bright ------------> PHULKARI / BAGH (practised in
                                          Punjab, Haryana, Delhi;
                                          registered for Punjab, Haryana,
                                          Rajasthan; Bagh on GREEN cloth)
       gold / silver metallic (zari) --> GOTA, KARCHOBI, ZARDOZI family
                                          (Rajasthan; Khandela and Jaipur
                                          for zari work)
       running-stitch cotton from
         reused cloth -----------------> KANTHA / NAKSHI KANTHA (West Bengal)
       muga silk, dark colours ---------> MANIPUR, on the borders of PHANEKS
       wool / fine thread on shawls ----> KASHIDA (Kashmir, on Cashmere shawls)
                          |
   Q2. WHAT IS ADDED BESIDES THREAD?
       tiny mirrors -------------------> SHISHA (Kutch, Gujarat) - fixed with
                                          HERRINGBONE and SATIN stitch
       cut fabric pieces --------------> APPLIQUE / PIPLI WORK (Pipli village,
                                          Odisha) - also used to make LAMPS
       cotton stuffing under flat
         stitches ---------------------> KARCHOBI (Rajasthan) - RAISED zari-like
                                          metallic pattern
                          |
   Q3. WHAT IS THE STITCH GEOMETRY?
       darning to make flower-like
         patterns ---------------------> PHULKARI ("making flowers")
       cross stitch, geometrical
         motifs -----------------------> KASUTI (Karnataka), folk embroidery,
                                          possibly dating to the CHALUKYAN era
       running stitch, quilting --------> KANTHA
                          |
   Q4. WHAT IS THE GROUND?
       cotton, mulmul ka cotton,
         polyester, voile -------------> CHIKANKARI's recorded grounds
       green cloth --------------------> BAGH
       Cashmere shawl -----------------> KASHIDA
       handkerchief -------------------> CHAMBA RUMAL (Himachal Pradesh),
                                          Pahari painting influence, KRISHNA
                                          themes and nature, often WEDDING GIFTS
```

Four questions, asked in order, place every tradition in this lesson without any need to recall a state first.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Embroidery is where name-to-state storage fails hardest, because there are a dozen traditions, several of them from the same broad region, and an examiner can describe any of them by process without naming it. The remedy is to stop learning names and start learning the **four variables** - thread, added material, stitch geometry, ground. Every tradition in the record is a distinct combination of those four, and the combination is what an item actually describes.

### 2. The definition, and why it is worth quoting

The source defines the family before listing it: amongst the crafts used on textiles, the art of creating **raised designs using threads or wooden blocks** is unique; this art is called **embroidery**, and it can be performed using **threads of gold, silver, silk or cotton** on cloth using **sewing needles**.

Two things in that definition repay attention. First, **raised** - embroidery adds relief, which is what distinguishes it by touch as well as by sight from anything printed or woven flat. Second, the four thread materials are named, and they map directly onto the first question of the identification grid.

### 3. The traditions, each as a four-variable record

**Chikankari (Chikan embroidery), Lucknow, Uttar Pradesh.** The source says Chikankari **defines the culture and cloth-making process in Lucknow**; it **uses white thread** to make **flowers and other beautiful patterns**; and it can be carried out on a variety of materials - **cotton, mulmul ka cotton, polyester and voile**. The examinable core is *white thread on a fine, often sheer ground*, worked with a needle after weaving. It is the permanent counterpart to Banaras brocade in the weave-versus-surface pair.

**Kashida, Kashmir.** Kashmir is well known for the **fine workmanship of Kashida embroidery**, which is **generally carried out on its famous Cashmere shawls**. The discriminating variable here is the **ground**: Kashida is defined in the record by the object it decorates.

**Phulkari, and its two different records.** The name **literally means 'making flowers'**, and the technique is **darning to create colourful flower-like patterns on the cloth**. Two statements in the record must be held apart, because they answer different questions.

- **Where the technique is practised.** The source's prose records Phulkari as **used in Punjab, Haryana and Delhi**.
- **What the registered indication covers.** The registered-goods row for Phulkari gives its geographical scope as **Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan** - and the official register records the same three States.

Those are not the same list, and neither is wrong. A practice statement describes where the needle is worked; a registration statement describes the territory the protected name covers. **Delhi appears in the first and not in the second; Rajasthan appears in the second and not in the first.** This session prints both and does not collapse them. Whichever way the question is framed, Phulkari remains the standing refutation of one-state exclusivity in the embroidery family - because on either record it is a three-State craft.

**Bagh, Punjab.** Bagh uses **silk threads** to embroider flower-like patterns on a **green cloth**. The ground colour is the discriminator, and the relationship to Phulkari is the kind of near-neighbour pair examiners build.

**Nakshi Kantha, West Bengal.** Listed among the famous embroideries as a registered good of **West Bengal**, with the record noting that the thread is drawn from **used cloth**. Kantha's identity is the reuse: running-stitch work built from and on recycled fabric. The wider craft record describes it as **running-stitch quilting and embroidery from reused cloth traditions** in **Bengal**, and marks reuse as **intrinsic to the form** - which is why calling Kantha "a Bengali floral embroidery" misses the point of it entirely.

**Kasuti, Karnataka.** In Karnataka, **beautiful geometrical motifs are created in cross stitch** in the **folk embroidery of Kasuti**. These are **hand-made** and **possibly date back to the Chalukyan era**. The hedge *possibly* is part of the fact. Kasuti's discriminator is the **stitch geometry**: cross stitch producing geometrical rather than naturalistic motifs.

**Shisha, Kutch, Gujarat.** Kutch is known for its **mirror work embroidery, called Shisha**, in which **tiny pieces of mirror are fixed to the fabric using herringbone and satin stitch**. The discriminator is the **added material**, and the two named stitches are the mechanism by which a rigid object is held on a flexible ground.

**The zari and metallic family, Rajasthan.** Three separate entries that must not be merged:

- **Gota (Rajasthan)** uses **gold threads** to embroider and to create **applique-like patterns** on the cloth.
- **Khandela and Jaipur** are **two famous places for zari work**.
- **Karchobi (Rajasthan)** entails **sewing flat stitches on cotton stuffing** to create a **raised zari-like metallic thread pattern**. The stuffing is the discriminator: Karchobi's relief is built, not merely stitched.

Alongside these, the record places **Banaras Brocade** - famous for **silver and golden zari work** on sarees, with **floral motifs**, **generally silk**, and **two to eight weeks** per piece - inside the embroidery section while describing it as a brocade. That placement is a genuine internal tension in the source and is recorded in this session as an open discrepancy rather than silently resolved. The safe examination position: **Banaras brocade is a supplementary-weft woven tradition**; metallic thread can be either woven in or stitched on, and the tradition names that use zari must each be checked for which one they mean.

**Chamba Rumals, Himachal Pradesh.** Embroidered **handkerchiefs** showing **Pahari painting influence**, embroidered with **Krishna themes and nature**, **often given as wedding gifts**, and made under the **patronage of the rulers** of the region. The record also lists Chamba Rumal as a registered good and, separately, among the fifty iconic textile crafts documented in 2022. Its four variables are unusually complete - thread, ground (a handkerchief), motif source (Pahari painting), and function (a gift) - which is why it is the single best embroidery case for a Mains paragraph.

**Manipuri muga-thread embroidery.** In Manipur, **delicate muga silk thread embroidery in dark colours** is carried out on the **borders of phaneks** - described here as traditional shawls - **worn by women during auspicious occasions such as weddings**. Note that the phanek is elsewhere recorded as a wraparound skirt; this session prints both descriptions and records the difference rather than choosing between them.

**Applique, or Pipli work, Pipli village, Odisha.** A **type of patchwork made of embroidered colourful fabric that is sewn together to create a single piece**, and the source adds that the technique **is used to make beautiful lamps**. Applique is strictly not embroidery - no raised thread design is the primary operation - but it sits in the same family because it is surface construction on a ground, and the source groups it there.

**Sujni embroidery, Bihar.** Sujni is an embroidery tradition of **Bihar**, associated with the **Muzaffarpur** area. It belongs with Kantha in the broad family of stitched work built on layered cloth, and it is recorded here for its **region** alone: this session holds **no process description, motif vocabulary or registration year** for it, and supplies none. It is included because the region attribution is examinable and because a bare guess between neighbouring states is exactly the error this lesson exists to prevent.

### 4. The near-neighbour pairs, and the one fact that separates each

| Pair | The one separating fact |
|---|---|
| **Chikankari** and **Banaras brocade** | one is white thread worked with a needle on finished cloth; the other is metallic supplementary weft built during weaving |
| **Phulkari** and **Bagh** | Bagh uses **silk threads on a green cloth**; Phulkari is darning in colourful flower-like patterns, practised across Punjab, Haryana and Delhi and registered for Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan |
| **Gota** and **Karchobi** | Gota creates applique-like patterns with gold thread; Karchobi builds **raised** metallic pattern over **cotton stuffing** |
| **Kasuti** and **Phulkari** | Kasuti is **cross stitch**, geometrical, Karnataka; Phulkari is **darning**, floral, north-western |
| **Kantha** and any decorative floral embroidery | Kantha's thread and ground come from **reused cloth**; reuse is intrinsic, not incidental |
| **Shisha** and any other Kutch work | Shisha is defined by **mirror pieces**, fixed with **herringbone and satin** stitch |
| **Chamba Rumal** and **Telia Rumal** | both are *rumals*; one is an **embroidered handkerchief** of Himachal Pradesh with Pahari influence, the other a **resist-dyed textile** historically associated with Chirala, Andhra Pradesh, whose registered form is *Puttapaka Telia Rumal* of Telangana |
| **Kashida** and **Kani** | Kashida is **embroidery on** Cashmere shawls; Kani is a **woven** shawl of pashm |

The last two pairs are the ones to rehearse hardest, because in each case the two members share a word or a region and differ in operation - which is exactly the shape of a well-made examination item.

### 5. Comparative treatment: what embroidery does that weaving cannot

Weaving builds pattern into structure; embroidery builds pattern onto structure. That difference has four consequences worth arguing rather than merely listing.

1. **Embroidery is separable from cloth production.** A weaver must own or access a loom; an embroiderer needs a needle, thread and a ground. This lowers the entry cost and is one structural reason why embroidery traditions are often household and often women's work - a point developed in Lesson 16.
2. **Embroidery can be added to bought cloth.** That makes it responsive to market taste in a way a loom set-up is not, and it also makes it vulnerable, because the value added can be squeezed by whoever supplies the ground fabric.
3. **Embroidery carries relief and applied material.** Mirrors, stuffing, metallic thread and cut fabric are all possible; none is possible on the loom.
4. **Embroidery is repairable and re-workable.** Kantha's entire logic - building new cloth from used cloth and drawing thread from it - is only available to a surface technique.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** The four-variable grid treats these traditions as recipes. But an embroidery tradition is a community's visual language, and reducing Phulkari to "darning, floral, three states" strips it of exactly what makes it a cultural form.

**Reply.** The objection identifies a real risk and points at the wrong remedy. The risk is reduction; the wrong remedy is to replace technical content with invented meaning. In this topic, the alternative to a technical record is not richer cultural understanding but fabricated symbolism - motifs assigned significance no source records, communities assigned a timeless relationship to a pattern. The grid is what makes a **defensible** cultural statement possible: once you know that Kasuti is cross stitch producing geometrical motifs, is hand-made, is described as folk embroidery, and is *possibly* Chalukyan in ancestry, you can say something about its place in Karnataka's material culture that survives scrutiny. Where the record supplies cultural content - that Chamba Rumals were royal-patronised, Pahari-influenced and given as wedding gifts, or that Nagaland's motifs reflect the cultural identity of each tribe - write it, exactly. Where it does not, the honest move is silence, not invention.

### Examination use

The **four-variable grid** is the deliverable of this lesson, and it should be run in order - thread, added material, stitch geometry, ground - on any described cloth. The **near-neighbour table** is the direct preparation for matching items, and the Chamba Rumal versus Telia Rumal pair is the highest-value single row in it. The **Chikankari record** - white thread, on cotton, mulmul ka cotton, polyester and voile, at Lucknow - is quotable and carries an unusual detail, the list of grounds, that most candidates do not hold. For a livelihood or gender answer, the **low entry cost of embroidery** compared with weaving is a structural argument, not an assertion, and it connects this lesson directly to Lesson 16.

### Traps introduced here

- Calling any metallic-thread textile "zari embroidery" without checking whether the metal was woven in or stitched on.
- Merging Gota, Karchobi and zardozi-type work; the record separates them by technique and by what lies under the stitch.
- Treating Kantha as a decorative floral style and dropping the reuse that defines it.
- Confusing Kashida, an embroidery on shawls, with Kani, a woven shawl.
- Confusing Chamba Rumal, an embroidered handkerchief, with Telia Rumal, a resist-dyed textile.
- Confining Phulkari to Punjab, or quoting only one of its two records: the practice passage gives Punjab, Haryana and Delhi, while the registered indication covers Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan.
- Dropping the hedge on Kasuti's Chalukyan ancestry, which the source marks as *possible*.

### Revision notes

1. **Embroidery** = creating **raised designs** using **threads of gold, silver, silk or cotton** on cloth with **sewing needles**.
2. **Chikankari**: **Lucknow**; **white thread**; flowers and other patterns; grounds recorded as **cotton, mulmul ka cotton, polyester and voile**.
3. **Kashida**: **Kashmir**; fine workmanship; generally on **Cashmere shawls**.
4. **Phulkari**: literally **making flowers**; **darning**. Two records: practised in **Punjab, Haryana and Delhi**; the registered indication covers **Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan**. Print both; do not merge them.
5. **Bagh**: **Punjab**; **silk threads**; flower-like patterns on a **green cloth**.
6. **Nakshi Kantha**: **West Bengal**; thread drawn from **used cloth**; running-stitch quilting; **reuse is intrinsic**.
7. **Kasuti**: **Karnataka**; **cross stitch**; **geometrical motifs**; hand-made; **possibly** Chalukyan-era.
8. **Shisha**: **Kutch, Gujarat**; **tiny mirror pieces** fixed with **herringbone and satin stitch**.
9. **Gota** (Rajasthan) = gold thread, applique-like patterns. **Karchobi** (Rajasthan) = flat stitches over **cotton stuffing**, raised metallic effect. **Khandela and Jaipur** = famous zari-work places.
10. **Chamba Rumal**: **Himachal Pradesh**; embroidered **handkerchief**; **Pahari painting influence**; **Krishna themes and nature**; **often wedding gifts**; royal patronage; registered good; among the fifty textile crafts documented in 2022.
11. **Manipuri muga-thread embroidery**: **dark colours** on the **borders of phaneks**, worn by women at **weddings and auspicious occasions**. The phanek is elsewhere recorded as a **wraparound skirt**; both descriptions are printed here.
12. **Applique / Pipli work**: **Pipli village, Odisha**; **patchwork of embroidered colourful fabric sewn into a single piece**; **used to make lamps**. **Sujni embroidery**: **Bihar**, associated with **Muzaffarpur**; region recorded, no process or year asserted.
13. **Banaras brocade** is placed in the embroidery section of the source while being described as a brocade; the safe position is that it is a **woven** supplementary-weft tradition. Recorded as an open discrepancy.
14. Embroidery's structural properties: low entry cost, separable from weaving, can carry applied materials, and is repairable - which is why Kantha is possible at all.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Indian embroidery traditions are often identified only by state. Explain, with examples, why identification by thread, applied material, stitch and ground is analytically superior. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

State-based identification collapses whenever an examiner describes a process instead of naming a product, and it fails outright where one state holds several traditions or one tradition spans several states.

**Thread** separates the closest pairs: Chikankari of Lucknow uses white thread on cotton, mulmul, polyester or voile; Bagh uses silk thread on a green cloth; Manipuri work uses dark muga silk on phanek borders.

**Applied material** separates Kutch's Shisha, defined by tiny mirrors fixed with herringbone and satin stitch, from Rajasthan's Karchobi, whose raised metallic effect is built over cotton stuffing.

**Stitch geometry** separates Karnataka's Kasuti, a cross-stitch folk embroidery of geometrical motifs, from Phulkari's darned floral work.

**Ground** separates Kashida, worked on Cashmere shawls, from Chamba Rumal, worked on a handkerchief.

Phulkari itself, practised across Punjab, Haryana and Delhi yet registered for Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan, shows why the state cannot be the primary key.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = each of the four variables used to separate a genuine near-neighbour pair 6 + one case where a single tradition spans several states 2 + one case where one process word is shared by two different operations, such as the two *rumals* 2.** A list of embroideries with states, however long, scores at most three.

### Concept check

**Question:** An item describes "a Himachal textile worked with a needle, showing Krishna themes drawn from a regional painting style, traditionally made under royal patronage and presented at weddings", and a second describes "an Andhra textile whose name means oily and whose pattern is produced by resist-dyeing". Identify both, and state the general lesson the pair teaches.

**Model answer:** The first is the **Chamba Rumal** of **Himachal Pradesh**: an **embroidered handkerchief** made under the **patronage of the rulers** of the region, showing **Pahari painting influence**, embroidered with **Krishna themes and nature**, and **often given as wedding gifts**. The second is the **Telia Rumal** of **Chirala, Andhra Pradesh**: a **resist-dyed textile** whose name the source glosses as **oily**, referring to the oil treatment the yarn undergoes in preparation. The general lesson is that **a shared word in a craft name is not a shared technique**. Both objects are *rumals* - square cloths of comparable size and everyday category - and the resemblance ends there. One is decorated **after weaving, with a needle**, and its motif vocabulary is borrowed from a painting tradition; the other has its pattern decided **before weaving, in the yarn**, by binding sections against the dye. Region, operation, moment of decoration and design source all differ. The identification grid handles this without difficulty, because its first question is about thread and its second about what else was applied - and the Telia Rumal answers neither, which immediately routes it out of the embroidery family and into the resist family of Lesson 6.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using a craft name's shared element - *rumal*, *jamdani*, *ikat*, *brocade*, *chikan* - as evidence of a shared tradition. Some of these words name an **object type**, some name an **operation**, and some name a **place**. Deciding which kind of word you are holding is the first move in any identification, and skipping it is what produces confidently wrong matches.
