CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four operations, and every named metal craft placed by operation

```text
  OPERATION 1: CASTING - pour molten metal into a mould
  --------------------------------------------------------------
   lost wax: model in wax -> encase -> melt the wax out ->
             pour metal into the void -> break the mould
       -> BASTAR DHOKRA (GI) ......... used for brass ornaments
       -> the ancient anchor: BRONZE DANCING GIRL, Mohenjo-daro,
          c. 2500 BC; copper + tin = bronze, the earliest
          non-ferrous metals used by man
       -> texts: MATSYA PURANA describes bronze casting methods;
          RASARATNAKARA discusses metal purity and zinc distillation
       -> centres: UTTAR PRADESH takes precedence - ETAWAH, SITAPUR,
          VARANASI, MORADABAD; ritual objects TAMRAPATRA,
          KANCHANTAL, PANCHPATRA
       -> TAMIL NADU: statues inspired by Pallava, Chola, Pandya,
          Nayaka art

  OPERATION 2: SHAPING SHEET METAL - hammer, raise, emboss
  --------------------------------------------------------------
       REPOUSSE (embossing) -> raised designs
       -> PEMBARTHI METAL CRAFT (GI), TELANGANA: exquisite SHEET
          METAL (BRASS) art to adorn CHARIOTS AND TEMPLES
       -> BADLA: a semi-circular or rounded ZINC pot, MARWAR region,
          Rajasthan
       -> PAHALDAR LAMPS: copper and brass lamps in different styles

  OPERATION 3: CUTTING INTO THE SURFACE - engrave, then fill or not
  --------------------------------------------------------------
       ENGRAVING -> cut-line patterns
       -> MORADABAD: BAARIK KAAM ("delicate work"), perfected using
          the NAKKASHI or engraving style, to create pots
       -> MARORI (Rajasthan): etchings on the base metal, the gaps
          then FILLED WITH BLACK LAC
       -> TARKASHI (Rajasthan): fine COPPER or BRASS WIRES embedded
          into CHISELLED GROOVES
       -> KOFTGIRI (JAIPUR and ALWAR): inlaying LIGHT metals onto a
          DARK surface
       -> BIDRI CRAFT (GI), BIDAR, KARNATAKA: BLACKENED ZINC AND COPPER
          ALLOY inlaid with SILVER - hookahs, decorative items

  OPERATION 4: ALLOYING FOR A PROPERTY - make the metal do something
  --------------------------------------------------------------
       -> ARANMULA KANNADI (GI), Kerala: a handmade METAL-ALLOY
          MIRROR, polished for SEVERAL DAYS to obtain its surface;
          the ALLOY COMPOSITION is held within the FAMILIES INVOLVED

  AND, IN SILVER:
       FILIGREE - fine wire worked into openwork
       -> ODISHA: silver anklets PAINRI and PAIJAM; knitted silver
          ornaments called GUNCHI
```

Four operations. Every named metal craft in the record falls into one of them, and several fall into two - which is the point at which a candidate stops memorising and starts identifying.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Bidri, Koftgiri, Tarkashi and Marori are four Indian metal crafts that all produce a dark surface with a bright pattern, are all associated with north and central India, and are constantly confused. They differ in exactly one respect each: **what the pattern is made of, and how it is held in place**. Once that single question is asked, the four separate and stay separated.

### 2. Casting, and the bronze anchor

The source opens bronze with its oldest object - **one of the oldest art forms is bronze work, as evinced by the bronze statue of a Dancing Girl from Mohenjo-daro, which has been dated to around 2500 BC** - and then supplies the chemistry: **the earliest non-ferrous metals used by man were copper and tin, which were mixed to form bronze**.

That second sentence is doing more work than it appears to. Bronze is an **alloy**, and the reason alloying matters is that it changes the metal's behaviour: it lowers the melting point and improves the flow into a mould, which is precisely what casting requires. The move from copper to bronze is therefore not a matter of fashion; it is the enabling condition for cast sculpture.

Two texts are recorded: the **Matsya Purana** describes **bronze casting methods**, and **Rasaratnakara** discusses **metal purity and zinc distillation**. Keep the two apart - one is about casting, the other about metallurgy - and keep both out of the religion topic, where the Purana otherwise belongs.

The centres are recorded with an explicit ranking: **amongst the bronze-craft-producing areas, Uttar Pradesh takes precedence**, with major centres at **Etawah, Sitapur, Varanasi and Moradabad**, producing decorative items such as **flower pots and images of Gods and Goddesses**, and ritual objects including **tamrapatra, kanchantal and panchpatra**. **Tamil Nadu** is known for **statues inspired by Pallava, Chola, Pandya and Nayaka art**.

That last entry is a boundary case worth naming. The *iconography* of Chola bronzes belongs to the sculpture owner. What belongs here is that **Tamil Nadu is a present-day metal-statue centre whose products are inspired by those earlier idioms** - a craft fact about contemporary production, not an art-historical claim about medieval bronzes.

### 3. Lost-wax casting, and Dhokra

Lost-wax casting is the technique behind most Indian figural metal work, and it is worth walking through because it explains the craft's economics. A model is built in **wax**; it is encased in a clay or clay-and-dung mould; the assembly is heated so that the wax **runs out**, leaving a void in the exact shape of the model; molten metal is poured into that void; and when the metal has set, the **mould is broken** to release the object.

Two consequences follow, and both are examinable.

1. **Every piece is unique.** The mould is destroyed in the process and the wax model no longer exists. There is no reusable pattern, which means there is no mass production and no two identical objects - the opposite of a die-cast or moulded product.
2. **Failure is total.** A flaw in the pour ruins the object, and the model cannot be recovered to try again. The labour, like yarn-resist labour in Lesson 6, is front-loaded and irrecoverable.

**Bastar Dhokra** is recorded as a registered craft, **used for brass ornaments**, among the current important centres of brass work across India. Two disciplines attach. First, **Dhokra is a lost-wax non-ferrous casting tradition practised by artisan communities across central and eastern India, and Bastar is a registered instance of it - not its sole home**; treating the registered name as the whole tradition is a textbook case of one-state exclusivity. Second, the record names the registered good and its use; it does not supply a registration year, and this session supplies none.

### 4. The inlay and engraving family, separated once and for all

| Craft | Where | What the surface is | What the pattern is made of | How it is held |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Bidri** (registered) | **Karnataka** - standardly associated with **Bidar**, and printed in the core record as "Bidri village" | **blackened zinc-and-copper alloy** | **silver** | inlaid into the alloy body; the blackening creates the contrast that makes the silver read |
| **Koftgiri** | **Jaipur and Alwar** | a **dark** surface | **light metals** | **inlaid onto** the dark surface |
| **Tarkashi** | **Rajasthan** | base metal | **fine copper or brass wires** | **embedded into chiselled grooves** |
| **Marori** | **Rajasthan** | base metal | the **absence** of metal - the pattern is the etched void | **etchings filled with black lac** |
| **Nakkashi / Baarik Kaam** | **Moradabad** | metal pots | **cut lines** | nothing is added; the pattern is the engraving itself |
| **Repousse** | general | sheet metal | **raised relief** | nothing is added; the metal is pushed from behind |

Read the fourth column down the table. Bidri's pattern is silver; Koftgiri's is a light metal; Tarkashi's is wire; Marori's is lac in an etched void; Nakkashi's is a cut line; repousse's is displaced metal. **Six different answers to one question**, and that question - *what is the pattern physically made of?* - is the whole discrimination.

One further record belongs beside them: **Pembarthi metal craft**, registered, of **Pembarthi in Telangana**, described as **exquisite sheet metal (brass) art to adorn chariots and temples**. Pembarthi is sheet work with a *stated function* - it decorates chariots and temples - and function is a field that most metal entries leave empty, which makes Pembarthi unusually easy to identify. It is also the metal entry most often quoted without its State, so fix the pairing now: **Pembarthi is Telangana**, and the core record's brass table supplies the craft and its function while the State comes from the register.

**Bidri, and the place to name.** The craft is standardly associated with **Bidar in Karnataka**, and that is the association to give a learner and to write in an answer. The core record prints the location as **"Bidri village of Karnataka"**, notes the **elusive and stark contrast** the blackening creates for the shining silver, and records that the craft **has received GI status**; the registered entry describes it as a **blackened zinc and copper alloy** worked with **silver** to make items such as hookahs and decorative pieces. The printed place-name form differs from the familiar town name, and this session records that difference rather than silently deleting it. The **State - Karnataka - is consistent across both entries**, and **Bidar** is the safe town to name.

### 5. Alloy for a purpose: the Aranmula metal mirror

**Aranmula Kannadi**, registered, of **Kerala**, is a **handmade metal-alloy mirror**, made in a town in Kerala, described as **rare pieces** that are **polished for several days to obtain** the reflecting surface, with the **composition of the alloy** held within the **families involved**.

This single entry is the richest short passage in the metal record, and it carries four separate points:

- The object is a **mirror made of metal**, not of glass - so the reflecting surface is the metal itself, and its quality is a function of alloy and polish.
- **Polishing takes several days**, which is a rare explicit labour-time statement.
- The **alloy composition is a family secret**, which is a statement about **how craft knowledge is held** - not in a patent, not in a manual, but within kin.
- It is therefore the perfect case for the limits of an origin-linked name: the name can be protected, and the knowledge that makes the object possible is protected by an entirely different mechanism - secrecy within a family - which no statute created and none maintains.

### 6. Silver, and filigree

The source records that **one of the most famous techniques used by silver jewellery artists is filigree work**; that **Odisha is well known for its silver anklets called painri and paijam**; and that **uniquely knitted ornaments made of silver called gunchi** are also made there. Filigree is fine wire worked into openwork, which relates it operationally to Tarkashi's wire-and-groove inlay while differing in that filigree wire forms the object rather than decorating another surface.

### 7. Comparative treatment: why metal crafts cluster where they do

Metal crafts have a distinctive geography, and three of its drivers are visible in the record.

1. **Fuel and furnace.** Casting and alloying require sustained high temperature, so casting traditions concentrate where fuel and furnace-building knowledge exist. That is a strong reason for clustering, and clustering in turn creates the specialised labour pools that the named centres represent.
2. **Metal supply rather than ore.** None of the crafts here depends on a local ore body; they depend on **metal**, which is tradeable, meltable and recoverable. This is why metal-craft centres such as Moradabad can persist far from any mine, and it is the sharpest contrast with the wool, wood and clay crafts, whose material must be local or transported bulkily.
3. **Demand from institutions.** Ritual vessels - tamrapatra, kanchantal, panchpatra - temple and chariot ornament, and images of deities are institutional demands, which are steadier and more standardised than household demand. This is why metal centres often survive market changes that destroy more fashion-dependent crafts.

Why does this comparison matter? Because it supplies the best counter-example in this session to the "all crafts are declining" narrative. A craft with a recoverable material, an institutional buyer and a clustered labour pool has a materially different risk profile from a craft dependent on a scarce fibre and a discretionary buyer. Saying so is analysis; lamenting both equally is not.

### Examination use

The **operation table** in section 4 is the deliverable, and it should be rehearsed as a single question - *what is the pattern physically made of?* - with six answers. **Bidri** should be held as **blackened zinc-and-copper alloy inlaid with silver, Bidar, Karnataka, registered**; **Pembarthi** as **sheet brass for chariots and temples, Pembarthi, Telangana, registered**; **Aranmula Kannadi** as **a Kerala metal-alloy mirror, polished for several days, its alloy held within the families involved**. The **lost-wax uniqueness argument** - mould broken, model gone, no two pieces alike - is the strongest available answer to any question about why hand metal craft cannot be industrialised. The **two texts**, Matsya Purana on casting methods and Rasaratnakara on purity and zinc distillation, are the kind of specific citation that distinguishes a prepared answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Merging Bidri, Koftgiri, Tarkashi and Marori; each is separated by what the pattern is made of.
- Treating Dhokra as the property of a single state; Bastar is a registered instance of a technique practised by artisan communities across central and eastern India.
- Attributing a registration year to any metal craft; none is recorded here.
- Writing about Chola bronze iconography under a craft question; what belongs here is the contemporary Tamil Nadu statue centre.
- Calling the Aranmula Kannadi a glass mirror.
- Assuming metal crafts must sit near mines; they sit near fuel, labour and markets, because metal is tradeable and recoverable.

### Revision notes

1. **Bronze** = **copper + tin**, the earliest non-ferrous metals used; alloying lowers the melting point and improves flow, which is what makes casting possible.
2. **Dancing Girl, Mohenjo-daro, around 2500 BC** - the bronze anchor.
3. **Matsya Purana** describes **bronze casting methods**; **Rasaratnakara** discusses **metal purity and zinc distillation**.
4. **Bronze centres: Uttar Pradesh takes precedence** - **Etawah, Sitapur, Varanasi, Moradabad**; decorative flower pots and images of deities; ritual **tamrapatra, kanchantal, panchpatra**. **Tamil Nadu**: statues inspired by **Pallava, Chola, Pandya and Nayaka** art.
5. **Lost wax**: wax model -> encase -> melt out -> pour -> **break the mould**. Therefore **every piece is unique** and **failure is total**.
6. **Bastar Dhokra** (registered) - used for **brass ornaments**; a registered instance of a technique held by artisan communities across central and eastern India.
7. **Bidri** (registered) - **Bidar, Karnataka**; **blackened zinc-and-copper alloy** inlaid with **silver**; hookahs and decorative items. The core record prints the location as "Bidri village of Karnataka"; the variant is recorded, and **Bidar** is the town to name.
8. **Koftgiri** - **Jaipur and Alwar**; inlaying **light metals onto a dark surface**.
9. **Tarkashi** - **Rajasthan**; **fine copper or brass wires** embedded into **chiselled grooves**.
10. **Marori** - **Rajasthan**; **etchings** on base metal, gaps **filled with black lac**.
11. **Moradabad** - **Baarik Kaam**, delicate work, perfected using the **Nakkashi** or **engraving** style, for pots. **Badla** - a **zinc** pot of the **Marwar** region, Rajasthan.
12. **Repousse** = embossing for raised designs; **engraving** = cut-line patterns.
13. **Pembarthi metal craft** (registered) - **Pembarthi, Telangana**; **sheet metal (brass)** to adorn **chariots and temples**.
14. **Pahaldar lamps** - copper and brass lamps in different styles and shapes.
15. **Aranmula Kannadi** (registered) - **Kerala**; **handmade metal-alloy mirror**; **polished for several days**; **alloy composition held within the families involved**.
16. **Silver**: **filigree**; **Odisha**'s anklets **painri** and **paijam**; knitted silver ornaments called **gunchi**.
17. One question separates the whole inlay family: **what is the pattern physically made of?** Silver, light metal, wire, lac in a void, a cut line, or displaced metal.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how Indian metal crafts can be identified by operation rather than by region, and show what this reveals about their differing vulnerabilities. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Indian metal crafts fall into four operations.

**Casting** pours molten metal into a mould. In the lost-wax method a wax model is encased, melted out and replaced by metal, after which the mould is broken - so each piece is unique and a failed pour is unrecoverable. Bastar Dhokra, used for brass ornaments, is a registered instance; the bronze Dancing Girl of Mohenjo-daro, around 2500 BC, is the antiquity anchor, and bronze itself is copper alloyed with tin.

**Sheet working** raises and embosses. Pembarthi sheet-brass art of Telangana, made to adorn chariots and temples, is the registered case.

**Surface cutting and inlay** covers the family most often confused, and one question separates it: what is the pattern made of? Bidri's is silver in a blackened zinc-and-copper alloy at Bidar; Koftgiri's is light metal on a dark ground at Jaipur and Alwar; Tarkashi's is copper or brass wire in chiselled grooves; Marori's is black lac filling an etched void; Moradabad's Nakkashi adds nothing at all.

**Alloying for a property** yields the Aranmula metal mirror of Kerala, polished for several days, its composition held within the families involved.

The vulnerabilities differ accordingly. Cast work risks total loss per piece. Inlay work risks visual imitation. The Aranmula case risks loss of knowledge held in kin rather than in any record - a risk no origin-linked name addresses.

**Scoring note:** **15 marks = the four operations named and defined 4 + the inlay family separated by what the pattern is made of, with at least four members 5 + one correctly attributed registered case per operation 3 + a distinct vulnerability argued for each operation 3.** Listing metal crafts by state, however completely, cannot exceed five.

### Concept check

**Question:** Four objects are described: (i) a dark vessel whose surface carries a bright silver pattern; (ii) a dark blade whose pattern is a pale metal laid onto the surface; (iii) a vessel whose pattern is a black line sitting in a cut groove; (iv) a vessel whose pattern is a cut line with nothing added. Identify each, and state the single question that resolved all four.

**Model answer:** The single question is: **what is the pattern physically made of, and how is it held?** Applying it: (i) a **dark vessel with a bright silver pattern** is **Bidri** - a registered craft of **Bidar, Karnataka**, in which **silver** is inlaid into a **blackened zinc-and-copper alloy**, the blackening supplying the stark contrast that makes the silver read, with hookahs and decorative items among the products. (ii) A **dark surface carrying a pale metal laid onto it** is **Koftgiri**, recorded for **Jaipur and Alwar**, described as the art of **inlaying light metals onto a dark surface**. (iii) A pattern that is a **black line sitting in a cut groove** is **Marori** of **Rajasthan**, in which etchings are made on the base metal and the gaps are then **filled with black lac** - note that the pattern here is not metal at all, which is what distinguishes it from (i) and (ii), and note also that if the groove had been filled with **fine copper or brass wire** instead, the craft would be **Tarkashi**, also of Rajasthan. (iv) A **cut line with nothing added** is engraving - the **Nakkashi** style through which **Baarik Kaam**, or delicate work, is perfected at **Moradabad** to create pots. Region would not have resolved (iii) and the Tarkashi alternative, since both are Rajasthani; only the fill material does.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a dark background is itself diagnostic. Three of the four crafts above present a dark ground, and the ground tells you almost nothing. The diagnostic is always the **added material** - silver, light metal, wire, lac - or the absence of any added material at all.

Five blocks and six materials - wood, bamboo, cane, leather, paper pulp and plant fibre - with one logic running through all of them: **organic materials are directional, perishable and workable with hand tools**, and every craft here is a way of exploiting one of those three properties.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This is the lesson where the record is at its most list-like, and where a candidate is most tempted either to memorise everything or to skip it. Neither works. The way through is to notice that every entry answers one of three questions - *which timber?*, *which construction?*, or *which decoration?* - and that the entries which name a **material** are the ones examiners use, because a material is checkable.

### 2. Wood: joinery, lattice and the named timbers

**Kashmir** is recorded as **one of the largest centres for making wooden furniture**, where the art **has flourished since the 11th century**. Kashmiri artists use **walnut and deodar wood** for **houses, houseboats, and intricate woodwork**, including **acche-dar**, **khatamband** and **azli-pinjra** lattice designs. **Khatamband** appears separately as a named craft of Jammu and Kashmir, described as wooden pieces **joined** together - which is the definitional point: khatamband is **joinery without nails or glue**, a ceiling built from interlocking small pieces.

**Gujarat** is recorded for **wooden latticework on windows and wooden doors**. In **Himachal Pradesh**, **Bharmour and Chhatrahi** are known for **naghbel, kutheriphool, jali and dori** temple carvings.

The timber table is the part most worth holding, because a timber is a material fact:

| State | Timber | What the record says it is used for |
|---|---|---|
| **Karnataka** | **Sandalwood** | used in the making of carved objects; separately recorded as **Santalum** sandalwood carving |
| **Andhra Pradesh** | **Raktachandan**, typical **red sandalwood** | **carved dolls** and related work |
| **Kerala** | **Teakwood** | recorded with a distinctive deep-brown character |
| **Nagaland** | **Kumisyng wood** | a **local wood found in the eastern states** |
| **Rajasthan** | **Rohida wood** | **decorative** work |

And the named wood crafts: **Nirmal paintings, furniture, toys and crafts**, registered, of **Telangana**, whose evolution the record connects to a named **dynasty**, involving a **precise** process on the surface with a final **varnish**; **Kashtakari wood carving** of **Goa**, visible in **household products** and also in **churches and temples**; **Santalum sandalwood carving** of **Karnataka**; and **Khatamband** of **Jammu and Kashmir**.

Two observations that turn this list into argument. First, **the timber is the constraint and the constraint is regional** - sandalwood, red sandalwood, teak, kumisyng and rohida are five different woods with five different hardnesses, scents, grains and growth rates, and the crafts follow them. Second, **wood crafts straddle the architecture boundary**: lattice windows, houseboats and temple carving are architectural elements made by craft technique. The rule is the one from Lesson 12 - the **technique and the timber** belong here; the **building** does not.

### 3. Bamboo and cane: two plants that are constantly treated as one

This session's scope promises bamboo **and** cane, and the two are not interchangeable. The distinction is botanical, it is structural, and it decides what can be made.

**Bamboo is a woody grass.** The piece a craftsman works is a **culm** - a **hollow** tube divided at intervals by solid **nodes**. Three consequences follow from that geometry. A hollow tube is stiff for its weight, which is why whole culms serve as straight structural members. A tube cannot be bent sharply when dry, because the wall buckles and the node splits, which is why bamboo work avoids tight curves. And a culm **splits cleanly along its length**, which is the single most exploited property of the material: split it into thin slivers and you have a weaving stock for mats, screens, baskets and containers.

**Cane - rattan - is the stem of a climbing palm.** It is **solid**, not hollow, and it grows as a long, slender, uniformly flexible stem. Its consequences are the mirror image. A solid stem, softened by soaking or steaming, can be **bent into a curve and will hold that curve** when it dries, which is what makes cane the material of curved furniture frames. Its hard outer layer can be **peeled into narrow strips** used for binding joints and for weaving seats and panels. Its softer core can be used as a round rod.

**The identification rule this yields:** *straight members, split slivers, mats and baskets point to bamboo; curved frames, bound joints and woven seat panels point to cane.* A single object frequently uses both - a bent cane frame with a bamboo-sliver panel - and naming which part is which is exactly the kind of process statement this session has asked for throughout.

**What this session owns, and what it does not.** The **bamboo** side has a named owned example: the **Moshti** grain storage container of southern Gujarat, made from bamboo and used by the **Gamit, Vasava and Dholiya Patel** communities, which appears in Lesson 12 as tribal storage technology. **For cane, the owners used in this session name no craft at all.** That gap is declared rather than filled: no cane centre, community, product or registration is supplied here, and none is inferred from the bamboo record or from any outside list. What is taught instead is the **classification and process boundary** above, which is what an examiner can test without a named example - because an item describing "a solid, flexible stem bent into a frame after soaking" has already identified the material class whether or not the candidate can name a centre.

### 4. Toys: the oldest continuously attested object class

The toy record is unusual because it runs from archaeology to registered goods without a gap in the middle, which makes it the best single illustration of the four age-classes from Lesson 3 operating within one object type.

**Class A, archaeological.** The **first evidence for toy carts** was found in the **Harappan civilisation** and **continued until the early historic period**. **Copper, bronze and terracotta toy carts** have been found at Harappan sites including **Alamgirpur, Ambkheri, Kalibangan and Lothal**; similar cart models have been found at early historic sites including **Atranjikhera, Nagarjunakonda, Sambhar, Sonapur, Ujjain and Brahmpuri**.

**Classes B and C, historical and living materials.** In ancient and medieval times, toys were made from **clay, paper, papier-mache and lacquered wood**, including **Tirupati dolls from redwood in Andhra Pradesh**. The living regional record: **Rajasthan** for colourful **cloth dolls**; **Assam** for **pith dolls**; **southern India** for wooden toys; **Mysore and Channapatna, Karnataka**, for **lacquered wooden toys**; and **Kondapalli, Andhra Pradesh**, for the **Ambari Hathi** made from **soft ponki wood**.

**Class D, registered goods.** **Channapatna Toys**, registered, of **Karnataka**, **traditionally made from ivory wood**, with Channapatna town also known as a toy town. **Kondapalli Bommalu Toys**, registered, of **Andhra Pradesh**, made using a **soft wood** from which **moisture is removed**, with the **parts of the toy carved separately and then joined**.

A caution that must be stated: **"ivory wood" is a timber, not elephant ivory.** The phrase is a recorded material description of the Channapatna tradition and should never be read as an animal product; misreading it produces a factual error and a needless controversy in an answer.

The Kondapalli description also repays attention, because it names three distinct process steps - **soft wood**, **moisture removal**, **separate carving and joining** - which is more process detail than most entries in the whole record carry. Any question asking for a described craft process can be answered from it.

### 5. Leather: tanning, region and the footwear record

The antiquity fact belongs to Lesson 3; the craft geography belongs here.

**Rajasthan** holds the **largest market for leather**, where **camel leather** is used to make bags of various shapes and sizes. **Jaipur and Jodhpur** are famous for **Mojaris**. **Kanpur** is famous for **leather and tanned products**. **Maharashtra** is famous for **Kolhapuri chappals**. **Chennai and Kolkata** are major centres for leather bag and footwear production. **Punjabi juttis** are well known and **use the applique technique** - which is a direct link back to Lesson 8, since applique is the same operation performed on a different material. At **Bikaner**, **Manoti art** involves **decorating articles with camel skin**; it is **also called Usta art**, derived from the Persian word **Ustad**, meaning **Master**.

The regional footwear table:

| Footwear | Where | Recorded feature |
|---|---|---|
| **Konglan stitched boots** | **West Bengal** | made in the northern part of the state; **thick leather soles**, with uppers of either leather or thick cloth |
| **Paabu stitched boots** | **Ladakh** | traditional **colourful knee-high** boots of the region |
| **Kolhapuri chappal** (registered) | **Karnataka and Maharashtra** | handcrafted **pure leather**, associated with the Kolhapur region of Maharashtra and with Karnataka |
| **Katki chappal** | **Odisha** | **ethnic leather footwear** |

The **Kolhapuri chappal entry is the single best anti-one-state-exclusivity citation in the entire session**, because the source names **two states** for one registered craft in the same row. Any answer arguing that craft boundaries do not follow administrative boundaries should cite it.

### 6. Paper, pulp, mask and fibre

**Kashmir papier mache**, of **Jammu and Kashmir**, uses **paper pulp combined with copper sulphate and rice flour**, worked into the desired shape. The recipe is the identification: pulp plus a binder plus an additive. **Tirukanur papier mache**, registered, of **Puducherry**, is a craft in which **paper sculptures are manufactured**.

**Gambhira masks**, of **West Bengal**, take as their theme the **Goddess Kali**. **Chhau masks**, registered, of **West Bengal**, are **mainly used to represent various mythological characters**. Two cautions attach to Chhau. First, the **mask** is owned here as a made object; the **dance** is owned by the performance topic - and the record for the dance describes Chhau as a mask dance using vigorous martial movements to narrate mythological stories, with **three main styles: Saraikela Chhau in Jharkhand, Mayurbhanj Chhau in Odisha and Purulia Chhau in West Bengal**, of which **Mayurbhanj Chhau artists do not wear masks**. Second, the mask entry is registered to **West Bengal** while the dance spans **three states** - and one of the three does not use masks at all. Reproduce both facts; do not collapse them.

**Gambhira** likewise has a performance counterpart recorded for the **Malda district of West Bengal** during the festival of **Chaitra Sankranti**, with the **masks made out of neem and fig trees** and the songs originating in Hindu mythology, especially **shakti and shaiva worship**. The **material fact - neem and fig wood** - is craft content and belongs here; the performance belongs to its owner.

**Ganjifa cards** and **Rambaans natural fibre craft** appear in the record among other famous Indian handicrafts. This session names them and does not elaborate beyond that, because the held record does not.

### 7. Comparative treatment: what organic materials share, and where they differ

| | **Wood** | **Leather** | **Paper pulp** | **Bamboo** | **Cane (rattan)** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Origin** | a grown, grained solid | an animal hide, chemically transformed by tanning | a manufactured, re-formable mass | the culm of a woody grass | the stem of a climbing palm |
| **Cross-section** | solid, grained | sheet | none; formless until shaped | **hollow**, with solid nodes | **solid** throughout |
| **Directional?** | strongly - grain governs every cut | slightly - hide has a stretch direction | no - pulp is isotropic | strongly - splits cleanly along the culm | strongly - flexible along its length |
| **Reworkable?** | no; subtractive | no | **yes**, before drying | no; but it splits into weaving slivers | no; but it bends when soaked and holds the curve |
| **Main operations recorded or taught** | carving, joinery, lattice, turning, lacquering | tanning, cutting, stitching, applique, surface decoration | pulping, shaping, painting | cross-cutting, splitting, sliver weaving, lashing | soaking or steaming, bending into frames, peeling into binding strips, seat weaving |
| **Named Indian cases here** | Kashmir joinery; Nirmal; Kashtakari; sandalwood | Mojari, Kolhapuri, jutti, Manoti / Usta, Paabu, Konglan, Katki | Kashmir and Tirukanur papier mache; masks | the **Moshti** grain container of southern Gujarat | **none - example gap declared** |
| **Characteristic vulnerability** | timber supply and species regulation | hide supply, tanning regulation and effluent control | low barriers, hence easy imitation | material access and land use | material access and land use |

The row worth arguing is **reworkable**. Paper pulp is the only organic material here that can be re-formed before it sets, which is why papier mache can produce complex hollow shapes cheaply and why its entry barriers are low. Low entry barriers cut both ways: the craft is accessible to new makers, and it is also the easiest to imitate. This is the same trade-off that appeared with felt in Lesson 9 and it is a general property of quick, low-capital crafts rather than a fact about any one of them.

### Examination use

Three high-value blocks, and one boundary. The **timber table** is checkable material knowledge - **sandalwood in Karnataka, red sandalwood in Andhra Pradesh, teak in Kerala, kumisyng in Nagaland, rohida in Rajasthan** - and it is exactly the kind of pairing an identification item uses. The **toy sequence** runs cleanly from Harappan cart models at four named sites, through early historic models at six more, to two registered traditions with their timbers and processes, and it is the best single demonstration of the four age-classes inside one object type. The **Kolhapuri chappal's two-state record** is the citation to use whenever an answer needs to refuse one-state exclusivity. The boundary is the **bamboo-against-cane distinction**: hollow noded culm against solid palm stem, splitting against bending, which answers a process-description item even where no centre can be named. For process-description questions, the **Kondapalli** entry - soft wood, moisture removed, parts carved separately and joined - and the **Kashmir papier mache** recipe are the two most detailed processes available here.

### Traps introduced here

- Reading Channapatna's "ivory wood" as elephant ivory; it is a timber.
- Assigning the Kolhapuri chappal to one state when the record names two.
- Treating the Chhau mask and the Chhau dance as one owned item; the mask is registered to West Bengal, the dance spans three states, and Mayurbhanj performers do not wear masks.
- Writing about houseboats, lattice windows or temple carvings as architecture; here they are timber and technique.
- Forgetting that the Punjabi jutti uses **applique**, the same operation as Pipli work in cloth.
- Elaborating on Ganjifa or Rambaans beyond what the record carries.
- Using "bamboo" and "cane" as synonyms; one is a hollow grass culm, the other a solid palm stem, and they support different constructions.
- Supplying a named cane or rattan craft, centre or community; this session declares that gap and fills it with the material boundary instead.

### Revision notes

1. **Kashmir**: largest wooden-furniture centre; flourishing **since the 11th century**; **walnut and deodar**; houses, **houseboats**; **acche-dar, khatamband, azli-pinjra** lattice designs. **Khatamband** = wooden pieces **joined** - interlocking joinery.
2. **Gujarat**: wooden **latticework** on windows and doors. **Himachal Pradesh**: **Bharmour and Chhatrahi** - **naghbel, kutheriphool, jali, dori** temple carvings.
3. **Timbers**: **Karnataka - sandalwood**; **Andhra Pradesh - raktachandan (red sandalwood), carved dolls**; **Kerala - teakwood**; **Nagaland - kumisyng**; **Rajasthan - rohida**.
4. **Named wood crafts**: **Nirmal** (registered, Telangana, dynastic evolution, varnished finish); **Kashtakari** (Goa, household products and churches and temples); **Santalum** sandalwood carving (Karnataka); **Khatamband** (Jammu and Kashmir).
5. **Toy archaeology**: Harappan **toy carts** in copper, bronze and terracotta at **Alamgirpur, Ambkheri, Kalibangan, Lothal**; early historic models at **Atranjikhera, Nagarjunakonda, Sambhar, Sonapur, Ujjain, Brahmpuri**.
6. **Toy materials historically**: clay, paper, **papier-mache**, **lacquered wood**; **Tirupati dolls** from **redwood**, Andhra Pradesh.
7. **Living toy regions**: **Rajasthan** cloth dolls; **Assam** pith dolls; **Mysore and Channapatna** lacquered wooden toys; **Kondapalli** **Ambari Hathi** from **soft ponki wood**.
8. **Channapatna Toys** (registered, Karnataka) - **traditionally ivory wood**, a **timber**; the town is known as a toy town. **Kondapalli Bommalu** (registered, Andhra Pradesh) - soft wood, **moisture removed**, **parts carved separately then joined**.
9. **Leather geography**: **Rajasthan** - largest market, **camel leather** bags; **Jaipur and Jodhpur** - **Mojaris**; **Kanpur** - leather and tanned products; **Maharashtra** - **Kolhapuri chappals**; **Chennai and Kolkata** - bags and footwear; **Punjabi juttis** use **applique**; **Bikaner** - **Manoti**, also **Usta** art from Persian **Ustad**, "Master", decorating with **camel skin**.
10. **Footwear table**: **Konglan** boots, **West Bengal**, thick leather soles; **Paabu** boots, **Ladakh**, colourful knee-high; **Kolhapuri chappal** (registered), **Karnataka AND Maharashtra**; **Katki chappal**, **Odisha**.
11. **Kashmir papier mache** - **paper pulp with copper sulphate and rice flour**. **Tirukanur papier mache** (registered) - **Puducherry**, paper sculptures.
12. **Gambhira masks** - **West Bengal**, theme **Goddess Kali**; the associated performance is at **Malda** during **Chaitra Sankranti**, masks made of **neem and fig** wood. **Chhau mask** (registered) - **West Bengal**, representing **mythological characters**; the dance has **Saraikela (Jharkhand), Mayurbhanj (Odisha) and Purulia (West Bengal)** styles, and **Mayurbhanj performers do not wear masks**.
13. **Ganjifa cards** and **Rambaans natural fibre craft** are recorded among other famous Indian handicrafts; nothing further is supplied here.
14. **Bamboo** is the **hollow, noded culm of a woody grass**: cross-cut, **split lengthwise into slivers**, woven and lashed. Owned example: the **Moshti** grain container of southern Gujarat.
15. **Cane (rattan)** is the **solid stem of a climbing palm**: soaked or steamed, **bent into curved frames**, **peeled into binding strips** and woven into seat panels. **Example gap declared** - the owners name no cane or rattan craft, and none is invented.
16. Identification rule: **straight members, split slivers, mats and baskets point to bamboo; curved frames, bound joints and woven seat panels point to cane.**
17. **Paper pulp is the only reworkable organic material here**, which lowers entry barriers and, for the same reason, raises exposure to imitation.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In the organic-material crafts, the material's structure dictates the operation." Illustrate with reference to Indian wood, leather, paper, bamboo and cane crafts. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

**Wood** is grained and directional, so operations are subtractive: carving, joinery, lattice. Kashmir's khatamband joins small pieces into interlocking ceilings in walnut and deodar. The craft follows the timber - sandalwood in Karnataka, red sandalwood in Andhra Pradesh, teak in Kerala, kumisyng in Nagaland, rohida in Rajasthan.

**Leather**, a tanned hide, is flexible and stitchable, so it is cut, stitched and decorated: Mojaris at Jaipur and Jodhpur, Kolhapuri chappals across Karnataka and Maharashtra, Punjabi juttis in applique.

**Paper pulp** is re-formable before drying, so it is moulded into hollow shapes - Kashmir papier mache, Tirukanur paper sculptures.

**Bamboo** is a hollow, noded culm, so it is cross-cut, split into slivers and woven: the Moshti grain container of southern Gujarat.

**Cane** is a solid palm stem, so it is soaked and bent into frames and peeled for binding.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = one physical structural property for each of the five materials 5 + the operation that property permits 3 + correctly attributed Indian cases where this session holds them 2.** The cane paragraph earns its marks from the structural claim alone, because no named cane craft is available; a fabricated cane example forfeits the final two marks.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Channapatna toys are made from ivory, and the Chhau mask is a West Bengal craft used in the Chhau dance of West Bengal." Correct both halves, and explain why each error is more than a slip.

**Model answer:** The first half misreads a **timber** as an **animal product**. Channapatna toys, a registered Karnataka craft from a town also known as a toy town, are recorded as **traditionally made from ivory wood** - a wood. The error matters beyond the fact because the session's ivory record is specifically a regulated-material case: ivory carving is treated as historical craft study under contemporary wildlife-protection constraint, so describing a living, registered toy tradition as an ivory craft imports a legal problem that does not exist and would undermine any policy argument built on it. The second half collapses a **mask** into a **dance** and a **dance** into a **state**. The Chhau mask is registered to **West Bengal** and is recorded as being mainly used to represent various mythological characters - and as a made object it belongs to the craft topic. The Chhau dance belongs to the performance owner, and the record gives it **three main styles - Saraikela in Jharkhand, Mayurbhanj in Odisha and Purulia in West Bengal** - of which **Mayurbhanj artists do not wear masks at all**. So the mask's registration and the dance's geography are different facts with different extents, and one of the dance's three styles falls outside the mask craft entirely. A corrected version: Channapatna toys of Karnataka are traditionally made from ivory wood; the Chhau mask is a registered West Bengal craft representing mythological characters, while the Chhau dance is performed in three regional styles across Jharkhand, Odisha and West Bengal, one of which is maskless.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a craft object and the practice it serves share a geography. Objects are registered, made and traded where their makers are; practices are performed where their communities are; and the two extents frequently differ. Always state which of the two the question is about before attaching a state to it.

## Lesson 15 - Folk and tribal traditions that are not objects: festival, ballad and martial forms

Progress: 15/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the three non-object folk traditions routed into this topic by the verified record, the agricultural and community context of a festival, the ballad as an event record, the martial form, and the discipline that keeps community description honest

Book context: Queried - the Chapchar Kut festival entry and the neighbouring Mizo festival record, the folk-music table entry for Khongjom Parba, the Thang-Ta entry in the folk-dance record, the Karma Naach entry, and the Chhau and Gambhira performance records that bound this lesson
