CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three traditions, three different kinds of thing

```text
  CHAPCHAR KUT                KHONGJOM PARBA            THANG-TA
  ------------                --------------            --------
  KIND: a FESTIVAL            KIND: a BALLAD            KIND: a MARTIAL
                                    (folk music)              PERFORMANCE
  ------------                --------------            --------
  WHERE: MIZORAM              WHERE: MANIPUR            WHERE: MANIPUR
  ------------                --------------            --------
  WHEN / WHAT:                THEME:                    WHAT:
  celebrated in MARCH,        the BATTLE OF             THANG = SWORD
  AFTER completion of         KHONGJOM (1891)           TA = SPEAR
  the locals' most                                      a unique display of
  arduous task of JHUM                                  skill, creativity and
  (shifting cultivation)                                agility; performers
  operations                                            enact a MOCK FIGHT
                                                        sequence - leaping to
                                                        attack and defend
  ------------                --------------            --------
  WHY IT IS HERE:             WHY IT IS HERE:           WHY IT IS HERE:
  the verified examination record routes all three of these to this topic,
  even though a festival, a ballad and a martial form are each, in other
  contexts, the property of another owner.

  ALSO RECORDED IN MIZORAM:
  ZO KUTPUI - a Mizoram festival intended to UNIFY MIZO TRIBES living in
  different parts of the world.

  THE STRUCTURAL POINT: each of the three is anchored to something
  outside itself - an AGRICULTURAL CYCLE, a DATED BATTLE, and a
  WEAPON PAIR. None of them is anchored to a symbol somebody invented.
```

Three traditions of three different kinds, in two states, each with a hard anchor. The anchors are the examinable content.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

A craft session that suddenly contains a festival, a ballad and a martial form looks incoherent. It is not: the verified examination record places these three squarely within this topic's ownership, and a candidate who has revised only objects will be unable to answer the item they appear in. The deeper reason they belong is that **folk and tribal tradition is not confined to things**. A community's material culture, its calendar, its memory and its bodily disciplines are made by the same people and are transmitted the same way - which is precisely what "living tradition" means.

### 2. Chapchar Kut: a festival anchored to an agricultural operation

The record is compact: **Chapchar Kut is celebrated in Mizoram during March after completion of the locals' most arduous task of jhum operation.**

Every clause is load-bearing.

- **Mizoram** fixes the place.
- **March** fixes the season.
- **After completion** fixes the position in a cycle - this is a festival of **completion**, not of beginning, sowing or harvest.
- **Jhum operation** fixes what has been completed: jhum is shifting cultivation, in which a plot is cleared and burnt before planting, and the clearing is the arduous phase.

So Chapchar Kut is a festival whose date is set by **agricultural labour**, not by an astronomical or scriptural calendar. That is the single most useful thing to say about it, and it converts a matching fact into an analytical one: in a community practising shifting cultivation, the hardest collective labour of the year ends at a definite point, and the festival marks that point.

The record adds a second Mizoram festival for contrast: **Zo Kutpui**, celebrated in Mizoram, which **intends to unify Mizo tribes living in different parts of the world**. Note the difference in kind. Chapchar Kut is anchored to a **labour cycle**; Zo Kutpui is anchored to a **social purpose** that is explicitly transnational. Two festivals, one state, two completely different organising logics - and a useful demonstration that "tribal festival" is not a single category.

### 3. Khongjom Parba: a ballad anchored to a dated event

The record places **Khongjom Parba** in **Manipur**, with its major theme given as the **Battle of Khongjom (1891)**.

The form is a **ballad** - a narrative sung in folk performance - and its subject is a **specific, dated military event**. That combination makes it a different kind of cultural object from a festival: it is a **memory technology**. A community that sings a ballad about a battle is maintaining a record of that battle, in a form that can be transmitted without writing and performed publicly.

Two disciplines apply. First, **the date belongs to the battle, not to the ballad.** 1891 is when the battle occurred; the record does not say when the ballad was composed, and this session does not supply a composition date. That is precisely the Lesson 3 clock discipline applied to a non-object tradition. Second, **the political history of the battle belongs to the modern-history owner.** What belongs here is that a Manipuri folk-music form takes that battle as its theme - a statement about the tradition, not about the war.

### 4. Thang-Ta: a martial form anchored to a weapon pair

The record is unusually precise, and the precision is linguistic: **Thang-Ta (Manipur). Thang means sword and Ta means spear. The dance performance is a unique display of skill, creativity and agility in which the performers enact a mock fight sequence - leaping to attack and defend.**

Four elements: the **state**, the **etymology**, the **quality being displayed**, and the **structure of the performance**. The etymology is the strongest identification fact in the entry, because it is checkable and because it names the two implements that define the form. The record separately notes that **Thang-ta and Sankirtana are also influenced by Manipuri dance**, which places the form inside a wider Manipuri performance ecology.

The boundary is worth stating exactly: the **martial art and its performance grammar belong to the performance owner**. What belongs here is the identification set - Manipur, sword and spear, mock combat - because that is what the verified examination record requires this topic to support, and because the weapons themselves are made objects.

Two neighbouring records prevent confusion:

- **Rangma (Nagaland)** is recorded as **the war dance**, in which dancers in **colourful costumes, jewellery and colourful headgears** enact **mock war formations and traditions**.
- **Paika (Odisha)** is recorded as a **martial folk dance of Jharkhand and southern Odisha**, performed with **wooden spears and shields**, showcasing **battle skills and agility in infantry-like formations**; the word **Paika means battle**.

Three martial folk forms, three states or state-groups, three different implement sets. An item that describes "a martial folk performance with mock combat" has not yet identified anything; the **implements and the state** do the work.

### 5. Karma Naach and the wider tribal performance record

The record notes **Karma Naach**, performed during the **tribal festival of 'Karma'** by **many tribes of Eastern India, especially in the Chota Nagpur Plateau**, in which **dancers form a circle and dance with their arms around each other's waist**.

The entry is included here for one reason: it is the record's clearest case of a tradition that is **explicitly multi-community and multi-state**. "Many tribes of Eastern India, especially in the Chota Nagpur Plateau" is the opposite of a one-state attribution, and an answer that attaches Karma Naach to a single state has contradicted the source. It is the non-object counterpart of the Kolhapuri chappal's two-state entry.

### 6. The disciplines for writing about a living community

This lesson is where the risk of fabrication is highest, because festivals, ballads and martial forms invite interpretation. Five rules, all enforced in this session.

1. **Name the anchor, not the meaning.** Chapchar Kut's anchor is the completion of jhum operations; Khongjom Parba's is the Battle of Khongjom of 1891; Thang-Ta's is the sword-and-spear pair. Write the anchor.
2. **Do not date a tradition from its subject.** 1891 dates the battle. It does not date the ballad.
3. **Do not homogenise.** Mizoram holds a labour-cycle festival and a diaspora-unity festival; Nagaland's textile record insists on tribe-specific motifs; Karma Naach is explicitly many-tribe. "Tribal culture" is not a category with contents.
4. **Do not use the past tense** for a tradition the record describes in the present.
5. **Do not assert decline** without the failing-link diagnostic of Lesson 2. A festival that requires a collective agricultural operation has a dependency - the operation - and *that* is a statable, checkable observation, as against a generalised lament.

The fifth rule deserves expansion, because it is the point at which this lesson connects to the rest of the session. Chapchar Kut is timed by the completion of jhum. Jhum is a land-use practice subject to law, ecology and economic change. So a festival whose timing depends on a cultivation practice is linked, through that practice, to everything that affects the practice. That is a genuine analytical observation about a living tradition - and it is available without inventing a single symbol, feeling or prophecy. **This session states the dependency and stops there**, because the land-use, forest-law and livelihood questions that follow belong to other owners.

### Examination use

All three traditions are examinable primarily as **identification**, and the safe form is state-plus-kind-plus-anchor: **Chapchar Kut - Mizoram - festival - celebrated in March after the completion of jhum operations**; **Khongjom Parba - Manipur - ballad - theme the Battle of Khongjom, 1891**; **Thang-Ta - Manipur - martial performance - thang means sword, ta means spear, mock fight sequence**. The **Zo Kutpui** contrast and the **Karma Naach** multi-community record are the two facts that let an answer refuse homogenisation. For a Mains demand on living traditions, the **anchor discipline** in section 6 is the structure: name the anchor, keep the clocks apart, refuse homogenisation, use the present tense, and diagnose before lamenting.

### Traps introduced here

- Attaching Thang-Ta, or any Manipuri form, to a neighbouring state.
- Dating the Khongjom Parba ballad to 1891; that is the battle's date.
- Treating Chapchar Kut as a harvest festival; the record ties it to the completion of jhum operations in March.
- Assigning Karma Naach to one state when the record says many tribes of Eastern India, especially the Chota Nagpur Plateau.
- Confusing Thang-Ta, Rangma and Paika; the implements and the state separate them.
- Supplying symbolic or spiritual meanings for any of these traditions.

### Revision notes

1. **Chapchar Kut** - **Mizoram**; celebrated in **March**, **after completion of the locals' most arduous task of jhum operation**. A **completion** festival tied to an **agricultural labour cycle**.
2. **Zo Kutpui** - **Mizoram**; intends to **unify Mizo tribes living in different parts of the world**. A social-purpose festival, explicitly transnational.
3. **Khongjom Parba** - **Manipur**; a **ballad** in the folk-music record; theme the **Battle of Khongjom (1891)**.
4. **1891 dates the battle, not the ballad.**
5. **Thang-Ta** - **Manipur**; **thang = sword, ta = spear**; a display of **skill, creativity and agility**; performers enact a **mock fight sequence**, leaping to attack and defend; recorded as influenced by Manipuri dance alongside **Sankirtana**.
6. **Rangma** - **Nagaland**; the **war dance**; colourful costumes, jewellery and headgears; **mock war formations**.
7. **Paika** - **Jharkhand and southern Odisha**; martial folk dance with **wooden spears and shields**; **Paika means battle**.
8. **Karma Naach** - performed during the tribal festival of **Karma** by **many tribes of Eastern India, especially the Chota Nagpur Plateau**; dancers form a **circle** with arms around each other's waists.
9. Each of the three owned traditions has a **hard anchor**: an agricultural operation, a dated battle, a weapon pair.
10. Five disciplines: name the anchor not the meaning; do not date a tradition from its subject; do not homogenise; use the present tense; diagnose before asserting decline.
11. The **dependency observation**: a festival timed by a cultivation practice is linked, through that practice, to whatever affects it. State the dependency; route the land-use and forest questions to their owners.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"A living folk tradition should be described by its anchors, not by its supposed meanings." Discuss with reference to named Indian folk traditions. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

An anchor is a checkable external fact to which a tradition is tied; a meaning is usually supplied by the writer.

**Chapchar Kut** of Mizoram is anchored to an **agricultural operation**: it is celebrated in March, after completion of the locals' most arduous task of jhum operation - a festival of completion within a labour cycle.

**Khongjom Parba** of Manipur is anchored to a **dated event**: the Battle of Khongjom of 1891 is its theme. The date belongs to the battle; the ballad's own date is not recorded and must not be supplied.

**Thang-Ta** of Manipur is anchored to **implements and etymology**: thang means sword, ta means spear, and performers enact a mock fight sequence.

Describing these by invented symbolism would replace three verifiable statements with three unverifiable ones, and would conceal the genuine analytical point - that a festival timed by cultivation depends on that cultivation.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = the anchor concept defined as a checkable external fact 2 + the three traditions with their exact anchors 5 + the clock discipline separating the battle's date from the ballad's 2 + the dependency observation drawn from one anchor 1.** Any invented symbolic meaning cancels the corresponding anchor's marks.

### Concept check

**Question:** Three descriptions are given: (i) a Mizoram festival held in March; (ii) a Manipuri folk-music form about an 1891 battle; (iii) a Manipuri performance in which performers enact mock combat. For each, state what additional fact the record supplies that converts the description into a secure identification, and explain why a description alone is insufficient.

**Model answer:** For (i), the description is insufficient because Mizoram holds more than one recorded festival - **Zo Kutpui** is also a Mizoram festival, one intended to **unify Mizo tribes living in different parts of the world**. The converting fact is the **anchor in the labour cycle**: Chapchar Kut is celebrated in March **after completion of the locals' most arduous task of jhum operation**. Timing within a cultivation cycle, not merely a month, is what fixes it. For (ii), the description is nearly sufficient but carries a dating risk. The converting fact is that **Khongjom Parba** is recorded in the **folk-music** register of Manipur with the **Battle of Khongjom (1891)** as its **theme** - so 1891 attaches to the battle, and no composition date for the ballad is available. A candidate who reads "an 1891 folk form" has manufactured a date. For (iii), the description could equally fit **Rangma** of Nagaland, recorded as the war dance with mock war formations, or **Paika** of Jharkhand and southern Odisha, performed with wooden spears and shields, where the word itself means battle. The converting facts for Thang-Ta are the **state** and the **etymology**: Manipur, **thang** meaning sword and **ta** meaning spear, with performers leaping to attack and defend in a mock fight sequence. In all three cases the general description names a *category* - festival, ballad, martial performance - and the record's specific anchor names the *instance*.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a region plus a genre identifies a tradition. North-eastern India holds several festivals, several ballads and several martial forms, and the examiner's standard construction is to give a true genre and a true region and attach the wrong name. Only the anchor - the operation, the event, the implement - is safe.

## Lesson 16 - Floor designs, household ritual craft, gender and the stages nobody counts

Progress: 16/19 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the floor-design record and its erasable materials, the household ritual craft as a daily practice, the gendered structure of craft labour, apprenticeship and transmission, and the structural argument about invisible stages

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the floor-designs section in full, the Mizoram weaving record naming women as primarily responsible, the Manipuri phanek-border embroidery record, the Aranmula family-held alloy record, the ginning-spinning-weaving sequence, and the Banaras production-time statement
CA search: "Not applicable - structural and social lesson; the session's single current record is held for Lesson 18"
CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the craft chain re-read for who does what, and who is counted

```text
  STAGE                     TYPICALLY PERFORMED BY      IS IT COUNTED?
  -----                     ----------------------      --------------
  raw material gathering    household, often women      rarely
  ginning                   household                   no
  spinning                  household, often women      no - yet without
                                                        it there is no warp
  dyeing / dye preparation  specialist or household     sometimes
  WEAVING                   named weaver                YES - this is the
                                                        stage that is named,
                                                        photographed,
                                                        registered
  finishing, washing,       household                   no
    trimming, folding
  marketing                 intermediary or trader      YES - and this is
                                                        where the margin is
  -----                     ----------------------      --------------

  WHAT THE RECORD ITSELF STATES ABOUT WOMEN AND CRAFT:
   MIZORAM ...... "women are PRIMARILY RESPONSIBLE for weaving" the Puan
   MANIPUR ...... muga-thread embroidery on the borders of PHANEKS worn
                  by WOMEN during auspicious occasions such as weddings
   MEGHALAYA .... Garo, Khasi and Jaintia tribes are skilled weavers
   KERALA ....... the Aranmula mirror's ALLOY COMPOSITION is held within
                  the FAMILIES involved - knowledge kept in kin, not in text
   FLOOR DESIGN . drawn during religious or auspicious FAMILY occasions,
                  freehand, in erasable material

  THE ARGUMENT, IN ONE LINE:
  Recognition, naming and legal protection attach to the VISIBLE stage.
  Whoever performs an INVISIBLE stage has no claim on the price,
  and no instrument in this topic reaches them.
```

The diagram is the lesson. Everything below either supports it or bounds it.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Craft answers that mention gender usually do so as a decorative sentence - "women play an important role in Indian handicrafts" - which is both unfalsifiable and unmarkable. The alternative is not more assertion but **structure**: identify the stages of production, identify who the record says performs them, and identify which stages are named and paid. That is a claim with parts, and it can be argued.

The same is true of transmission. "Traditional skills are being lost" is a lament. "The technique is held within families rather than in any written form, so an interrupted generation is an irreversible loss" is a claim about **how knowledge is stored**, and the record supplies an example of it.

### 2. Floor designs: a craft defined by being erased

The source's twelfth material class is **floor designs**, and its description is short enough to hold entirely: the floor designs are **often drawn during religious or auspicious family occasions**; the designs are **drawn freehand from the centre in concentric geometric patterns**, using **natural, erasable materials like chalk, lime, powdered marble, or rice-lime mixtures**.

Four features, each of which distinguishes this from every other craft in the session:

1. **The occasion, not the market, triggers production.** It is made for a religious or auspicious family event. There is no buyer.
2. **The composition method is specified**: freehand, from the centre, in concentric geometric patterns. That is a process description, and it is the only one available for this class.
3. **The materials are deliberately erasable**: chalk, lime, powdered marble, rice-lime mixtures. Impermanence is designed in.
4. **It is domestic.** The site is the household floor.

Now notice what those four features do to the instruments of Lesson 11 and Lesson 17. A craft with **no product to sell**, **no durable object**, **no workshop** and **no market** is a craft that a documentation exercise can record, that a certification cannot test, and that a Geographical Indication - which protects the name of a **good** - cannot readily reach at all. Floor design is therefore the limiting case of this entire topic: unambiguously a craft by the source's own classification, and almost entirely outside the reach of the instruments the topic discusses.

That is not a defect in the instruments. It is a demonstration that **the instruments are designed for traded goods**, and that a large part of craft practice - daily, domestic, occasion-driven, unpaid - lies outside the traded economy. An answer that makes this point with the floor-design case is making an argument that very few candidates will make.

### 3. What the record actually says about women and craft

This session refuses to generalise about gender, and instead uses exactly what the record supplies.

- **Mizoram:** **women are primarily responsible for weaving** elegant Puan patterns. This is the most explicit gendered-production statement in the entire craft record, and it is stated about the **named**, visible stage - weaving itself.
- **Manipur:** muga silk thread embroidery in dark colours is carried out on the **borders of phaneks**, worn by **women** during auspicious occasions such as **weddings**. Here gender attaches to **use** rather than to production.
- **Meghalaya:** the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** tribes are **skilled weavers**. The record names communities, not genders.
- **Floor designs:** made for **family** occasions in the household.

Four records, three different relationships between gender and craft - production, use, and unnamed - and that variety is itself the finding. The honest statement is: **the record explicitly identifies women as the primary weavers in one named case, identifies a garment as women's wear in another, and is silent elsewhere.** That is more defensible, and more useful, than a general claim, because it can be cited.

### 4. The structural argument about invisible stages

Here is where analysis is permitted, provided it is marked as reasoning rather than as record.

The textile sequence is **ginning, spinning and weaving**. Three stages. Only the third produces a named practitioner, a named product and, where registration exists, a named protected good. The first two are preparatory, are frequently domestic, and have no separate product to sell.

From that structure, three consequences follow as reasoning:

1. **Whoever performs an unnamed stage cannot price it.** A stage with no separate product has no separate market, and therefore no independent price. Its value is absorbed into the next stage's cost.
2. **Recognition instruments attach to the visible stage.** A registered good is a finished article. A documentation entry describes a finished textile. A certification tests a finished product. The preparatory stages appear, if at all, inside someone else's specification.
3. **Therefore, distributional questions inside the household are untouched by every instrument in this topic.** This is not a criticism of those instruments; it is a statement of their scope.

The khadi record of Lesson 4 is the historical counter-example, and it is worth restating here because it shows the argument is not a law of nature. **Khadi is defined as hand-spun cotton fabric**: the definition is built around the **spinning** stage. The **All India Spinners' Association**, established in **1925** after the Non-Cooperation Movement, was organised around **spinning**. The name of the resulting institution - a *Spinners'* Association - makes the invisible stage the named one. So it is possible to define a product by its preparatory stage, and doing so changes who is visible in it. That is a genuine, source-grounded illustration of the argument's practical stake.

### 5. Transmission: how craft knowledge is actually stored

The record gives one unambiguous statement about the storage of craft knowledge, and it is worth treating as the centre of this section: the **Aranmula Kannadi**'s **alloy composition is held within the families involved**.

Consider what that means. The technical core of the craft - the proportions that make a metal surface reflect - exists in no manual, no patent and no specification. It exists in people, and it passes along kinship lines. Four consequences follow directly:

1. **The knowledge cannot be licensed, sold or taught at scale**, because it is not written.
2. **An interruption is irreversible.** If the holders do not transmit it, it is gone; there is nothing to recover it from. This is the sharpest possible statement of why transmission, and not documentation alone, is what actually preserves a craft.
3. **Documentation is in tension with secrecy.** Writing down a family-held alloy would preserve the knowledge and destroy the family's exclusive advantage in it - which is a real dilemma, not a policy oversight, and naming it as a dilemma is worth more than proposing a solution.
4. **Legal instruments do not touch it.** A registered name protects who may call goods by that name. It does not protect, transmit or compensate the knowledge that makes the goods possible.

Alongside this sit the time facts from earlier lessons, which quantify the transmission problem without any invented statistics: a **Banaras brocade saree takes two to eight weeks**; the **Aranmula mirror is polished for several days**. A craft whose single unit takes weeks is a craft whose apprentice earns very little for a long time, and that is the economic content of "transmission is failing" - stated as a mechanism rather than as a mood.

### 6. Comparative treatment: four ways craft knowledge can be stored

| Storage form | Example in this session | Can it be recovered if transmission breaks? | Who controls it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Held in kin** | the Aranmula alloy composition, held within the families involved | **no** - there is no external record | the family |
| **Held in a named community** | the weaving expertise attributed to the Garo, Khasi and Jaintia; the Vankar weavers of Kutch; Mizo women weavers | partially - other members of the community hold it | the community |
| **Held in a workshop or town** | Moradabad's Nakkashi engraving; Firozabad's chandeliers; Channapatna's lacquered toys | yes, while the cluster survives | the cluster's labour pool |
| **Held in a written record** | a documentation publication describing histories and production processes | yes, to the extent the description is complete | whoever holds the record |

Reading down the third column gives the ranking of fragility, and it explains why the same policy instrument produces different results in different crafts. A cluster can absorb the loss of individual artisans; a family-held secret cannot absorb the loss of a single generation. This is the most precise thing this session can say about craft vulnerability, and it rests entirely on recorded facts.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** This lesson's central claim - that invisible stages go unpaid - is asserted as reasoning without a single figure. Given the session's own insistence on evidence, is the argument not exactly the sort of unsupported generalisation it forbids elsewhere?

**Reply.** The distinction is between an **empirical claim** and a **structural claim**, and only the first requires figures. The structural claim is: *a stage with no separate product has no separate market and therefore no independently determined price*. That is true by the construction of the value chain, and the session states it as reasoning, not as a finding. What would require evidence - and what this session therefore does **not** assert - is any claim about how much anyone earns, what proportion of artisans are women, how many households perform preparatory work, or whether the situation is improving. None of those numbers appears here, and an answer must not invent them either. The permitted move is to state the structure, to cite the record's one explicit gendered-production statement, and to use the khadi case to show that the structure can be deliberately changed. That is a complete argument without a single fabricated number.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the **social and distributional half** of any craft answer, and it supplies it in citable form. The **floor-design record** - drawn freehand from the centre in concentric geometric patterns, in erasable chalk, lime, powdered marble or rice-lime mixtures, during religious or auspicious family occasions - is the limiting case that shows where craft instruments cannot reach, and very few answers will contain it. **Mizoram's statement that women are primarily responsible for weaving** is the one explicit gendered-production citation available. The **Aranmula family-held alloy** is the best single illustration of irreversible knowledge loss and of the documentation-versus-secrecy dilemma. The **khadi counter-example** shows that a definition can be built around an invisible stage. Together these convert a sentimental paragraph into four evidenced ones.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that "women dominate Indian handicrafts" without the record's specific, limited statements.
- Supplying proportions, incomes or employment figures; none exists in this session.
- Treating floor designs as a minor or decorative class rather than as the limiting case for craft instruments.
- Assuming documentation solves transmission; a written description is not a transmitted skill, and where knowledge is deliberately secret, documentation and transmission are in tension.
- Reading the invisible-stage argument as an empirical finding rather than as a structural one.
- Forgetting that khadi is defined by its **spinning** stage, which is the counter-example to the whole argument.

### Revision notes

1. **Floor designs** are drawn during **religious or auspicious family occasions**, **freehand from the centre in concentric geometric patterns**, using **natural, erasable materials - chalk, lime, powdered marble, or rice-lime mixtures**.
2. Floor design is the **limiting case**: no buyer, no durable object, no workshop, no market - and therefore largely outside the reach of documentation, certification and registration alike.
3. The instruments in this topic are designed for **traded goods**; a large part of craft practice is domestic, occasion-driven and untraded.
4. **Mizoram**: **women are primarily responsible for weaving** the Puan - the record's clearest gendered-production statement.
5. **Manipur**: muga-thread embroidery on **phanek** borders, worn by **women** at weddings and auspicious occasions - gender attached to **use**.
6. **Meghalaya**: **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** named as skilled weavers - communities, not genders.
7. The sequence is **ginning, spinning, weaving**; only **weaving** produces a named practitioner and a named good.
8. Structural reasoning: a stage with **no separate product** has **no separate market** and therefore **no independent price**; recognition attaches to the visible stage; distributional questions inside the household are untouched by every instrument here.
9. **Khadi is the counter-example**: defined as **hand-spun** cotton fabric, with the **All India Spinners' Association** of **1925** named for the preparatory stage itself.
10. **Aranmula Kannadi**: the **alloy composition is held within the families involved** - knowledge stored in kin, unwritten, unlicensable, and irrecoverable if transmission breaks.
11. The documentation-versus-secrecy **dilemma**: recording a family-held formula preserves the knowledge and destroys its exclusivity.
12. **Four storage forms, ranked by fragility**: kin (irrecoverable) - community (partly recoverable) - cluster (recoverable while the cluster lives) - written record (recoverable to the extent it is complete).
13. Time facts that quantify the transmission problem without inventing data: **two to eight weeks** per Banaras brocade saree; **several days** of polishing for an Aranmula mirror.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The instruments available to protect Indian crafts are designed for traded goods, while much craft practice is untraded." Examine this proposition. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is best tested against the class of craft least like a traded good.

**Floor designs** are one of the twelve recorded classes of Indian handicraft. They are drawn during religious or auspicious family occasions, freehand from the centre in concentric geometric patterns, in natural, erasable materials such as chalk, lime, powdered marble or rice-lime mixtures. There is no buyer, no durable object, no workshop and no product.

Apply the three instruments. **Documentation** can record the practice and its method, and this is the only one of the three that reaches it at all. **Certification** tests a sample against a published specification, and there is no sample. **Registration** under the Geographical Indications of Goods (Registration and Protection) Act, 1999 protects a place-linked name for **goods** - and where nothing is sold, there is no name in commerce to misuse.

The same logic reaches inside traded crafts. The recorded sequence is ginning, spinning and weaving, but only weaving yields a named practitioner and a named good; the preparatory stages, frequently domestic, have no separate product and therefore no independent price.

The proposition is therefore sound, with one important qualification: the scope of the instruments can be **chosen**. Khadi is defined as hand-spun cotton fabric, and the body organised around it in 1925 was a Spinners' Association - a deliberate decision to make the preparatory stage the named one.

**Scoring note:** **15 marks = the floor-design record reproduced with its materials and occasion 4 + each of the three instruments tested against it separately 5 + the ginning-spinning-weaving extension to traded crafts 3 + the khadi qualification showing scope is a choice 3.** An answer supplying any artisan income or employment figure loses three marks, since none is available here.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Aranmula metal mirror is a better illustration of craft fragility than a generalised statement that traditional skills are dying, and identify the dilemma it creates for any documentation-based response.

**Model answer:** It is better because it identifies **where the knowledge is stored**, and storage location determines recoverability. The record states that the Aranmula Kannadi is a handmade metal-alloy mirror of Kerala, polished for several days to obtain its surface, and that the **composition of the alloy is held within the families involved**. The technical core of the craft therefore exists in no manual, specification or patent; it exists in people and travels along kinship lines. Four consequences follow with no need for any statistic. The knowledge **cannot be licensed or taught at scale**, because it is unwritten. An interruption in transmission is **irreversible**, because there is no external record from which to reconstruct it. A **registered name does not touch it** - registration governs who may lawfully use a place-linked product name, not who holds the knowledge that makes the product possible. And the labour figure, several days of polishing per mirror, gives the economic mechanism behind any transmission failure: a long unpaid learning period against a small number of finished pieces. The **dilemma** for documentation is direct. Writing the alloy composition down would preserve the knowledge against loss - which is documentation's entire purpose - and would simultaneously destroy the exclusivity on which the families' position rests, because a published formula can be used by anyone. So the instrument that best addresses the risk of loss is the instrument that most threatens the holders' interest, and the honest examination move is to state the dilemma rather than to propose a resolution the evidence cannot support.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that documenting a craft is always in the craftspeople's interest. Where knowledge is held deliberately and exclusively - in kin, as here - documentation transfers the knowledge out of their control. The interests of the craft, of the public and of the craftspeople can diverge, and an answer that notices the divergence is doing better analysis than one that recommends documentation as an unqualified good.
