### Visual: the instrument, drawn as it actually operates

```text
  WHAT IT IS
  A GEOGRAPHICAL INDICATION is a SIGN that a particular item has
  ORIGINATED FROM A PARTICULAR REGION ONLY. It ensures that NONE
  OTHER than those registered as AUTHORISED USERS are allowed to
  use the popular PRODUCT NAME.

  ELIGIBILITY
  To get a GI tag, a product needs to be PRODUCED, PROCESSED OR
  PREPARED in a specific region.

  THE STATUTE AND THE MACHINERY
   Act ......... GEOGRAPHICAL INDICATIONS OF GOODS
                 (REGISTRATION & PROTECTION) ACT, 1999
   In force .... 2003
   Certifier ... the Government of India
   Registry .... GI REGISTRY, office in CHENNAI
   Under ....... DEPARTMENT FOR PROMOTION OF INDUSTRY AND INTERNAL
                 TRADE (DPIIT), MINISTRY OF COMMERCE AND INDUSTRY
   Scope ....... ONLY GOODS. NOT SERVICES.
   Validity .... 10 YEARS, RENEWABLE every 10 years
   Classes ..... 34
   Fee ......... the prescribed application fee under the Act's rules

  REGISTRATION IS PROHIBITED WHERE THE INDICATION:
   1. would be likely to DECEIVE or cause CONFUSION
   2. would be CONTRARY TO ANY LAW in force
   3. comprises or contains SCANDALOUS or OBSCENE matter
   4. comprises or contains matter likely to HURT THE RELIGIOUS
      SUSCEPTIBILITIES of any class or section of citizens of India
   5. would otherwise be DISENTITLED TO PROTECTION IN A COURT
   6. is determined to be a GENERIC name, or has ceased to be
      protected / has fallen into disuse in its country of origin
   7. although literally true as to the territory, FALSELY REPRESENTS
      that the goods originate in ANOTHER territory, region or locality

  INTERNATIONAL FRAME
   Paris Convention for the Protection of Industrial Property
   WTO Agreement on Trade-Related Aspects of Intellectual Property
     Rights (TRIPS)
   WIPO: THREE METHODS OF PROTECTION -
     1. SUI GENERIS - a law applying to GIs exclusively and specifically
     2. CERTIFICATION MARKS and COLLECTIVE MARKS
     3. MEASURES FOCUSING ON BUSINESS PRACTICES (e.g. consumer
        protection, suppressing unfair competition)
   India's route is method 1: a dedicated statute.

  NAMED CASES
   DARJEELING TEA ... India's FIRST GI-tagged product, GI status 2004-05
   KANGRA TEA ....... Himachal Pradesh; registered as a PROTECTED GI
                      in the EUROPEAN UNION
   NILAMBUR TEAK .... Kerala; Nilambur is a municipality in MALAPPURAM
                      district; OLDEST TEAK PLANTATION IN THE WORLD;
                      hosts the WORLD'S FIRST TEAK MUSEUM

  WHAT IT DOES **NOT** DO
   X not a QUALITY certificate      X not a UNESCO or heritage category
   X not PERMANENT (10-year term)   X does not cover SERVICES
   X does not secure INCOME, working conditions, raw material or
     skill transmission
   X does not travel abroad automatically - KANGRA TEA had to be
     registered SEPARATELY in the EU
   X does not stop a NON-REGISTERED producer copying the DESIGN, so
     long as the protected NAME is not used
```

Every line in that box is examinable, and the last block is where most candidates lose marks.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Almost everyone can say that a GI tag is given to products from a particular region. Almost nobody can say what it is a right *to do*, what it is a right *against*, how long it lasts, what it excludes, or where its limits fall. The gap between those two states of knowledge is the difference between a sentence and an answer.

### 2. The definition, read word by word

**A Geographical Indication is a sign that a particular item has originated from a particular region only. It ensures that none other than those registered as authorised users are allowed to use the popular product name.**

Take it in three pieces.

- **"A sign"** - the thing protected is a *sign*, which in practice is the product's place-linked **name**. Not the product, not the process, not the community.
- **"has originated from a particular region only"** - the claim the sign makes is about **origin**. It is not a claim about quality, age, authenticity, tradition or virtue.
- **"none other than those registered as authorised users are allowed to use the popular product name"** - the legal effect is **exclusion from a name**. The right-holder's remedy is against someone *using the name*, not against someone *making a similar product*.

Alongside this sits the eligibility rule: **to get a GI tag, a product needs to be produced, processed or prepared in a specific region.** The three verbs matter - production, processing and preparation are alternatives, so the link to the region can attach at any of those stages rather than requiring all of them.

### 3. The statutory machinery, stated precisely

- India enacted the **Geographical Indications of Goods (Registration & Protection) Act, 1999**, which **came into force in 2003**.
- **Certification of Registration** of a GI is given by the **Government of India** under that Act.
- It is given by the **GI Registry** of the Government of India, with its **office in Chennai**.
- The GI Registry works under the **Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade**, under the **Ministry of Commerce and Industry**.
- **Only goods can be given GI certification - not services.**
- **GI registration is valid for 10 years and can be renewed every 10 years.**
- A GI certification is given in **34 classes**.
- The application carries a **prescribed fee** under the Act's rules.

Four of these are the most frequently mis-stated facts in the whole topic, and each should be rehearsed as a correction of a specific error: the Act is **1999** but in force from **2003** (two dates, not one); the Registry is at **Chennai** under **DPIIT** in the **Ministry of Commerce and Industry** (not a culture ministry, and not a UNESCO body); the scope is **goods only**; and registration is **not permanent** but runs for ten years and must be renewed.

### 4. The grounds on which registration is prohibited

This is the least-known part of the instrument and therefore the most valuable. Registration is prohibited for geographical indications:

1. **the use of which would be likely to deceive or cause confusion**;
2. **the use of which would be contrary to any law for the time being in force**;
3. **which comprise or contain scandalous or obscene matter**;
4. **which comprise or contain any matter likely to hurt the religious susceptibilities of any class or section of the citizens of India**;
5. **which would otherwise be disentitled to protection in a court**;
6. **which are determined to be generic names or indications of goods and are therefore not, or cease to be, protected in their country of origin, or which have fallen into disuse in that country**;
7. **which, although literally true as to the territory, region or locality in which the goods originate, falsely represent to persons that the goods originate in another territory, region or locality.**

Two of these repay real thought.

**The generic-name ground (6)** is the instrument's most interesting internal tension. A name that becomes generic - used by everyone for a type of product rather than for goods from a place - loses its capacity to indicate origin and therefore loses protection. Commercial success and the survival of the right are consequently in tension: the more widely a place-linked name is used, the closer it moves towards genericity. This is a genuinely sophisticated point and very few answers contain it.

**The literally-true-but-misleading ground (7)** is the subtle one. A name can be factually accurate about where goods were made and still be barred, if it would lead people to think they came from somewhere else - for instance because an identical or similar place name is famous elsewhere. The ground shows that the statute is concerned with the **impression created in the buyer's mind**, not merely with cartographic truth.

### 5. The international frame, and India's chosen route

Geographical indications, as part of **intellectual property rights**, are included in the **Paris Convention for the Protection of Industrial Property**. They are also governed through the **WTO's Agreement on Trade-Related Aspects of Intellectual Property Rights (TRIPS)**. Both play an important role at the international level.

According to the **World Intellectual Property Organization (WIPO)**, there are **three methods of protection** of GIs:

1. the ***sui generis* method**, which applies to GIs **exclusively and specifically**;
2. **certification marks and collective marks**;
3. **measures focusing on business practices**, for example laws relating to **consumer protection** or **suppressing unfair competition**.

**India's route is the first**: a dedicated statute enacted for geographical indications alone. Saying so converts a descriptive answer into a comparative one, because it invites the observation that a country could instead have protected origin names through trade-mark law or through unfair-competition law, and that India chose not to.

The second method also explains something important for this topic: a **certification mark** is a recognised route to GI protection in some jurisdictions, which is exactly why the certification-versus-registration firewall of Lesson 11 matters. A voluntary product-safety certification is **not** a certification mark used as a GI mechanism, and conflating the two is an error the instrument's own taxonomy makes easy to avoid.

### 6. The named cases

- **Darjeeling Tea** became the **first GI-tagged product in India** and was given GI status in **2004-05**. This is the one registration date this session asserts, because it is recorded, and it is the correct opening fact for any GI answer.
- **Kangra Tea** from **Himachal Pradesh** got registered as a **protected Geographical Indication in the European Union**. The tea is known for its unique fragrance and taste and contains antioxidants, amino acids and other beneficial compounds. Its analytical value is that it answers the question *does a domestic GI protect a product abroad?* - and the answer is no: cross-border protection required a **separate registration** in that jurisdiction.
- **Nilambur Teak** of **Kerala** got the GI tag. **Nilambur is a municipality in the Malappuram district of Kerala**; it has the **oldest teak plantation in the world** and hosts the **world's first Teak Museum**.
- **Bidri craft** (Bidar, Karnataka), **Pembarthi metal craft** (Pembarthi, Telangana), **Aranmula Kannadi**, **Bastar Dhokra**, **Channapatna Toys**, **Kondapalli Bommalu**, **Khurja Pottery**, **Nizamabad Black Pottery**, **Blue Pottery**, **Pokhran Pottery**, **Nakshi Kantha**, **Phulkari** (Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan), **Chamba Rumal**, **Puttapaka Telia Rumal** (Puttapaka, Telangana), **Kani Shawl**, **Tangaliya Shawl**, **Kachchh Shawl**, **Pochampalli**, **Patan Patola**, **Baluchari**, **Kolhapuri chappal**, **Chhau Mask**, **Tirukanur Papier Mache** and **Nirmal** crafts have all been encountered in this session as registered goods. **No registration year is asserted for any of them**, because none is recorded here, and inventing one is the commonest fabrication in this topic.

The **total number of registered goods changes continuously**, and any figure must carry the date on which it was true and the source from which it came. This session treats totals in the ledger rather than in the teaching, and it does not put an undated number into a learner's memory.

### 7. Comparative treatment: what a GI is, against three things it is confused with

| | **Geographical Indication** | **Trade mark** | **Quality or safety certification** | **International heritage recognition** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **What is protected** | a place-linked product **name** | a trader's own distinctive sign | nothing is protected; a specification is **tested** | nothing is protected; an element is **listed** |
| **Who may use it** | **registered authorised users** who produce in the region | the proprietor and licensees | anyone whose sample passes | not applicable |
| **Basis of the right** | **origin** | distinctiveness and use in trade | conformity with published criteria | cultural significance, assessed by a body |
| **Duration** | **10 years, renewable** | renewable in terms fixed by trade-mark law | as long as the certificate is maintained | indefinite, as a listing |
| **Owner** | a collective of producers, through registration | typically a single enterprise | the certifying body owns the scheme | no owner |
| **Does it certify quality?** | **no** | no | **yes, against stated criteria** | no |
| **Does it relate to India's cultural ministries?** | **no** - DPIIT, Ministry of Commerce and Industry | no | no | that is the heritage owner's territory |

The most important row is the last. A GI is repeatedly mis-described as a cultural or heritage tag. It is **commercial intellectual property administered by the trade ministry's department**, and saying so precisely is worth a mark on its own.

### 8. The four things a GI does not do, argued rather than listed

1. **It does not certify quality.** The definition is about origin. Two producers in the same registered region, both authorised users, may make goods of very different quality, and both are entitled to the name.
2. **It does not secure livelihood.** Nothing in the instrument addresses price, working conditions, raw-material access or skill transmission - the four links of Lesson 2's chain where craft failures actually occur.
3. **It does not travel.** Kangra Tea's separate European registration is the proof. A domestic registration is a domestic right.
4. **It does not stop copying of the design.** Because the right attaches to the **name**, a producer outside the region can lawfully make a visually identical article and sell it under a different name. This is the gap through which appropriation claims run, and it is the single most under-stated limitation of the instrument.

A fifth, structural limitation belongs with them: because a GI is defined by a **region**, it fits imperfectly where technique is held by a **community** that does not coincide with the region - the North-East case of Lesson 10, and the family-held Aranmula alloy of Lesson 16.

### 9. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** This lesson is relentlessly negative about the instrument. If a GI does so little, why has India built a dedicated statute, a registry and a promotional apparatus around it?

**Reply.** Because the thing it does, nothing else does - and because that thing is genuinely valuable. Before registration, a place-linked name is a **common resource**: anyone can use it, so no producer can capture the reputation attached to it, and the incentive to maintain the standards that built that reputation is weak. Registration converts the name into an **excludable asset held collectively by producers in the region**. That is a real economic transformation, it is achieved by no other instrument in this topic, and it is why the *sui generis* route exists at all. The criticisms in section 8 are therefore not criticisms of the instrument but a specification of its boundary - and the correct examination position is exactly that: **a GI is a necessary and narrow instrument that solves one problem completely and leaves the rest of the chain untouched**. An answer that says "GI has failed to save Indian crafts" has misunderstood what it was for; an answer that says "GI protects our heritage crafts" has misunderstood it in the opposite direction.

### Examination use

This lesson is the topic's highest-yield single block, and it should be reproducible almost verbatim: **definition, Act of 1999 in force 2003, GI Registry at Chennai under DPIIT in the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, goods only, ten years renewable, 34 classes, prescribed fee**. The **seven prohibited grounds** are the part that distinguishes a prepared answer, and the **generic-name ground** is the sophisticated observation to deploy in a 20-mark answer. **Darjeeling Tea, 2004-05, India's first** is the standard opening fact; **Kangra Tea's separate European registration** is the standard answer to the cross-border question; **Nilambur Teak** supplies a non-craft example with two superlatives attached. The **four things a GI does not do** are the concluding paragraph of almost any GI answer.

### Traps introduced here

- Giving 1999 as the year the Act came into force; it was enacted in 1999 and came into force in 2003.
- Placing the GI Registry under a culture ministry; it is under DPIIT, Ministry of Commerce and Industry, with its office at Chennai.
- Saying GI covers services; it covers goods only.
- Treating registration as permanent; it is valid for ten years and renewable every ten years.
- Listing GI beside international heritage categories as though it were one of them.
- Asserting that a domestic GI protects a product abroad.
- Supplying a registration year for any craft other than Darjeeling Tea's recorded 2004-05.
- Quoting a national total of registered goods without its date and source.

### Revision notes

1. **Definition**: a **sign** that an item has **originated from a particular region only**, ensuring that **none other than registered authorised users** may use the **popular product name**.
2. **Eligibility**: the product must be **produced, processed or prepared** in a specific region.
3. **Act**: **Geographical Indications of Goods (Registration & Protection) Act, 1999**; **in force 2003**.
4. **Registry**: **GI Registry**, office at **Chennai**, under **DPIIT**, **Ministry of Commerce and Industry**.
5. **Scope**: **only goods**, not services. **Validity**: **10 years, renewable every 10 years**. **Classes**: **34**. **Fee**: as prescribed under the Act's rules.
6. **Seven prohibited grounds**: deception or confusion; contrary to law; scandalous or obscene; likely to hurt religious susceptibilities; otherwise disentitled to protection in a court; generic or lapsed in the country of origin; literally true but falsely representing another origin.
7. **The generic-name tension**: wide commercial use pushes a name towards genericity, which is itself a ground for refusing protection.
8. **International frame**: **Paris Convention**; **WTO TRIPS**. **WIPO's three methods**: ***sui generis***; **certification and collective marks**; **measures focusing on business practices** such as consumer protection and suppressing unfair competition. **India uses the *sui generis* route.**
9. **Darjeeling Tea** - India's **first** GI-tagged product, **2004-05**.
10. **Kangra Tea** - Himachal Pradesh; registered as a **protected GI in the European Union** - proof that cross-border protection requires **separate registration**.
11. **Nilambur Teak** - Kerala; **Nilambur** is a municipality in **Malappuram** district; **oldest teak plantation in the world**; **world's first Teak Museum**.
12. **No registration year is asserted** for any craft in this session other than Darjeeling Tea's recorded 2004-05.
13. **Four limits**: not a quality certificate; no livelihood guarantee; no automatic foreign protection; no protection of the **design** against a producer who avoids the name. **Fifth, structural**: a region-defined right fits community-held or family-held technique imperfectly.
14. **What it does achieve**: it converts a place-linked name from a common resource into an **excludable asset held collectively by producers of the region** - which no other instrument here does.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"A Geographical Indication is a necessary but strikingly narrow instrument." Critically examine, with reference to Indian handicrafts. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

A Geographical Indication is a sign that an item has originated from a particular region only, ensuring that none other than registered authorised users may use the product name. India protects it through the *sui generis* route - the Geographical Indications of Goods (Registration and Protection) Act, 1999, in force from 2003 - administered by the GI Registry at Chennai under the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade, Ministry of Commerce and Industry. It covers goods only, in 34 classes, for ten renewable years.

**Why it is necessary.** An unregistered place-linked name is a common resource: anyone may use it, so no producer can capture the reputation attached to it. Registration converts that name into an excludable asset held collectively by producers of the region. No other instrument here achieves this. Darjeeling Tea, registered in 2004-05, was the first Indian case.

**Why it is narrow.** It protects a name, not a product: a non-registered producer may lawfully copy a design while avoiding the name. It does not certify quality, since all authorised users in the region may use it regardless. It lapses if not renewed. It does not travel - Kangra Tea required separate registration in the European Union. And because it is region-defined, it fits imperfectly where technique is community-held, as with North-Eastern weaving, or kin-held, as with the Aranmula mirror's alloy.

It therefore solves one problem completely and leaves the craft chain's material, income and transmission links untouched.

**Scoring note:** **20 marks = full statutory particulars, with 1999 and 2003 distinguished 5 + the common-resource-to-excludable-asset argument for necessity 5 + at least four distinct limits, each argued from the instrument's own definition 6 + two named cases used as evidence rather than decoration 2 + the structural region-versus-community mismatch 2.** Verdicts such as "GI has saved Indian crafts" or "GI has failed" both lose the final four marks, because neither outcome claim is evidenced here.

### Concept check

**Question:** A producer outside a registered region makes an article visually indistinguishable from a registered craft and sells it under an invented brand name, without using the registered name at all. What, if anything, does the Geographical Indications Act reach? What does your answer show about the instrument?

**Model answer:** On the facts given, the registered right is **not engaged**. The statutory definition protects a **sign** indicating that an item has originated from a particular region only, and its effect is that **none other than those registered as authorised users are allowed to use the popular product name**. The wrong the instrument addresses is therefore the **use of the name**. A producer who avoids the name entirely - selling under an invented brand - has not done the thing the right prohibits, however similar the article looks. Two qualifications should be noted rather than over-claimed. First, the prohibited-registration grounds show that the statute is alert to **impressions**, not only to literal truth: an indication that is literally true about origin but **falsely represents that goods originate in another territory** cannot be registered, which signals a general concern with misleading the buyer. Second, the article's design may be exposed to entirely different bodies of law that this topic does not own and this session does not assert. What the answer shows about the instrument is its precise shape: it is a **name right**, collectively held by authorised users in a region, and it is close to inert against **design copying** by a producer who keeps clear of the name. That gap is exactly where appropriation complaints arise, and it is why documentation, community organisation and market access - rather than registration alone - are the responsive measures where the grievance is about design rather than about nomenclature.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that registration creates a general monopoly over a craft. It creates an exclusive right to a **name**, held by those registered as authorised users producing in the region, for a renewable ten-year term. Everything else about the craft - its design, its technique, its market and its workforce - remains outside the right, and an answer that treats the tag as a general shield will over-claim in a way an examiner can test with a single hypothetical of exactly this kind.

## Lesson 18 - The institutional and market layer as it now stands, and what is not measured

Progress: 18/19 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the promotional and facilitation apparatus around registered goods, the handicrafts-side enforcement support, the one-district initiative, what an official reply does and does not supply, and the discipline of citing a dated figure

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Geographical Indications chapter's statutory and institutional content, and the handicrafts chapter's livelihood framing, both of which this lesson extends to the present institutional layer
CA search: "current institutional support for Geographical Indication registration, marketing and export of GI-tagged products in India, with a dated official record"
CA found: Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 2292, "Export of GI Tags Registered Products", answered on 7 August 2026 by the Minister of State in the Ministry of Commerce and Industry - the session's single current linkage, verified 29 September 2026
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what sits around a registered craft today

```text
                         A REGISTERED CRAFT
                                 |
     +---------------------------+---------------------------+
     |                           |                           |
  GETTING REGISTERED        REACHING A BUYER           DEFENDING THE NAME
     |                           |                           |
  handholding support        fairs, pavilions and       enforcement of
  for applicant              exhibitions at national    registered GIs and
  associations and           and international level    reimbursement of
  authorised bodies                                     legal expenses
     |                           |                           |
  assistance with            one-district initiative    provided by the
  deficiencies in            programmes sanctioned      Office of the
  applications and           for GI products            Development
  compliance with the                                   Commissioner
  1999 Act and its           promotion across           (HANDICRAFTS),
  Rules                      media, transport hubs      MINISTRY OF TEXTILES
                             and retail outlets
     |                           |                           |
  APPLICATION FEE            facilitation and           financial assistance
  REDUCED BY 80 PER CENT     awareness through          of up to Rs. 1.50 lakh
  by notification dated      Intellectual Property      towards enforcement
  3 NOVEMBER 2025            Facilitation Centres       and legal expenses
     |
  reimbursement of 100 PER CENT of actual registration expenditure,
  subject to a ceiling of Rs. 2 LAKH per registered GI, under the
  Intellectual Property Rights component of the MSME Innovative Scheme

                                 |
                                 v
                    WHAT IS **NOT** MEASURED
        "There is no dedicated database exclusively for exports
         of GI-tagged products."
        -> so no export value for GI goods can honestly be quoted
```

Everything in that diagram comes from one dated official answer, and the last box is the most useful part of it.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Lesson 17 established what the instrument *is*. A candidate then faces a different question in the examination hall: what actually happens around it now, and what figures can be safely quoted? The honest answer has two halves - a set of concrete, dated facilitation measures, and an explicit statement of what nobody measures. Most answers supply neither and instead fill the space with invented figures. This lesson replaces both halves with recorded content.

### 2. The single current record, and exactly what it says

The record is a **Rajya Sabha unstarred question on the export of GI-tag registered products, answered on 7 August 2026** by the **Minister of State in the Ministry of Commerce and Industry**. The question asked for the total number of GIs registered over five years, the quantum of exports of GI-tagged products over the same period, and the steps taken to promote marketing, exports and assistance to producers and artisans. The reply contains the following, each of which is a hard fact with an identified source and date.

**On registration facilitation.** The **GI Registry provides continuous handholding support** to applicant associations and authorised bodies **throughout the registration process**, including assistance in **addressing deficiencies in applications** and ensuring **compliance with the provisions of the Geographical Indications of Goods (Registration and Protection) Act, 1999 and the Rules framed thereunder**.

**On the cost of applying.** By **notification dated 3 November 2025**, the **fee payable for filing GI applications and related proceedings has been reduced by 80 per cent**, stated as making GI protection **more affordable for artisans, farmers, producer organisations and traditional communities**.

This single fact has an important consequence for Lesson 17's statutory block, and it must be handled precisely. An older printed figure for the application fee is superseded to the extent of this reduction. The disciplined formulation is therefore: **the Act's rules prescribe an application fee, and that fee was reduced by 80 per cent with effect from the notification dated 3 November 2025**. This session does not print a current rupee figure, because the reply states a percentage reduction rather than a resulting amount.

**On support to applicants.** Under the **Intellectual Property Rights component of the MSME Innovative Scheme**, the **Ministry of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises** provides consultancy, IP facilitation and awareness programmes covering geographical indications, with **reimbursement of 100 per cent of the actual expenditure incurred for registration of a GI, subject to a maximum ceiling of Rs. 2 lakh per registered GI**, including professional fees, government fees, preparation of applications, prosecution, hearings, GI logo design, laboratory testing and other incidental expenses. Applicants also receive end-to-end support through **Intellectual Property Facilitation Centres**.

**On market access.** The Ministry has organised **25 GI Mahotsavs across different States** to provide market access to authorised GI users, has **sanctioned 164 programmes under the One District One GI initiative**, and has facilitated participation of GI producers in international exhibitions. The Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade has separately undertaken promotion through fairs and pavilions at national and international exhibitions, documentaries and television campaigns, social media campaigns, airport and metro branding, and dedicated GI retail outlets.

**On enforcement support, from the textiles side.** The **Office of the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts), Ministry of Textiles**, provides **financial assistance of up to Rs. 1.50 lakh** towards **enforcement of registered GIs** and **reimbursement of legal expenses incurred for protection of GI rights**, as approved by the competent authority.

**On what is not measured.** Asked for the quantum of exports of GI-tagged products over five years, the reply states plainly: **there is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged product.**

### 3. Why the last fact is the most valuable one

A candidate's instinct on reading "no dedicated database" is to treat it as a gap in the answer. It is the opposite: it is the most useful sentence in the entire record, for three reasons.

1. **It licenses a refusal.** Any answer that quotes an export value for GI-tagged goods is quoting something that, on the ministry's own statement, is not separately compiled. Knowing this lets a candidate decline to supply a figure and say **why** - which reads as command of the subject rather than as ignorance.
2. **It identifies a real policy gap.** If the promotional apparatus is directed at exports and market access, and no dedicated export data exists for the category being promoted, then the effect of that apparatus on the category cannot be assessed from official data. That is a genuine, citable observation about evaluation capacity.
3. **It demonstrates the session's whole method.** Lesson 2 argued that craft answers fail by asserting unmeasured things. Here is an official document saying, in terms, that a particular thing is not measured. The discipline of naming what is unknown is not a scruple; it is the thing that makes the rest of the answer credible.

### 4. Reading the institutional layer against the chain

Map each measure onto the seven links of Lesson 2, and the pattern is immediate.

| Measure | Which link it touches | What it does not reach |
|---|---|---|
| Handholding through the registration process | link 6, legal recognition | material, preparation, skill, community |
| 80 per cent reduction in the application fee | link 6, by lowering the **cost of access** to it | the same four links |
| 100 per cent reimbursement up to Rs. 2 lakh per registered GI | link 6, by removing the cost entirely for those who qualify | the same four links |
| Intellectual Property Facilitation Centres | link 6, by supplying expertise | the same four links |
| GI Mahotsavs, fairs, pavilions, retail outlets, media campaigns | **link 5, market access** - the first measure in this table that does | material, preparation, skill, and the price actually received |
| One District One GI programmes | **link 5**, and indirectly link 4 by organising producers around a district identity | material and preparation |
| Enforcement assistance up to Rs. 1.50 lakh and legal-expense reimbursement | **link 6's enforcement side** - the right is worthless if it cannot be asserted | everything before link 5 |

Three conclusions follow, and all three are usable.

**First**, the apparatus is concentrated on links 5 and 6, which is exactly where Lesson 17 said the instrument operates, and it is therefore coherent rather than misdirected.

**Second**, the **enforcement measure is the most under-appreciated**, because a right that a small producer association cannot afford to litigate is a right in name only. Financial assistance towards enforcement and legal expenses addresses precisely that, and it comes from the **handicrafts side of the Ministry of Textiles** rather than from the registering department - which is itself worth noting, since it shows two ministries operating on the same instrument from different ends.

**Third**, **nothing in the apparatus reaches links 1 to 4** - material, preparation, skill and community. That is not a criticism. It is the same boundary Lesson 17 drew, now visible in the institutional record rather than only in the statute.

### 5. The discipline for citing current facts

Four rules, and this lesson is the place to fix them because it is the only lesson in the session that cites a current source at all.

1. **Name the instrument, the date and the answering authority.** "A parliamentary reply of 7 August 2026 by the Minister of State in the Ministry of Commerce and Industry" is citable; "recent government data" is not.
2. **Quote the form the source used.** The reply gives a **percentage** reduction in fees, a **ceiling** for reimbursement, **counts** of events and programmes, and a **maximum** for enforcement assistance. Reproduce the form; do not convert a percentage into a rupee figure the source did not state.
3. **Do not derive a total the source did not state.** The reply directs the reader to a published annexure for the number of GIs registered over five years, and a five-year registration list is **not** a cumulative national total. This session therefore asserts **no current national GI total**, and it flags any older printed total as carrying its own date.
4. **Treat "not measured" as a finding.** Where an authority says data does not exist, that statement is itself the citable fact.

### 6. Comparative treatment: three eras of institutional support for craft

| | **Court and guild era** | **The organised-movement era** | **The present apparatus** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Who supports the craft** | patron, temple, guild, merchant | an organised association built around a process stage | statutory registry, two ministries, facilitation centres |
| **What is supplied** | demand, material, protection of the craftsman's place | an organisation for **development, production and selling** | legal recognition, cost relief, market access, enforcement money |
| **What defines membership** | attachment to a patron or guild | performing the defined stage | being a **registered authorised user** producing in the region |
| **Recorded instance** | the ivory guild at Vidisha; Mughal karkhanas; Himroo for royal dress; Chamba Rumal under the rulers' patronage | the **All India Spinners' Association, 1925**; **KVIC, 1956** | the GI Registry, DPIIT, MSME facilitation, the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts) |
| **Its characteristic limit** | the craft dies with the patron | it reaches only the stage it is built around | it reaches only links 5 and 6 of the chain |

Why compare them? Because it shows that **every support structure in this topic's history has had a defined reach, and each failed at a different edge.** Patronage collapsed when patrons did. A process-defined association reached spinners and not, by itself, dyers or weavers. A registration apparatus reaches names and markets and not materials or households. The correct conclusion is not that the present arrangement is uniquely inadequate; it is that support structures are always partial, and that the analytical task is to identify which link is currently unattended.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** One parliamentary reply is a thin basis for an entire lesson on the institutional layer. A single document, however official, cannot describe a national apparatus.

**Reply.** Correct, and the lesson is deliberately bounded by that limitation rather than concealing it. Three things follow. First, everything asserted here is **what the reply states**, attributed to it, and nothing is extrapolated into a general description of Indian craft policy. Second, the reply is unusually well suited to the purpose because it answers questions about **registration facilitation, producer assistance, marketing and exports** simultaneously, which is precisely the institutional span this lesson needs. Third, and most importantly, the reply's own admission that no dedicated export database exists is a statement **against interest** - an answering ministry recording a limit in its own data - and such statements are the most reliable kind. The honest scope of this lesson is therefore: *here is what one dated official record establishes about the apparatus around registered goods, and here is what it explicitly says is not measured.* Anything beyond that would require sources this session has deliberately not gone looking for.

### Examination use

This is the lesson that supplies **current, citable, dated content** to an answer that would otherwise end on statute alone. The three most valuable items are the **80 per cent fee reduction by notification dated 3 November 2025**, the **reimbursement of registration expenditure up to a ceiling of Rs. 2 lakh per registered GI under the MSME Innovative Scheme's IPR component**, and the **enforcement and legal-expense assistance of up to Rs. 1.50 lakh from the Office of the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts), Ministry of Textiles**. The **25 GI Mahotsavs** and **164 One District One GI programmes** are the market-access counts. And the single most distinctive sentence available anywhere in this session is the official statement that **there is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged products** - which lets a candidate decline to supply a figure and explain why.

### Traps introduced here

- Converting the 80 per cent fee reduction into a rupee amount the source did not state.
- Quoting an export value for GI-tagged goods; the answering ministry states no dedicated database exists.
- Treating a five-year registration list as a cumulative national total of registered goods.
- Attributing the enforcement assistance to the registering department; it comes from the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts), Ministry of Textiles.
- Describing the apparatus as reaching artisan incomes; it reaches links 5 and 6 of the chain.
- Citing "recent government initiatives" without naming the instrument, its date and the answering authority.

### Revision notes

1. The current record is a **Rajya Sabha unstarred question on the export of GI-tag registered products, answered 7 August 2026** by the **Minister of State, Ministry of Commerce and Industry**.
2. **GI Registry handholding**: continuous support to applicant associations and authorised bodies, including **addressing deficiencies** and ensuring **compliance with the 1999 Act and its Rules**.
3. **Application fee reduced by 80 per cent** by **notification dated 3 November 2025**, stated to make protection more affordable for **artisans, farmers, producer organisations and traditional communities**.
4. **MSME Innovative Scheme, IPR component**: **100 per cent reimbursement of actual registration expenditure**, ceiling **Rs. 2 lakh per registered GI**, covering professional and government fees, application preparation, prosecution, hearings, **GI logo design**, laboratory testing and incidentals; end-to-end support through **Intellectual Property Facilitation Centres**.
5. **25 GI Mahotsavs** organised across States; **164 programmes sanctioned under One District One GI**; participation facilitated in international exhibitions.
6. **Office of the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts), Ministry of Textiles**: financial assistance of **up to Rs. 1.50 lakh** towards **enforcement of registered GIs** and **reimbursement of legal expenses**.
7. **No dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged products** - a citable statement that licenses a refusal to quote an export figure.
8. Map to the chain: registration support, fee relief, reimbursement and facilitation reach **link 6**; Mahotsavs, fairs and the one-district initiative reach **link 5**; enforcement assistance protects the **assertability** of the right. **Links 1 to 4 are unreached.**
9. **Two ministries** operate on the same instrument from different ends - Commerce and Industry registers and promotes; Textiles funds enforcement through the handicrafts office.
10. **Citing discipline**: name the instrument, the date and the answering authority; reproduce the **form** of the figure; do not derive a total the source did not state; treat "not measured" as a finding.
11. **Historical comparison**: patronage died with patrons; the **1925 Spinners' Association** and **KVIC of 1956** reached the stage they were built around; the present apparatus reaches names and markets.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Assess the adequacy of the current institutional support available to India's registered craft products, using dated official evidence. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

A parliamentary reply of 7 August 2026 by the Minister of State in the Ministry of Commerce and Industry sets out the present apparatus.

**Access to the right has been made cheaper and easier.** The GI Registry provides continuous handholding through registration, including help with deficiencies and compliance with the 1999 Act and its Rules. By notification dated 3 November 2025 the application fee was reduced by 80 per cent. Under the MSME Innovative Scheme's intellectual-property component, 100 per cent of actual registration expenditure is reimbursed, subject to Rs. 2 lakh per registered GI, with support through Intellectual Property Facilitation Centres.

**Market access is being organised.** Twenty-five GI Mahotsavs have been held across States, 164 programmes have been sanctioned under One District One GI, and producers have been placed in national and international exhibitions.

**The right is being made assertable.** The Office of the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts), Ministry of Textiles, gives financial assistance of up to Rs. 1.50 lakh towards enforcement of registered GIs and legal expenses - significant, because an unenforceable right is nominal.

**The gaps are two.** Nothing here reaches raw material, preparatory labour, skill transmission or the price actually received by the producer. And the reply states that there is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged products, so the effect of the export-facing effort cannot be assessed from official data.

Adequate for the instrument's own purposes; silent on the rest of the chain.

**Scoring note:** **15 marks = the source named with its date and answering authority 2 + at least five distinct measures reproduced in the form the source used 6 + the mapping of measures onto the chain's links 4 + the two gaps, including the explicit no-database statement 3.** Any invented export value or artisan income figure loses five marks outright, because the record states the data does not exist.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate wishes to write that "GI-tagged handicraft exports from India have grown substantially in recent years." What is wrong with the sentence, what could be written instead, and why is the substitute stronger?

**Model answer:** The sentence asserts a trend in a quantity that is **not separately compiled**. Asked in Parliament for the quantum of exports of GI-tagged products over five years, the answering ministry stated on **7 August 2026** that **there is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged product**. A growth claim about that category therefore has no official basis, and an examiner who knows the record will treat it as fabricated. What could be written instead is a statement about **effort** rather than **outcome**, using the same source: that the Government has directed substantial effort at the export and market-access link - **25 GI Mahotsavs** across States, **164 programmes sanctioned under One District One GI**, participation in international exhibitions, and dedicated retail and promotional channels - while simultaneously recording that **no dedicated export database exists for the category**, so that the effect of this effort cannot be assessed from official data. The substitute is stronger for three reasons. It is **citable**, since every element is attributed to a dated reply by a named authority. It is **safer**, because it claims only what the source claims. And it is **analytically better**, because it converts an unverifiable assertion into a genuine finding about evaluation capacity - namely that a promotional programme exists for a category whose principal outcome variable is not measured. That observation is the kind of thing that distinguishes a prepared answer, and it costs nothing but accuracy.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that an absence of data is a weakness in your answer. The absence, when it is officially recorded, is data. Candidates routinely invent figures to fill a perceived gap; the better move is to state, with attribution, that the figure does not exist - which is both true and more impressive than any number would have been.

## Lesson 19 - Attribution, chronology, region, terminology and the construction of a craft answer

Progress: 19/19 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the four controls assembled, the directive-and-demand map, mark-scaled structures, thesis options, the four prohibited narratives, and the worked construction of an answer

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the whole handicrafts and geographical-indications material re-read for internal consistency, together with the topic's own ownership statement, directive map, mark-scaled structures, thesis options and factual-risk controls
CA search: "Not applicable - synthesis and answer-construction lesson; the session's single current record was placed in Lesson 18"
CA found: None sought for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the four controls, as a checklist run over every sentence you write

```text
  BEFORE A SENTENCE LEAVES YOUR PEN, RUN FOUR CHECKS.

  [1] ATTRIBUTION CONTROL --------------------------------------------
      Have I named the TECHNIQUE, or only the place?
      Have I named the COMMUNITY where the record names one?
      Am I claiming a maker, a meaning or a motive the record
        does not supply?
      FAIL EXAMPLES: "Banarasi embroidery" | "tribal art symbolising
        nature" | "the weavers of this region have always..."

  [2] CHRONOLOGY CONTROL ---------------------------------------------
      Which of the SIX CLOCKS does this date belong to?
        material | technique | institution | patronage | practice | status
      Am I dating a LIVING PRACTICE from an EXCAVATED MATERIAL?
      Am I supplying a REGISTRATION YEAR that no source gives?
      FAIL EXAMPLES: "this 5,000-year-old craft" | "GI-tagged in 2010"

  [3] GEOGRAPHY CONTROL ----------------------------------------------
      Does the record give a REGION or a STATE? Use what it gives.
      Does the record name MORE THAN ONE state? Then so must I.
      Does this name identify a PLACE, an OBJECT TYPE or an OPERATION?
      FAIL EXAMPLES: "Kolhapuri chappal - Maharashtra only" |
        "ikat - Odisha" | "Jamdani - Bengal only"

  [4] TERMINOLOGY CONTROL --------------------------------------------
      Weave / surface: is the pattern IN the cloth or ON it?
      Resist: on the YARN or on the CLOTH?
      Inlay: WHAT is the pattern physically made of?
      Instrument: DOCUMENTATION, CERTIFICATION or REGISTRATION?
      FAIL EXAMPLES: "zari embroidery" for a brocade | "tie and dye"
        used for both bandhani and ikat without distinction |
        "UNESCO GI tag"

  IF ALL FOUR PASS, THE SENTENCE IS DEFENSIBLE.
```

The four controls are the accumulated output of the previous eighteen lessons, compressed into a form that can be run in the examination hall in about two seconds a sentence.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

By this point the content is in place, and the remaining failure mode is **construction**: writing an answer that contains correct facts in an order that does not argue anything, or writing a defensible paragraph followed by an indefensible one. This lesson supplies the controls, the structures and the thesis options, and then demonstrates them on a worked example.

### 2. Control one: attribution

Three rules and one habit.

- **Name the operation.** The topic's own instruction is exact: name plus region alone is a matching exercise; name plus region plus **process** is an answer. Ikat, brocade, embroidery, painted or printed cloth and applique use different production logics, and the logic is the discriminator.
- **Name the community where the record names one.** Garo, Khasi and Jaintia; Adi, Apatani, Nyishi and Monpa; Vankar; Gamit, Vasava and Dholiya Patel; Nayaka; Rabari; Mizo women weavers; the families holding the Aranmula alloy. Each of those is a citable attribution, and each raises an answer above the general.
- **Do not supply what the record withholds.** A marketed label cannot establish tribal authorship, unchanged continuity, fair remuneration or community consent. Nor can a motif's appearance establish its meaning.

The habit: after writing any craft name, ask *and what do the hands do?* If you cannot answer, the sentence is not yet an answer.

### 3. Control two: chronology

The six clocks from Lesson 3 - material, technique, institution, patronage, practice, status - and one rule: **state the number, then state the noun it belongs to**.

The specific prohibitions, each of which corresponds to a real error:

- Do not state a **registration year** for a craft unless it is recorded. In this session, **only Darjeeling Tea's 2004-05** is asserted.
- Do not present a **total** - of registered goods, of artisans, of exports - without the date on which it was true and the source that supplied it. A five-year registration list is not a cumulative total, and no export figure exists for GI-tagged goods.
- Do not date a **living practice** from an **archaeological material**.
- Do not date a **tradition** from the event it commemorates. The Battle of Khongjom is 1891; the ballad's date is unrecorded.
- Carry the source's hedges. Sericulture at Harappa and Chanhudaro is **suggested**; Indus weaving and dyeing was **perhaps** well established; Kasuti **possibly** dates to the Chalukyan era. A deleted hedge is an invented certainty.

### 4. Control three: geography

- **Use the specificity the record uses.** Patan; Bhoodan Pochampally; Chirala; Puttapaka in Nalgonda district; Pipli village; Bulandshahr district; Azamgarh district; Murshidabad; Panipat; Aurangabad; Bikaner; Nubra; Malappuram; Bidar; Pembarthi. Where the register names a district or a village, the district or the village is the discriminating fact, not the State.
- **Keep the historic home and the registered territory apart.** Telia Rumal is historically associated with **Chirala, Andhra Pradesh**, while the registered indication is **Puttapaka Telia Rumal** of **Puttapaka, Telangana**; Phulkari is practised across **Punjab, Haryana and Delhi** while its registration covers **Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan**. In both cases two true statements describe two different things.
- **Reproduce multiplicity.** Kolhapuri chappal - **Karnataka and Maharashtra**. Phulkari - practised in **Punjab, Haryana and Delhi**, registered for **Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan**. Bandhani - **Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu**. Ikat - **Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh**. Bohra cap weaving - **Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh**. Karma Naach - **many tribes of Eastern India, especially the Chota Nagpur Plateau**. Chhau dance - **Jharkhand, Odisha and West Bengal**, one style maskless.
- **Identify what kind of word you are holding.** Some craft names contain a place - Patan Patola, Awadh Jamdani, Banaras brocade, Kolhapuri chappal, Pochampalli. Some name an **operation** - ikat, brocade, batik, applique, filigree, repousse. Some name an **object type** - rumal, puan, phanek, durrie, chappal. Only the first kind fixes a place.
- **Remember the items that identify nothing by themselves.** The **mekhela chador** is woven in two states. A **rumal** is a square cloth, not a technique. A **dark ground with a bright pattern** describes at least three different metal crafts.

### 5. Control four: terminology

Four pairs that are not synonyms, each resolved by a single question.

| Pair | The resolving question |
|---|---|
| **Weave** and **surface decoration** | Is the pattern built during weaving, or added to finished cloth? |
| **Yarn resist** and **cloth resist** | Was the resist applied before or after the cloth existed? |
| **Single** and **double ikat** | Is one set of yarns resist-dyed, or both? Only **Patola** is recorded as both |
| **Documentation**, **certification** and **registration** | Does it describe, test, or confer an exclusive right to a name? |

And four terms that must never be merged: **mordant** (fixes dye to fibre) and **resist** (prevents dye reaching the material); **inlay** (setting one material into another) and **engraving** (cutting a line and adding nothing); **applique** (cut fabric sewn on) and **embroidery** (thread worked with a needle); **GI** (statutory, origin-linked, goods only, ten renewable years, DPIIT) and a **voluntary product certification** (private, specification-based, says nothing about origin).

### 6. The directive and demand map

| If the question says | It is really testing | Do NOT write |
|---|---|---|
| *Discuss GI as an instrument for safeguarding craft* | what the instrument legally does, and the four or five things it does not | "GI protects our traditional crafts" |
| *Craft as livelihood and heritage* | the material-to-market chain, and which link is failing | "handicrafts are our cultural heritage" |
| *Identify a named craft or textile* | region, community, process and product, discriminated from the nearest look-alike | a one-word state match |
| *Decline of Indian handicrafts under colonialism* | this topic's material vocabulary and production organisation, handed to the primary owner | the deindustrialisation verdict itself |
| *Cultural diversity and socio-economic marginality* | one concrete craft-community instance, handed to the primary owner | the sociological proposition |
| *Ecology and craft* | raw-material dependence, regulation and sustainability | romanticised "eco-friendly traditional art" |
| *Gender and craft* | where women's labour sits in the chain and where it is invisible | unsupported generalisation |
| *Folk and tribal traditions* | named communities, anchors, technique and living change | timeless authenticity |
| *Institutional support for artisans* | the registration, market-access and enforcement measures, each mapped to a link | a wish-list of schemes |

### 7. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis -> the instrument or craft defined precisely -> two named cases with region and process -> one limitation -> verdict |
| **15** | Thesis -> the craft-ecology chain -> three or four named craft or textile cases -> the mechanism or statutory detail in full -> livelihood or market limitation -> verdict |
| **20** | All of the above -> **plus** the historical production layer, from archaeological material through court and guild organisation to the colonial market and the present -> ecology, gender and appropriation dimensions -> international protection with a named case -> the institutional route -> a verdict that answers the exact wording |

### 8. Four thesis options, ready to deploy

1. **The narrow-instrument thesis.** A Geographical Indication is origin-linked intellectual property, not a safeguarding programme: it protects the exclusive right of registered authorised users to a place-linked product **name**, for goods only, for a renewable ten-year term. It does not by itself secure income, working conditions, raw-material access or skill transmission.
2. **The chain thesis.** A craft survives only if every link holds - material, preparation, skill, producing community, market access, legal recognition and safeguarding - and public policy typically intervenes at the legal and market links while the material and household links fail unattended.
3. **The continuity-with-change thesis.** Craft continuity does not mean unchanged production: court, temple, guild, merchant, colonial and contemporary certification-and-tourism markets each reshaped design, scale and labour. Tanchoi arrived through trade; batik originated abroad; Chamba Rumal borrowed a painting idiom. Adaptation is the normal condition, not a fall from authenticity.
4. **The knowledge-storage thesis.** What is most at risk in Indian craft is not the object but the **place where the knowledge is kept** - in kin, in a community, in a cluster or in a record - and the fragility of a craft can be read directly from that storage form. The Aranmula alloy, held within the families involved, is the limiting case.

### 9. The four prohibited narratives, and what replaces each

| Prohibited narrative | Why it fails | What to write instead |
|---|---|---|
| **One-state exclusivity** - "X is the craft of state Y" | the record itself names multiple states for several crafts, and technique travels | reproduce the multiplicity: Kolhapuri for Karnataka and Maharashtra; ikat across four states; Phulkari across three |
| **Fabricated symbolism** - "the motif represents the community's bond with nature" | no source supplies it; it is the writer's invention | write what the record says - that Nagaland's motifs **reflect cultural identity** - and stop |
| **Fixed tribal authenticity** - "unchanged for centuries" | no continuity evidence is supplied, and it denies the community a history | describe the present practice, name the community, and mark any continuity claim as an inference |
| **The generic dying craft** - "this dying art must be saved" | it names no failing link and proposes no matching instrument | run the five-question diagnostic: material, preparation, transmission, market access, name misuse - and name the first failure |

A fifth, quieter prohibition belongs with them: **the unsupported GI year**. Writing "granted GI status in 2015" for a craft whose registration year no source supplies is the most easily detected fabrication in this topic, and it contaminates an otherwise sound answer.

### 10. A worked construction

Take a demand of the kind this topic actually attracts: *"Examine the relationship between craft, community and legal recognition in India."*

**Step one - decide what is being asked.** Three nouns, so three sections and a relationship to argue. This is a 15-mark shape: thesis, chain, cases, mechanism, limitation, verdict.

**Step two - choose a thesis.** The knowledge-storage thesis fits best, because it connects all three nouns: craft is knowledge, knowledge is held by communities, and recognition attaches to regions rather than to communities.

**Step three - assemble the evidence, running the four controls.**

- *Community-held technique, attributed:* Garo, Khasi and Jaintia weavers of Meghalaya with eri silk; Vankar weavers of the Kutch shawl; Mizo women as primary weavers of the Puan; the Aranmula alloy held within the families involved; the Gamit, Vasava, Dholiya Patel, Nayaka and Rabari storage traditions. *(Attribution control: every name is recorded. Terminology control: "weavers", "alloy composition", "storage containers" are process terms.)*
- *The instrument, stated exactly:* a sign that an item originated from a particular region only; registered authorised users; goods only; ten renewable years; the 1999 Act in force from 2003; the Registry at Chennai under DPIIT. *(Chronology control: two dates, correctly assigned.)*
- *The mismatch:* the right is defined by **region**; the technique is held by **community** or **kin**. The mekhela chador, woven in Assam and Meghalaya by different communities from different fibres, is the concrete instance. *(Geography control: multiplicity reproduced.)*
- *The institutional layer:* facilitation, fee reduction by notification dated 3 November 2025, reimbursement up to Rs. 2 lakh per registered GI, and enforcement assistance up to Rs. 1.50 lakh from the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts). *(Chronology control: the date is named and attached to the right noun.)*

**Step four - write the verdict.** Not "GI is good" or "GI has failed", but: *registration secures a name against misuse and lowers the cost of obtaining and enforcing that protection; what it cannot do is hold knowledge that lives in kin and community, and the instrument responsive to that risk is transmission support rather than nomenclature.*

**Step five - check the four controls once over the whole answer.** No invented registration year. No undated total. No single-state claim where the record gives two. No confusion of instrument types. No symbolism that nobody recorded.

### Examination use

Everything in this lesson is examination use. The order of operations to memorise: **decide the shape from the marks; choose one of the four theses; assemble evidence running the four controls; write a verdict that answers the exact wording; re-run the controls over the finished script.** The **prohibited-narrative table** should be read the evening before the paper, because its four entries are the four sentences a tired candidate writes by reflex.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing a correct set of facts in an order that argues nothing.
- Deploying a thesis that does not fit the question's nouns.
- Running the controls on individual facts but not on the finished answer.
- Choosing the 20-mark structure for a 10-mark demand, and running out of time before the verdict.
- Allowing one indefensible sentence - an invented year, an invented symbol, a single-state claim - to contaminate an otherwise sound script.

### Revision notes

1. **Four controls**: attribution, chronology, geography, terminology. Run them on every sentence, then on the whole answer.
2. **Attribution**: name the **operation**; name the **community** where the record names one; supply nothing the record withholds. After every craft name, ask *what do the hands do?*
3. **Chronology**: six clocks - material, technique, institution, patronage, practice, status. State the number, then its noun. **Only Darjeeling Tea's 2004-05 registration year is asserted here.**
4. **Hedges are load-bearing**: sericulture is **suggested**; Indus weaving and dyeing was **perhaps** established; Kasuti is **possibly** Chalukyan.
5. **Geography**: use the record's specificity; reproduce multiplicity; identify whether a name holds a place, an operation or an object type; remember that the **mekhela chador**, the word **rumal** and a **dark ground with a bright pattern** identify nothing alone.
6. **Terminology**: weave against surface; yarn resist against cloth resist; single against double ikat; documentation against certification against registration. Never merge mordant with resist, inlay with engraving, applique with embroidery, or a GI with a voluntary certification.
7. **Directive map**: nine demand types, each with what it really tests and what not to write.
8. **Mark scaling**: 10 - thesis, definition, two cases, one limit, verdict. 15 - add the chain, more cases, the full mechanism, a livelihood limit. 20 - add the historical production layer, ecology, gender and appropriation, international protection with a named case, and the institutional route.
9. **Four theses**: narrow instrument; chain; continuity-with-change; knowledge storage.
10. **Four prohibited narratives**: one-state exclusivity; fabricated symbolism; fixed tribal authenticity; the generic dying craft. Plus the **unsupported GI year**.
11. **The five-question diagnostic** replaces the decline narrative: material, preparation, transmission, market access, name misuse.
12. The verdict is never "it worked" or "it failed"; it is **which link the instrument reaches, and which remains unattended**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Craft, community and legal recognition in India do not map onto one another. Critically examine this proposition and indicate what follows for craft policy. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Indian craft knowledge is frequently held by a **community** or a **family**, while legal recognition is defined by a **region**. The two units do not coincide, and the consequences are practical.

**The evidence for community-held technique is strong.** In Meghalaya the Garo, Khasi and Jaintia are recorded as skilled weavers, working eri silk and cotton into shawls, sarongs and mekhela chadors. The Kachchh shawl is mainly woven by the Vankar community. In Mizoram, women are primarily responsible for weaving the Puan. Most sharply, the Aranmula metal mirror's alloy composition is held within the families involved - knowledge in kin, not in any record.

**The instrument, by contrast, is regional.** A Geographical Indication is a sign that an item originated from a particular region only, restricting the product name to registered authorised users; it covers goods, for ten renewable years, under the 1999 Act in force from 2003, through the Registry at Chennai under DPIIT.

**The mismatch is demonstrable.** The mekhela chador is woven in both Assam and Meghalaya, by different communities, from different fibres. A single place-linked name must either exclude genuine makers or include non-holders of the technique.

**What follows.** Access measures help - the application fee was reduced by 80 per cent by notification dated 3 November 2025, registration costs are reimbursed up to Rs. 2 lakh, and enforcement assistance of up to Rs. 1.50 lakh is available. But a name right cannot hold kin-held knowledge; transmission support can.

**Scoring note:** **20 marks = four community- or kin-attached technique records 5 + the instrument's statutory particulars stated exactly 4 + the mismatch demonstrated with a concrete two-state case rather than asserted 5 + dated institutional measures in the form the source used 3 + a policy conclusion that names transmission rather than recommending "more GIs" 3.** An answer recommending simply that more crafts be registered loses the final three marks, because it does not respond to the mismatch it has just established.

### Concept check

**Question:** Run the four controls over this paragraph and rewrite it defensibly: "Dhokra is an ancient 4,000-year-old tribal art of Chhattisgarh, whose motifs symbolise harmony with nature. It received a GI tag in 2008, which has certified its quality and saved the dying craft."

**Model answer:** **Attribution control fails three times.** "Art" conceals the operation: Dhokra is **lost-wax non-ferrous casting**, in which a wax model is encased, melted out and replaced by molten metal, after which the mould is broken. "Tribal art of Chhattisgarh" suppresses the fact that the technique is practised by artisan communities across central and eastern India, of which **Bastar Dhokra** is a registered instance used for brass ornaments. And "motifs symbolise harmony with nature" is **fabricated symbolism** - no source supplies it. **Chronology control fails twice.** "4,000-year-old" dates a living practice from an archaeological material: what is recorded is that bronze casting is attested by the Mohenjo-daro Dancing Girl of around 2500 BC, which says nothing about the age of any present community's practice. "A GI tag in 2008" is an **unsupported registration year**; this session asserts only Darjeeling Tea's recorded 2004-05. **Geography control fails once**: one state is given where the technique spans several. **Terminology control fails twice**: a Geographical Indication does **not certify quality** - it restricts a place-linked product **name** to registered authorised users - and "saved the dying craft" is an outcome claim with no failing link named and no evidence. A defensible rewrite: *Dhokra is a lost-wax non-ferrous casting tradition practised by artisan communities across central and eastern India, in which the mould is broken to release each piece, so that no two are identical. Bastar Dhokra is registered as a good and is used for brass ornaments; no registration year is recorded here. Casting in bronze - copper alloyed with tin - is attested in India from around 2500 BC by the Mohenjo-daro figure, but that dates the material and the technique, not any living community's practice. Registration protects the place-linked name against use by others; it does not certify quality, secure income or reach the raw-material, transmission or market links on which the tradition's survival actually depends.*

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a paragraph containing several true-sounding facts is safe. The original contains one recoverable fact - that Dhokra is associated with Bastar - and five separate fabrications wrapped around it. In this topic, errors are usually **additive**: each embellishment adds a claim the record does not support, and a single script can accumulate five of them in three sentences. The controls exist to be run sentence by sentence, precisely because the fabrications arrive one at a time.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

## Ownership audit, stated before anything is claimed

Every routing ledger held for this syllabus was searched for questions owned by this topic; the locally held official Preliminary and Main papers were opened and read; and the core art source's reproduced question lists for the handicrafts chapter, the geographical-indications chapter and the folk-tradition chapters were read in full. The result is set out exactly, including where the result is nil.

| Window | What was checked | Result for this topic |
|---|---|---|
| **Main Examination, General Studies Paper I, 2018-2023** | the complete routing ledger for that window | **No question owned.** No item in the window is routed to this topic |
