**Answer-neutrality, observed absolutely.** No answer letter, truth-value marking, option elimination or corrective mapping is recorded anywhere in this session. The **provisional 2026 Set-A key held locally was not consulted**. The reproducing source prints its own answer keys beneath each of its reproduced question lists; **those keys were encountered during extraction and were deliberately not carried into this session**. No official Preliminary key for 2018 is held in this repository.

**Reproduction policy for objective items, stated once.** Where this session owns the discriminating material in an item, reproducing the item's pairings or response choices alongside the teaching would effectively disclose the answer. Those items are therefore recorded as an **exact demand description** - the item's structure, the named subjects it tests, and the lessons that supply the material - and their pairings and response choices are **withheld**. Where this session does **not** own the discriminating material, the item is reproduced exactly and the gap is declared. Main Examination items carry no response choices and are reproduced in full.

## The three owned Preliminary items

### 2018, Preliminary Examination, General Studies Paper I, question 22 - three folk traditions and their States

**Recorded as a demand description; pairings and response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact structure.** A "consider the following pairs" item setting out **three pairs of tradition against State**, followed by four response choices, and asking which of the pairs given above is or are correct. The three traditions named in the item are the **Chapchar Kut festival**, the **Khongjom Parba ballad** and the **Thang-Ta dance**.

*(Structure and the three named traditions read directly from the locally held official 2018 Preliminary General Studies Paper I, **Set C**. The same item is separately reproduced in the core art source's handicrafts chapter and again in its festivals chapter.)*

**Key status.** **No official 2018 Preliminary key is held in this repository.** The reproducing source prints its own key beneath the item; it was **not carried into this session**. No answer letter is recorded or inferred here.

**Spelling variance recorded.** The ballad's name appears as **Khongjom Parba** in the source's folk-music table and in one of its reproduced lists, and as **Khongjom Parva** in another reproduction of the same item. The martial form appears as **Thang-Ta** and as **Thang-ta**. The variants are recorded, not resolved.

**Demand.** A **three-pair matching** item across three *different kinds* of tradition - a festival, a ballad and a martial performance - all drawn from the north-east. It is built on the assumption that a candidate who has revised "north-eastern culture" as a block will be unable to separate them.

**Why the pairings are withheld.** This session owns the discriminating material for all three traditions.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 15 records each with its anchor: **Chapchar Kut** is celebrated in **Mizoram** during **March**, after completion of the locals' most arduous task of **jhum operation**; **Khongjom Parba** is recorded in the **Manipur** folk-music register with the **Battle of Khongjom (1891)** as its theme; and **Thang-Ta** is recorded for **Manipur**, with **thang** meaning sword and **ta** meaning spear, its performers enacting a **mock fight sequence**. The same lesson supplies the near neighbours that an item of this kind uses as distractors - **Zo Kutpui**, also of Mizoram; **Rangma**, the Nagaland war dance; and **Paika**, the martial folk dance of Jharkhand and southern Odisha, performed with wooden spears and shields.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** It is a pure **anchor-recall** item. A candidate who holds the agricultural operation, the dated battle and the weapon etymology can resolve it; a candidate who holds only "north-eastern tradition" cannot. That is the entire lesson of Lesson 15's discipline, tested directly.

### 2018, Preliminary Examination, General Studies Paper I, question 54 - three craft traditions and their States

**Recorded as a demand description; pairings and response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact structure.** A "consider the following pairs" item setting out **three pairs under the headings *Craft* and *Heritage of***, followed by four response choices, and asking which of the pairs given above is or are correct. The three crafts named in the item are **Puthukkuli shawls**, **Sujni embroidery** and **Uppada Jamdani sarees**.

*(Structure and the three named crafts read directly from the locally held official 2018 Preliminary General Studies Paper I, **Set C**. The same item is reproduced in the core art source's handicrafts chapter, where the first craft is printed as "Puthukkuli".)*

**Key status.** **No official 2018 Preliminary key is held in this repository.** The reproducing source's printed key was **not carried into this session**. No answer letter is recorded or inferred here.

**Demand.** A **three-pair craft-to-State matching** item that deliberately mixes three different operations - a **shawl**, an **embroidery** and a **figured weave** - so that a candidate who has stored crafts as name-to-state pairs has no fallback when one pairing is altered.

**Why the pairings are withheld.** This session owns the region attribution for all three.

**What this session supplies.** **Puthukkuli** is recorded in Lesson 9 as the shawl of the **Toda** community of the **Nilgiris, Tamil Nadu**. **Sujni** is recorded in Lesson 8 as an embroidery tradition of **Bihar**, associated with the **Muzaffarpur** area. **Uppada Jamdani** is recorded in Lesson 5 as a saree tradition of **Uppada, in coastal Andhra Pradesh**, belonging to the Jamdani technique family alongside Jamdani of Bengal and Awadh Jamdani at Varanasi. For all three, this session records the **region and, where available, the community**, and asserts **no registration year, no process description beyond the family placement, and no motif vocabulary**, because its substantive base does not supply them.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** It demonstrates why Lesson 5's insight - that **Jamdani names a technique family with several regional homes** - has direct examination value. A candidate who has stored "Jamdani equals Bengal" will misplace the Andhra centre; a candidate who has stored the technique will not.

### 2026, Preliminary Examination, General Studies Paper I, question 29 - Oeko-Tex certification for eri silk

**Recorded as a demand description; statement text and response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact structure.** A two-statement item asking which of the statements given is or are **the most significant implication or implications of obtaining Oeko-Tex certification for Eri Silk in the global textile industry**, followed by the standard four-way selection code covering each statement alone, both, and neither. The certification is named here because the official item names it and the routing ledger records it under that name; **no statement text, response choice, truth-value or answer letter is reproduced or inferred.**

*(Structure read directly from the locally held official 2026 Preliminary General Studies Paper I, Set A.)*

**Key status.** A **provisional** 2026 Set-A key is held locally. **It was not consulted, and no answer letter is recorded or inferred here.**

**Demand.** An **implication-evaluation** item rather than a recall item. It requires the candidate to know what kind of instrument the named certification is, and therefore what kind of claim a certificate can and cannot support.

**Why the statements and choices are withheld.** This session owns both halves of the discriminating material - the production fact about the fibre and the institutional character of the certification.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 4 and Lesson 10 record that **eri silk is commonly produced in Meghalaya**, where the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** tribes are skilled weavers producing **shawls, sarongs and mekhela chadors** from **both silk and cotton** - together with the standing cautions that eri must not be attributed exclusively to Assam, that **muga is the golden silk of Assam**, and that the **mekhela chador identifies no state by itself**. Lesson 11 records the institutional character of the relevant instrument class: a **voluntary, privately administered product-safety certification**, run by an **international association of independent textile research and testing institutes**, whose principal standard tests articles for **harmful substances** with limits graded by **intended use and degree of skin contact**, subject to **periodic renewal** - and which is **silent about origin** and is **not an intellectual-property right**. Lesson 11's three-column firewall and Lesson 17's statutory block together supply the certification-against-registration distinction on which any evaluation of the item's statements rests.

**What this session does not supply, declared.** For the **Oeko-Tex** scheme named in the item: no limit value, no product-class detail beyond the skin-contact principle, no fee, no certificate duration, no Indian producer's certification status, no export figure and no commercial-effect evidence. Those are declared gaps and are not filled from any source.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** It is the clearest possible demonstration that this topic's examinable content has moved from **identification** towards **instrument literacy**. The item cannot be answered by knowing where eri silk is made; it can only be answered by knowing what a testing certification is for. That is precisely the firewall Lesson 11 builds.

## Owned items reproduced in the core source's question lists

### Civil Services Examination, 2015 - the definition of a painted textile tradition

**Recorded as a demand description; response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact structure.** A single-line definitional item asking what a named Indian textile tradition **refers to**, followed by four descriptive response choices, each combining a **technique**, a **material** and a **region**.

*(Structure read from the reproduced objective list in the core art source's handicrafts chapter. The source prints its own key; it was not carried here.)*

**Key status.** No official key for this year is held in this repository, and the reproducing source's key was not carried.

**Demand.** A **three-variable definitional** item. Each response choice varies technique, material and region together, so a candidate must hold all three to eliminate reliably.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 7 records the tradition as **hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours, commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh**, places it within the **mordant-and-dye-bath** system on a **cotton** ground, and notes that the wider craft record describes the tradition as spanning hand-drawn and block-printed work at Andhra Pradesh and Telangana centres. The same lesson supplies the near-neighbour operations that such an item uses to build its alternatives - block printing, wax resist and needle embroidery - and the boundary rule that separates a painted **cloth** from a painting **style**.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** It is the reason Lesson 7 insists that this tradition is a **textile process** rather than a painting style. A candidate who has filed it among folk paintings will be unable to discriminate among four choices that all sound plausible as folk art.

### Civil Services Examination, 2015 - which named items hold geographical-indication status

**Recorded as a demand description; the listed items and response choices withheld under the reproduction policy above.**

**Exact structure.** A three-item list asking which of the named goods **has or have been accorded 'Geographical Indication' status**, followed by a four-way selection code. The three items span a **textile**, a **prepared dish** and a **temple food offering**.

*(Structure read from the reproduced objective list in the core art source's geographical-indications chapter. The source prints its own key; it was not carried here.)*

**Key status.** No official key for this year is held in this repository, and the reproducing source's key was not carried.

**Demand.** A **status-recall** item, testing whether a candidate knows that the instrument covers a wide range of goods across categories rather than only crafts, and that not everything culturally famous is registered.

**Why the listed items are withheld.** This session owns the statutory framework that determines what is registrable, and reproducing the list beside that framework would effectively resolve the item.

**What this session supplies.** Lesson 17 records that a Geographical Indication covers **goods only**, in **34 classes**, and that to obtain one a product must be **produced, processed or prepared** in a specific region. It also records the **seven grounds on which registration is prohibited**, including the **generic-name** ground and the **literally-true-but-misleading** ground - which together explain why some famous names are registrable and others are not. Lesson 17 additionally records the named cases this session can evidence: **Darjeeling Tea**, India's first registration, in **2004-05**; **Kangra Tea**, separately registered as a protected indication in the **European Union**; and **Nilambur Teak** of Kerala.

**What the item teaches beyond itself.** It shows why Lesson 17's registrability rules matter more than a memorised list of registered goods. The list changes continuously; the rules do not.

## Cross-owned Main Examination items, reproduced in full

### 2024, Main Examination, General Studies Paper I, question 13

> **13.** How far was the Industial Revolution in England responsible for the decline of hadicrafts and cottage industries in India?
>
> (Answer in 250 words) **15**

*(Reproduced exactly from the locally held official 2024 General Studies Paper I, including the two spelling defects - "Industial" and "hadicrafts" - which are preserved as printed.)*

**Directive.** *How far was ... responsible* - a **degree-of-causation** directive requiring a calibrated verdict, not a yes or no.

**Ownership.** **Routed to the modern-history owner** covering the economic impact of British rule. The deindustrialisation debate - mechanised competition, tariff and trade policy, market displacement, the historiographical dispute - is that owner's analytical territory, and this topic does not attempt it.

**What this topic supplies.** The **object** of the alleged displacement, named precisely: the twelve material classes of Indian handicraft, with **glassware**, **cloth handicrafts** and **other metal crafts** as the classes most directly exposed to industrial substitution; and the **production organisation** that preceded the colonial market - household production, the guild attested at Vidisha, the court **karkhana**, and **putting-out or dadni** arrangements under which a merchant advanced material and took the goods. Lesson 2's chain supplies the analytical frame for saying *which link* an industrial competitor actually attacks: not the technique, but market access and the price a producer can obtain.

**Answer approach.** Open by naming the craft classes and the pre-existing production organisation, so that the answer is about something concrete. State explicitly that the causal economic verdict belongs to the modern-history and economy papers and hand it to them in one clause. Then contribute what this topic uniquely can: that a hand technique's cost structure - two to eight weeks for a single brocade saree - makes it vulnerable to volume substitution at the market link while remaining intact as a skill, which is why some crafts survived the same period that destroyed others. Close with a calibrated verdict that matches the directive's *how far*.

**What must not be attempted.** Tariff schedules, revenue systems, trade statistics, or an adjudication of the historians' dispute.

### 2024, Main Examination, General Studies Paper I, question 20

> **20.** Critically analyse the proposition that there is a high correlation between India's cultural diversities and socio-economic marginalities.
>
> (Answer in 250 words) **15**

*(Reproduced exactly from the locally held official 2024 General Studies Paper I.)*

**Directive.** *Critically analyse* - examine the proposition from both sides and reach a qualified position.

**Ownership.** **Routed to the Indian-society owner** covering the salient features and diversity of Indian society. The correlation claim itself is a sociological proposition and is not adjudicated here.

**What this topic supplies.** **One concrete instance**, stated with named evidence rather than as a generalisation: communities whose livelihood depends on a culturally specific, non-transferable skill, with weak market power. The recorded cases available are the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** weavers of Meghalaya; the **Vankar** weavers of the Kutch shawl; the **Mizo women** who are primarily responsible for weaving the Puan; and the families holding the **Aranmula** alloy composition. Lesson 2's margin table supplies the mechanism - that the margin attaches to control of credit, design and market information rather than to control of skill - and Lesson 16 supplies the structural point that preparatory stages have no separate product and therefore no independent price.

**Answer approach.** Supply the instance early and precisely, name the mechanism that links cultural specificity to weak market power, and then hand the sociological proposition to its owner explicitly rather than improvising a discipline this topic does not hold. The qualification that belongs here, and that strengthens the answer: cultural specificity is simultaneously an **economic asset** - it is what an origin-linked instrument exists to protect - and an **economic vulnerability**, and any correlation claim must account for both directions.

**What must not be attempted.** Any correlation claim, any statistic on marginality, or a general sociological argument about Indian diversity.

> Consider the following pairs:

**What the item nonetheless teaches.** It is a community-attribution item of exactly the kind Lesson 1's third field anticipates, and its existence is the reason this session insists that a **community** is a separate variable from a **region**. # CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These checks run across lesson blocks rather than within one lesson. Each is answerable only by combining material taught in at least two places.

## Block A - foundations and the chain (Lessons 1-3)

### Cumulative check 1

**Question:** Take one craft - glass - and show how it produces facts in all four age-classes. Then state precisely what a candidate may and may not conclude from the combination.

**Model answer:** **Class A, excavated artefact:** glass beads appear in the **Painted Grey Ware culture of the Ganges Valley around 1000 BC**; a **glass industry at Brahmapuri and Kolhapur in Maharashtra** operated between the **2nd century BC and the 2nd century AD**, producing **lenticular beads**; glass has been found at **Maski in Karnataka**, a Chalcolithic Deccan site, and remains at **Ahar, Hastinapur, Ahichchhatra, Eran, Ujjain and Firozabad**. **Textual evidence, which is Class A's companion:** the first reference to glass making is in the **Mahabharata**; the **Shatapatha Brahmana** uses **kanch or kaca**; the **Vyasayogacharita** refers to early spectacles. **Class B, court craft:** under the **Mughals** glassware flourished, visible in the **Sheesh Mahal** and in **hukkas, ittardans and engraved glasses**. **Class C and D, living and contemporary:** the industry today has many facets - bangles associated with Hyderabad's **churi ka jodas**, chandeliers at **Firozabad**, toys at **Saharanpur** and **Tikuli beads** at **Patna**. What may be concluded is that glass as a **material and a technique** is attested in India across a very long sequence, and that named centres work it today. What may **not** be concluded is continuity between any find-spot and any modern centre, even where the place name is identical: **Firozabad appears in both lists**, and the honest sentence records both facts without joining them. Nor may any motif, meaning or social role be inferred from a bead.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a coincidence of place names across two millennia as evidence of continuous production. The correct response to the Firozabad overlap is to state it as a striking coincidence worth noticing and to decline to build a lineage on it.

### Cumulative check 2

**Question:** Using material from at least three lessons, show that the intermediary in the Indian craft economy is a historical constant rather than a modern intrusion - and state what follows for policy.

**Model answer:** **Three historical forms, from three different periods.** The **guild** is attested by an inscription from Sanchi of the **2nd century BC** recording an **ivory guild at Vidisha**. The **karkhana** - the supervised court or elite workshop - organised high-input production for a patron, and the court record shows what that produced: **Himroo** for medieval royal dress, Mughal glassware, **Chamba Rumals** under the patronage of the rulers. **Putting-out, or dadni**, in which a merchant advanced material or money to household producers and took the finished goods, organised export textiles for distant markets. **The structural common feature** is that in each arrangement someone other than the maker controlled **raw material, credit, design or access to the buyer** - which is exactly the list in Lesson 2's margin table, where the margin is shown to attach to control of market information and credit rather than to control of skill. **What follows for policy** is that "eliminate the middleman" is not a coherent objective, because the intermediary function - financing production, aggregating output, bearing market risk, reaching distant buyers - has to be performed by someone. The real question is **who performs it and on what terms**: a merchant on advance, a cooperative owned by producers, a public procurement channel, or a direct retail route. Lesson 18's institutional record shows the state operating on precisely this link, through fairs, pavilions, a one-district initiative and retail outlets - all of which are attempts to shorten the distance between maker and buyer rather than to abolish the function.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the history as a moral story about exploitation. The record supports a structural claim - that control of credit, material and market access determines the division of the margin - and supports no claim at all about anyone's intentions.

## Block B - the textile sequence (Lessons 4-11)

### Cumulative check 3

**Question:** Four cloths are described: a metallic floral figure on a silk ground built during weaving; soft-edged geometric figures identical on both faces; a drawn narrative figure in vegetable dye on cotton; and white thread flowers worked on fine voile. Place each in its family, name the moment the pattern was decided, and give one Indian tradition for each.

**Model answer:** **The first** is **supplementary-weft brocade**. The pattern was decided **on the loom**, by an extra weft carried across only where the design falls and caught into the ground. The Indian case is **Banaras brocade** - silver and golden zari, floral motifs, generally silk sarees, **two to eight weeks** per piece - with **Tanchoi** at Varanasi as its near relative, recorded as having come via trade, inspired by Chinese weaving. **The second** is **yarn resist**, because a pattern identical on both faces means the colour is inside the thread. The pattern was decided **before the loom**, by binding sections of yarn against the dye. The Indian case is the **ikat** family, with centres in **Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh**, and the double form recorded for **Patola of Patan, Gujarat**. **The third** is **hand-painted mordant-dyed cloth**. The pattern was decided **after weaving**, by drawing mordant or dye onto a prepared cotton ground and letting the dye bath supply the colour. The Indian case is the tradition recorded as **hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours, commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh**. **The fourth** is **embroidery** - raised designs worked with a needle into finished cloth. The pattern was decided **after weaving**, with thread added to the surface. The Indian case is **Chikankari** of **Lucknow**, white thread on grounds recorded as **cotton, mulmul ka cotton, polyester and voile**. The organising principle across all four is the **moment of decision** - before the loom, on the loom, or after it - and it separates families that a purely visual description cannot.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the presence of metallic thread identifies a family. Metallic thread can be **woven in** as supplementary weft, as in brocade, or **stitched on**, as in the Gota and Karchobi work of Rajasthan. The material tells you nothing until you know at which moment it entered the cloth.

### Cumulative check 4

**Question:** Show, using at least three lessons, that "one craft, one state" is refuted by the record itself, and explain why the refutation matters analytically rather than merely factually.

**Model answer:** **The record refutes it repeatedly and explicitly.** Ikat's major centres are given as **Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat and Andhra Pradesh** - four states in a single sentence. Bandhani is largely practised in **Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu**. Phulkari is used in **Punjab, Haryana and Delhi**. Bohra cap weaving is recorded for **Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh**. The Kolhapuri chappal is registered with **Karnataka and Maharashtra** named together in one row. Karma Naach is performed by **many tribes of Eastern India, especially in the Chota Nagpur Plateau**. The Chhau dance has **three styles across Jharkhand, Odisha and West Bengal**, of which the Mayurbhanj performers do not wear masks at all, while the Chhau **mask** is registered to West Bengal alone. And the **mekhela chador** is woven in **both Assam and Meghalaya**, by different communities, from different fibres. **Why it matters analytically** is that the unit which holds a technique is frequently a **community**, and communities do not coincide with administrative boundaries. Lesson 10's North-East record makes this explicit: technique is attributed to the Garo, Khasi and Jaintia; to the Adi, Apatani, Nyishi and Monpa; to Mizo women; to the Vankar of Kutch; and, at its most extreme, to the **families holding the Aranmula alloy composition**. A right defined by **region** - which is what a Geographical Indication is - therefore fits a **community-held** technique imperfectly, either excluding genuine holders outside the region or including producers inside it who do not hold the technique. The one-state habit is thus not merely inaccurate; it conceals the central mismatch in the topic's policy argument.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that naming more states is the whole remedy. The point is not pluralism for its own sake but the identification of the **technique-holding unit**, which may be a community, a cluster, a family or a district, and which the state boundary may cut across.

## Block C - materials beyond cloth (Lessons 12-14)

### Cumulative check 5

**Question:** Assemble from at least three lessons the crafts whose identity rests on an **alloy or a surface treatment** rather than on a shape, and state what that tells you about how such crafts fail.

**Model answer:** **The alloy and surface-treatment crafts.** **Bronze** is **copper and tin**, and the alloy exists because it flows into a mould better than pure copper - so the alloy is the enabling condition for casting, anchored by the **Mohenjo-daro Dancing Girl of around 2500 BC**. **Bidri** is a **blackened zinc-and-copper alloy** whose blackening supplies the contrast against inlaid **silver**; without the blackening there is no Bidri. The **Aranmula Kannadi** is a **metal-alloy mirror** whose reflecting surface is obtained by **polishing for several days**, and whose **alloy composition is held within the families involved**. **Nizamabad black pottery** takes its identity from a **dark fired surface** rather than from a form. **Marori** is defined by **black lac filling an etched void**. **Kashmir papier mache** is defined by a recipe - **paper pulp with copper sulphate and rice flour**. **What this tells you about failure.** A craft identified by a **shape** can be reproduced by anyone who can copy the shape, so its vulnerability is imitation. A craft identified by an **alloy or a treatment** is different: the knowledge of the proportion, the blackening, the firing atmosphere or the polish is the craft, and that knowledge is usually **unwritten**. Its vulnerability is therefore not imitation but **loss** - and the Aranmula case is decisive, because knowledge held in kin has no external record from which it could be reconstructed. Lesson 16's storage table ranks the fragility: kin-held knowledge is irrecoverable; community-held knowledge is partly recoverable; cluster-held knowledge survives while the cluster does; written knowledge survives to the extent the description is complete.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a registered name protects the alloy. Registration governs **who may lawfully use a place-linked product name**. It does not require, reveal, transmit or compensate the technical knowledge that makes the product possible, which is why a name right and a knowledge risk are addressed by entirely different measures.

### Cumulative check 6

**Question:** Trace the toy across the four age-classes and state what its trajectory demonstrates about this topic's evidence.

**Model answer:** **Class A:** the **first evidence for toy carts** is Harappan, continuing into the early historic period, with **copper, bronze and terracotta** cart models found at **Alamgirpur, Ambkheri, Kalibangan and Lothal**, and similar models at **Atranjikhera, Nagarjunakonda, Sambhar, Sonapur, Ujjain and Brahmpuri**. **Class B and the historical materials:** in ancient and medieval times toys were made from **clay, paper, papier-mache and lacquered wood**, including **Tirupati dolls from redwood** in Andhra Pradesh. **Class C, living regional practice:** **Rajasthan** for colourful cloth dolls, **Assam** for pith dolls, southern India for wooden toys, **Mysore and Channapatna** for lacquered wooden toys, and **Kondapalli** for the **Ambari Hathi** made of soft **ponki** wood. **Class D, contemporary registered goods:** **Channapatna Toys** of Karnataka, traditionally made from **ivory wood** - a timber, not an animal product - with the town known as a toy town; and **Kondapalli Bommalu** of Andhra Pradesh, made from a soft wood with **moisture removed** and the **parts carved separately and then joined**. **What the trajectory demonstrates** is that a single object class can generate evidence in all four columns without the columns being a sequence. The Harappan cart model does not date the Channapatna workshop; the Channapatna registration says nothing about the Harappan cart. What the combination does establish is that toy-making is one of the longest continuously **attested** object classes in the Indian record - and "attested at many points" is a defensible formulation where "continuous for four thousand years" is not.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading "ivory wood" as elephant ivory. It is a timber name, and misreading it converts a living registered craft into a regulated-material problem that does not exist - the opposite of the genuine regulated-material case, which is ivory carving itself.

## Block D - people, instruments and the present (Lessons 15-19)

### Cumulative check 7

**Question:** Using at least four lessons, set out everything a Geographical Indication does not reach, and for each gap name the measure or condition that would reach it.

**Model answer:** **The instrument reaches one thing:** it makes a place-linked product **name** an excludable asset held by **registered authorised users**, for **goods**, for **ten renewable years**, under the **1999 Act in force from 2003**, administered by the **GI Registry at Chennai under DPIIT, Ministry of Commerce and Industry**. **What it does not reach, with the responsive measure for each.** **Raw material** - where a craft depends on a regulated or depleting substance, as ivory carving does, the responsive measures are documentation, museum conservation and material substitution, not registration. **Preparatory labour** - ginning, spinning and finishing have no separate product and therefore no independent price; the responsive measure is to define the product by the preparatory stage itself, which is exactly what **khadi** does as hand-spun cotton fabric, organised through the **All India Spinners' Association of 1925** and later **KVIC, 1956**. **Skill transmission** - where knowledge is held in kin, as with the **Aranmula alloy**, the responsive measure is transmission support and apprenticeship income, because a name right neither transmits nor compensates knowledge. **Quality** - registration certifies origin, not quality, so the responsive instrument is a **specification-based certification**, which tests a sample against published criteria and says nothing about origin. **Design copying** - because the right attaches to the name, a producer outside the region may lawfully make a similar article under a different name; the responsive measures are documentation, community organisation and market access. **Foreign markets** - a domestic registration does not travel, which is why **Kangra Tea** required separate registration in the **European Union**. **Enforcement cost** - a right that cannot be litigated is nominal, and the responsive measure is exactly the **financial assistance of up to Rs. 1.50 lakh towards enforcement and legal expenses** provided by the **Office of the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts), Ministry of Textiles**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from this list that the instrument is weak. It performs a transformation nothing else performs - converting a name from a common resource, which no producer can capture, into a collectively held excludable asset. The list above is a **boundary specification**, not a verdict.

### Cumulative check 8

**Question:** A question asks you to assess the state of Indian handicrafts today. Construct the skeleton of a 15-mark answer that uses no invented figure, and state which single sentence in it does the most work.

**Model answer:** **The skeleton.** *Thesis:* handicrafts are simultaneously a **livelihood and a cultural inheritance**, especially for tribal and rural communities - the source's own framing - and the two halves fail in different ways, so any assessment must say which half is in difficulty. *The chain:* raw material, preparation, skill, producing community, market access, legal recognition, safeguarding. *Diagnosis with named cases:* material risk, illustrated by a craft dependent on a regulated substance; preparation invisibility, illustrated by the ginning-spinning-weaving sequence in which only weaving is named; transmission risk, illustrated by the Aranmula alloy held within families and by the two-to-eight-week production time of a single brocade saree; market risk, illustrated by the historical constancy of intermediary control of credit and design. *The instruments and their reach:* documentation describes; certification tests a specification; registration confers a name right for goods, for ten renewable years. *The present apparatus, dated:* handholding through registration; the application fee reduced by **80 per cent** by notification dated **3 November 2025**; registration cost reimbursed up to **Rs. 2 lakh** per registered GI; **25 GI Mahotsavs** and **164 One District One GI programmes**; enforcement assistance up to **Rs. 1.50 lakh**. *The evaluation gap:* the answering ministry's own statement that **there is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged products**. *Verdict:* the instruments reach the market and legal links and are being made cheaper and more enforceable; the material, preparation and transmission links remain unattended, and the principal outcome variable is not measured. **The sentence that does the most work** is the last but one - the statement that no dedicated export database exists - because it simultaneously supplies a dated official fact, licenses a refusal to quote a figure, and converts the answer's conclusion from an opinion about performance into a finding about evaluation capacity.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a strong answer needs numbers. This skeleton contains five figures, every one of them dated and attributed, and not a single estimate of artisan numbers, incomes or export values - because none exists in the record. The absence, stated with attribution, is worth more than any invented total.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Six original questions follow, none of which reproduces or closely restates any verified question recorded in the PYQ section above, and none of which repeats a lesson-local stem. Word ceilings are stated and observed: **10 marks - 150 words; 15 marks - 250 words; 20 marks - 250 words**, the difference between the last two lying in the number of dimensions carried and the depth of qualification, not in length. Each model ends with a scoring note specific to that answer.

## Original 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt.** *"Indian craft design has always followed its patron." Examine this proposition with examples. (150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

The proposition is largely sound and must be stated carefully.

**Design has demonstrably followed the buyer.** Tanchoi silk arrived through **trade**, was inspired by **Chinese weaving**, and was then perfected at **Varanasi** as brocade resembling miniature paintings. **Batik** originated in **Indonesia** and became most popular in Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal. **Chamba Rumals** borrowed the idiom of **Pahari painting** and were made under the **patronage of the rulers**, often as wedding gifts. **Himroo** was made for **medieval royal dress** and survives at Aurangabad under a different market.

**The qualification.** Patronage shaped **design, scale and finish**; it did not create the **technique**, which is held by weavers, casters and embroiderers and outlives any particular buyer.

The proposition should therefore be accepted in a modified form: craft aesthetics track the patron, craft knowledge tracks the community, and the two change at different speeds.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = three named cases of design following a patron or market 5 + the design-versus-technique distinction stated explicitly 3 + a modified acceptance rather than a flat yes or no, matching the directive *examine* 2.** An answer that treats patronage influence as a loss of authenticity loses the final two marks.

## Original 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Prompt.** *Discuss the ecological dimension of Indian handicrafts, distinguishing it from generalised appeals to sustainability. (150 words)*

**Answer, within the 150-word ceiling.**

Every craft is a dependence on a substance, and the ecological question is about that dependence rather than about virtue.

**Named dependencies.** Kondapalli toys require soft **ponki wood**; Channapatna toys **ivory wood**; Kashmiri joinery **walnut and deodar**; Rajasthani decorative work **rohida**; Nagaland's carving **kumisyng**, a wood local to the eastern states. The Kani shawl requires **pashm from the underbelly of wild Tibetan and related goats**. Bronze requires **copper and tin**; Bidri a **zinc-and-copper alloy**.

**The regulated case.** Ivory carving has a full documented history - Harappan exports, a second-century-BC ivory guild at Vidisha, Mughal workshops, centres at Delhi, Jaipur and Murshidabad - and no contemporary production case, because its material is protected. The correct response is documentation and museum conservation, not revival.

**The distinction.** A sustainability claim is unfalsifiable; a **material dependency** is nameable, checkable and can be shown to be secure, scarce or legally closed.

**Scoring note:** **10 marks = at least four named material dependencies, each tied to its craft 4 + the regulated-material case argued to its correct conclusion 4 + the explicit contrast between a nameable dependency and an unfalsifiable sustainability claim 2.** The phrase "eco-friendly traditional art", used without a named material, forfeits the final two marks.

## Original 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *"A craft is a production system, not a product." Examine this statement with reference to how Indian craft production has been organised. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

A product can be described; a production system must be analysed. The Indian record supports the second reading.

**Four historical forms of organisation.** **Household production**, in which the household owns the material and custom sets the design - the bamboo **Moshti** grain container of the Gamit, Vasava and Dholiya Patel communities, the mud-and-dung-plastered **Porasi** of the Nayaka, and the **Kothi** grain room of the Rabari of Kutch. The **guild**, attested by a Sanchi inscription of the second century BC recording an **ivory guild at Vidisha**. The **karkhana**, a supervised workshop producing for a patron - Mughal glassware, **Himroo** for royal dress. **Putting-out, or dadni**, in which a merchant advanced material or money to household producers and took the finished goods for distant markets.

**What varies across them is control, not skill.** Ownership of raw material, authorship of design and bearing of market risk shift between household, guild, patron and merchant. The technique does not move.

**Why this matters.** The margin in a craft chain attaches to control of **credit, design and market information**, not to control of skill - which is why the most skilled stage is frequently the least well paid, and why the preparatory stages of **ginning** and **spinning**, having no separate product, have no independent price at all.

**Consequence.** Policy that treats a craft as a product to be certified addresses the last two links of the chain. Policy that treats it as a production system must address material, credit, design authorship and market access.

**Scoring note:** **15 marks = the four organisational forms named with a recorded instance each 6 + the control-not-skill observation stated as the variable that changes 4 + the preparatory-stage pricing argument 3 + a policy consequence that follows from the analysis rather than being appended 2.** An answer listing crafts rather than forms of organisation cannot exceed five.

## Original 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *Explain why the description of living folk and tribal traditions demands a different discipline from the description of court crafts, and illustrate the risks of failing to observe it. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

A court craft is described from **records**; a living tradition is described from **people who can be misrepresented**. The evidential difference produces a difference in discipline.

**Court crafts come with named patrons and datable objects.** Himroo was used for royal dress in medieval Muslim courts and is made at Aurangabad; Chamba Rumals were embroidered under the patronage of the rulers, show Pahari painting influence, carry Krishna themes and were often given as wedding gifts; Mughal glassware survives in the Sheesh Mahal, in hukkas and in ittardans. Each statement is anchored to a patron, an object or a place.

**Living traditions come with named communities and present-tense practice.** The Garo, Khasi and Jaintia are skilled weavers where eri silk is commonly produced; Mizo women are primarily responsible for weaving the Puan; each Nagaland tribe has unique patterns and motifs which reflect their cultural identity; Chapchar Kut is celebrated in Mizoram in March after completion of jhum operations.

**Three risks follow from failing to observe the difference.** Writing in the **past tense** asserts that a living practice has ended. Supplying **symbolism** - that a stripe or an arrow "represents" something - invents evidence about people who exist. Asserting **unchanging continuity** denies a community its history and cannot be supported by any record here.

**The discipline.** Name the community; name the anchor - an operation, a dated event, an implement; use the present tense; and where the record is silent, be silent.

**Scoring note:** **15 marks = the evidential contrast between record-anchored and people-anchored description 4 + two court cases and three living cases, all correctly attributed 6 + the three named risks 3 + the four-part discipline stated as instructions 2.** Any invented symbolic meaning cancels the corresponding attribution's marks.

## Original 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *"Indian handicrafts show both the power and the limits of understanding culture through its materials." Critically examine. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**The power.** The standard classification of Indian handicrafts is by material - glassware, cloth, ivory, silver, clay and pottery, bronze, other metals, leather, wood, toys, stoneware and floor designs - and this makes three things visible that a regional list conceals. It exposes **dependence**: ponki wood, walnut and deodar, pashm from the underbelly of wild Tibetan goats, copper and tin, zinc and copper. It groups **technique families** across regions: lost-wax casting, supplementary weft, yarn resist, inlay. And it resists **one-state exclusivity**, since ikat is recorded for four states and the Kolhapuri chappal for two.

**The limits are three, and each is serious.** First, **material does not explain practice**: cotton grows very widely and painted cloth does not, so feasibility is not performance. Second, **material misses the holder of the knowledge**: the Aranmula mirror is an alloy, but what is at risk is a composition held within the families involved, and that is a fact about kinship, not about metal. Third, **material cannot see the untraded**: floor designs are one of the twelve classes, yet they are drawn freehand in erasable chalk, lime, powdered marble or rice-lime mixtures for family occasions - a craft with no product, no market and no buyer, invisible to any material economics.

**Verdict.** A material classification is the best available **spine** for an answer and the worst available **explanation** of a craft's survival. The material tells you what is possible; community, market and transmission tell you what actually happens.

**Scoring note:** **20 marks = the twelve-class spine and its three analytical advantages, each illustrated 7 + three distinct limits, each with a named case rather than an assertion 8 + the feasibility-versus-performance formulation 3 + a verdict that separates spine from explanation 2.** An answer that only praises the material classification, however fully, cannot exceed nine.

## Original 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Prompt.** *Assess the place of North-Eastern and tribal India in the map of Indian crafts, and examine the cautions that such an assessment demands. (250 words)*

**Answer, within the 250-word ceiling.**

**The place is central, and unusually well specified.** The record attributes technique to named groups more consistently here than anywhere else. In **Meghalaya** the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** are skilled weavers, **eri silk is commonly produced**, and shawls, sarongs and mekhela chadors are woven from **both silk and cotton**. **Assam** is known for **golden muga silk**. **Manipur** holds **Moirang Phee**, a silk-and-cotton fabric, and the **Phanek**. **Nagaland**'s tribes each hold **unique patterns and motifs reflecting cultural identity**, using **natural dyes**. In **Arunachal Pradesh** the **Adi, Apatani and Nyishi** are named for intricate weaves and the **Monpa** for vibrant colours. In **Mizoram**, **women are primarily responsible** for weaving the **Puan**. In Gujarat, tribal storage technology is recorded with equal precision - **Moshti**, **Porasi** and **Kothi**, with the Gamit, Vasava, Dholiya Patel, Nayaka and Rabari communities named.

**Four cautions.** **One:** the **mekhela chador** is woven in two states and identifies neither; the discriminating facts are fibre and community. **Two:** these are **present-tense practices**, and the past tense asserts an ending the record does not support. **Three:** motifs are recorded as reflecting **identity**, and any further symbolism is invented. **Four:** the technique-holding unit is a **community**, not a region, so a region-defined legal instrument fits imperfectly.

**Assessment.** The region supplies this topic's strongest evidence for community-held technique - and therefore its strongest argument that transmission, not nomenclature, is what preserves a craft.

**Scoring note:** **20 marks = at least six state entries with their correct communities, fibres or products 8 + the tribal storage-technology record as the non-textile instance 3 + the four cautions, each stated as an instruction 6 + a closing assessment that draws the community-versus-region consequence 3.** An answer describing the region as "rich in weaving traditions" without named communities cannot exceed six.

# REMEDIATION

Sixteen recurring errors, each with the correction, the reason the error is attractive, and the drill that fixes it.

## Error set A - technique and terminology

**Error 1: calling a brocade an embroidery.**
*Why it is attractive:* both use metallic thread on fine cloth, and the source itself places a brocade description inside its embroidery section.
*Correction:* a **brocade** is built on the loom by a **supplementary weft** carried across only where the pattern falls; an **embroidery** is **raised design worked with sewing needles into finished cloth**. Banaras brocade is generally on silk sarees with silver and golden zari and floral motifs and takes **two to eight weeks** per piece; Chikankari is white thread on cotton, mulmul, polyester or voile at Lucknow.
*Drill:* before naming any textile, answer one question - was the pattern decided **before**, **during** or **after** weaving?

**Error 2: treating bandhani and ikat as the same operation.**
*Why it is attractive:* the source calls both tie-and-dye methods.
*Correction:* **bandhani** is applied to the **finished cloth** by repeated knotting and dyeing; **ikat** is applied to the **yarn** before weaving. A warp and a weft only exist as yarn, so a tradition described as resist-dyed in warp and weft - **Patola of Patan** - is necessarily yarn resist.
*Drill:* ask where the resist sits. On cloth, or on thread?

**Error 3: asserting that Pochampalli is double ikat.**
*Why it is attractive:* it is the best-known Indian ikat after Patola, and the symmetry feels right.
*Correction:* the record places Pochampalli at **Bhoodan Pochampally, Telangana**, as **silk and cotton sarees with an ikat style of dyeing**, and does not state the degree. **Patola of Patan** is the recorded double case.
*Drill:* state only what the record states, and mark the difference between a family placement and a degree.

**Error 4: merging Bidri, Koftgiri, Tarkashi and Marori.**
*Why it is attractive:* all four give a bright pattern on a dark ground.
*Correction:* **Bidri** is **silver** in a **blackened zinc-and-copper alloy**, **Bidar, Karnataka**. **Koftgiri** is **light metal on a dark surface**, Jaipur and Alwar. **Tarkashi** is **copper or brass wire in chiselled grooves**, Rajasthan. **Marori** is **black lac filling an etched void**, Rajasthan.
*Drill:* ask what the pattern is **physically made of**. The ground is never diagnostic.

**Error 5: confusing the two *rumals*, or Kashida with Kani.**
*Why it is attractive:* each pair shares a word or a region.
*Correction:* **Chamba Rumal** is an **embroidered handkerchief** of Himachal Pradesh with Pahari influence and Krishna themes; **Telia Rumal** is a **resist-dyed textile** historically associated with **Chirala, Andhra Pradesh**, its name glossed as *oily*, and registered as **Puttapaka Telia Rumal** for **Puttapaka, Telangana**. **Kashida** is **embroidery worked on** Cashmere shawls; **Kani** is a **woven** shawl of pashm.
*Drill:* for every shared word, decide whether it names a **place**, an **operation** or an **object type**.

## Error set B - chronology

**Error 6: dating a living practice from an excavated material.**
*Why it is attractive:* the antiquity chains in this topic are genuine and impressive.
*Correction:* glass beads in the **Painted Grey Ware culture around 1000 BC** and a glass industry at **Brahmapuri and Kolhapur between the 2nd century BC and the 2nd century AD** date a **material and a technique**. Firozabad's chandeliers date a **present practice**. Firozabad appears in both lists and the two facts still do not join.
*Drill:* write the number, then write the noun it belongs to - material, technique, institution, patronage, practice or status.

**Error 7: supplying a registration year.**
*Why it is attractive:* every registered craft feels as though it must have a well-known year.
*Correction:* the only registration year asserted in this session is **Darjeeling Tea, 2004-05**, India's first. For every other registered craft encountered here, no year is recorded and none is supplied.
*Drill:* before writing a year beside a registered craft, ask which source supplied it. If you cannot name one, delete it.

**Error 8: dating a tradition from the event it commemorates.**
*Why it is attractive:* the tradition and the event share a name.
*Correction:* the **Battle of Khongjom** is dated **1891**. The ballad's own date is unrecorded.
*Drill:* separate the subject's date from the work's date every time both appear in one sentence.

**Error 9: deleting the source's hedge.**
*Why it is attractive:* a hedge reads as weakness.
*Correction:* sericulture and wild silk at Harappa and Chanhudaro are **suggested**; Indus weaving and dyeing was **perhaps** well established; Kasuti **possibly** dates to the Chalukyan era. The hedge is part of the fact.
*Drill:* scan your own sentence for a qualifier you have removed; in this topic they are load-bearing.

## Error set C - geography and attribution

**Error 10: one-state exclusivity.**
*Why it is attractive:* matching items train the habit, and a single state is easier to remember.
*Correction:* **ikat** - Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh. **Bandhani** - Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu. **Phulkari** - practised in Punjab, Haryana and Delhi; registered for Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan. **Kolhapuri chappal** - Karnataka and Maharashtra. **Bohra cap weaving** - Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh. **Karma Naach** - many tribes of Eastern India, especially the Chota Nagpur Plateau.
*Drill:* whenever you attach a single state, ask whether the record names another.

**Error 11: using an item that identifies nothing as though it identified something.**
*Why it is attractive:* the word sounds specific.
*Correction:* the **mekhela chador** is woven in Assam and Meghalaya; *rumal* names an object type; *ikat*, *brocade*, *batik*, *applique*, *filigree* and *repousse* name operations; a dark ground with a bright pattern fits at least three metal crafts.
*Drill:* classify the word before you use it - place, operation, or object type.

**Error 12: fabricating symbolism.**
*Why it is attractive:* it feels respectful and fills space.
*Correction:* the record says Nagaland's motifs **reflect cultural identity** and that the Puan carries **needlework in the shape of a stripe or an arrow**. It supplies no meanings, and neither should an answer.
*Drill:* underline every sentence in your answer that states what something "represents" or "symbolises", and delete any you cannot attribute.

**Error 13: freezing a community.**
*Why it is attractive:* "unchanged for centuries" sounds like praise.
*Correction:* the record describes living traditions in the **present tense** and records change - natural dyes here, vibrant colours there, both silk and cotton in one place, different attributes for different communities within one state.
*Drill:* check the tense of every verb attached to a named community.

## Error set D - the instruments

**Error 14: treating a Geographical Indication as a quality certificate, a heritage tag or a permanent status.**
*Why it is attractive:* all official recognitions feel alike.
*Correction:* it is a **sign that an item originated from a particular region only**, restricting the **product name** to **registered authorised users**; it covers **goods only**, for **ten years, renewable**, in **34 classes**, under the **1999 Act in force from 2003**, administered by the **GI Registry at Chennai** under **DPIIT, Ministry of Commerce and Industry**.
*Drill:* for any recognition claim, name the **authority**, the **instrument**, the **object** protected and the **duration**.

**Error 15: assuming a registration travels, or reaches a design.**
*Why it is attractive:* a right feels like a general shield.
*Correction:* **Kangra Tea** required **separate registration in the European Union**, which is the proof that a domestic right is domestic. And because the right attaches to the **name**, a producer outside the region may lawfully make a similar article under a different name.
*Drill:* state what the right is a right **against**, not merely what it is a right **to**.

**Error 16: asserting an outcome that nobody measures.**
*Why it is attractive:* an answer feels incomplete without a figure.
*Correction:* asked in Parliament for the quantum of exports of GI-tagged products over five years, the answering ministry stated on **7 August 2026** that **there is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged product**. A five-year registration list is also not a cumulative national total.
*Drill:* for every number you are about to write, name its source and its date; if you cannot, write the absence instead, with attribution.

## Drill sets

**Drill set A - name the moment.** For each, state whether the pattern was decided before the loom, on the loom, or after it, and name the family: a metallic floral figure on silk; soft-edged geometry identical on both faces; small reserved white dots across a coloured field; white thread flowers on voile; a drawn narrative in vegetable dye on cotton; cut fabric pieces sewn onto a ground; a raised metallic pattern over cotton stuffing; tiny mirrors held by herringbone and satin stitch.

**Drill set B - what is the pattern made of.** For each, name the material of the pattern and the craft: silver in a blackened alloy; light metal on a dark surface; copper or brass wire in chiselled grooves; black lac in an etched void; a cut line with nothing added; displaced sheet metal raised from behind.

**Drill set C - place the fibre and the community.** For each, state the fibre, the community where recorded, and the garment: golden silk of Assam; silk commonly produced in Meghalaya; the wraparound skirt of Manipuri women; the cloth decorated with a stripe or an arrow in Mizoram; the shawl mainly woven by the Vankar of Kutch; the shawl of the Toda of the Nilgiris; the delicate fibre from the underbelly of wild Tibetan goats.

**Drill set D - name the date type.** For each, state which of the six clocks the date belongs to: 1000 BC and glass beads; 2500 BC and a bronze figure; the 2nd century BC and an ivory guild; 3000 BC and tanning; 1999 and a statute; 2003 and the same statute; 2004-05 and a tea; 1891 and a battle; 1925 and an association; 1956 and a commission; 2022 and a list of fifty textiles; 3 November 2025 and a fee.

**Drill set E - four constructions.** For each, state whether the fibre was spun, whether a loom was used, and whether there is a raised surface: namda; a Navalgund durrie; a panja durrie; a kaleen; a khabdan; a musallah rug.

**Drill set F - which instrument, and what does it reach.** For each failure, name the responsive instrument or condition and the link of the chain it touches: a competitor selling goods under the craft's place name; buyers unable to verify that a fabric meets a published specification; a technique described nowhere in writing; an alloy composition held within one family; a raw material under legal protection; a producer unable to afford litigation; a preparatory stage performed without payment.

**Drill set G - state the boundary.** For each demand, say which owner it belongs to and what this topic supplies: the decline of Indian handicrafts under colonial rule; the iconography of Chola bronzes; the performance grammar of the Chhau dance; the ware sequence of a Harappan pottery type; the correlation between cultural diversity and socio-economic marginality; the plan and meaning of a Mughal tomb; bamboo as a minor forest produce; the administration of international cultural recognition.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 - the complete craft chain, with the instruments placed exactly

```text
 [1] RAW MATERIAL
     cotton | mulberry, muga, eri, wild silk | wool, pashm | bast and leaf
     clay | quartz-and-glass batch | zinc, copper, tin, brass, silver
     walnut, deodar, sandalwood, raktachandan, teak, kumisyng, rohida,
       ponki, ivory wood | hide (camel, and others) | paper pulp | lac
     RISK: regulated species (IVORY), depletion, price set elsewhere
         |
 [2] PREPARATION
     GINNING -> SPINNING | scouring | tanning | clay levigation |
     alloy preparation | timber seasoning | pulp making
     RISK: uncounted and unpaid; no separate product = no separate price
     THE COUNTER-CASE: KHADI is DEFINED by this stage (hand-spun);
       All India Spinners' Association 1925; KVIC 1956
         |
 [3] SKILL / TECHNIQUE
     WEAVE: plain | supplementary weft (Banaras brocade, Tanchoi,
       Jamdani of Bengal / Awadh Jamdani / Uppada Jamdani) | Mashru |
       Khes double weave | Pata | Bohra cap | Patku
     RESIST: cloth (bandhani, laharia, batik, Thigma) |
       yarn (ikat; DOUBLE = Patola of Patan; Pochampalli;
       Telia Rumal - historic Chirala, AP; registered as
       PUTTAPAKA TELIA RUMAL, Puttapaka, Telangana)
     SURFACE: painted (Kalamkari) | printed (Ajrakh) |
       embroidered (Chikankari, Kashida, Phulkari, Bagh, Nakshi Kantha,
       Sujni, Kasuti, Shisha, Gota, Karchobi, Chamba Rumal, Manipuri
       muga-thread) | appliqued (Pipli; and in leather, the Punjabi jutti)
     NON-WOVEN: namda felt | kaleen knotted | khabdan pile |
       Navalgund and panja durrie | musallah rug
       METAL: lost-wax casting (Bastar Dhokra) | sheet work (Pembarthi,
         Telangana) | engraving (Moradabad Nakkashi/Baarik Kaam) |
         inlay (Bidri - Bidar, Karnataka,
       Koftgiri, Tarkashi) | lac fill (Marori) | alloy-for-property
       (Aranmula Kannadi) | filigree (Odisha painri, paijam, gunchi)
     CLAY: Khurja | Nizamabad black | Blue | Pokhran | Dalgate | Karigari
     WOOD: Kashmir khatamband, acche-dar, azli-pinjra | Nirmal |
       Kashtakari | sandalwood | toys (Channapatna, Kondapalli, Tirupati)
     LEATHER: Mojari | Kolhapuri | jutti | Manoti/Usta | Paabu | Konglan |
       Katki
     PAPER/FIBRE: Kashmir and Tirukanur papier mache | Chhau and
       Gambhira masks | Ganjifa | Rambaans | bamboo Moshti
     STONE: marble (Makrana, Jhansi, Agra) | Pietra Dura inlay
     RISK: TIME. Banaras brocade = 2-8 WEEKS per saree.
       Aranmula mirror = polished SEVERAL DAYS.
       Lost wax = mould broken, model gone, EVERY PIECE UNIQUE.
         |
 [4] PRODUCING COMMUNITY
     Garo, Khasi, Jaintia (Meghalaya) | Adi, Apatani, Nyishi, Monpa
     (Arunachal) | Mizo WOMEN (Mizoram) | Vankar (Kutch) | Toda
     (Nilgiris) | Gamit, Vasava, Dholiya Patel | Nayaka | Rabari (Gujarat)
     | the FAMILIES holding the Aranmula alloy
     RISK: transmission. KNOWLEDGE STORAGE decides recoverability:
       KIN (irrecoverable) > COMMUNITY (partly) > CLUSTER (while it
       lives) > WRITTEN RECORD (to the extent it is complete)
         |
 [5] MARKET ACCESS
     household | temple and court | GUILD (Vidisha ivory guild, 2nd c BC)
     | KARKHANA | PUTTING-OUT / DADNI | export merchant | cooperative |
     exhibition | tourist | online
     RISK: the margin attaches to control of CREDIT, DESIGN and MARKET
       INFORMATION, not to control of SKILL
     REACHED TODAY BY: GI Mahotsavs (25) | One District One GI (164
       programmes sanctioned) | fairs, pavilions, retail outlets
         |
 [6] LEGAL RECOGNITION  <-- THE ONLY LINK A GI TOUCHES
     GI OF GOODS (REGISTRATION & PROTECTION) ACT, 1999; IN FORCE 2003
     GI Registry, CHENNAI, under DPIIT, Ministry of Commerce and Industry
     GOODS ONLY | 10 YEARS, RENEWABLE | 34 CLASSES | prescribed fee,
       REDUCED 80% by notification dated 3 NOVEMBER 2025
     REACHED TODAY BY: registry handholding | 100% reimbursement up to
       Rs. 2 LAKH per registered GI (MSME Innovative Scheme, IPR
       component) | IP Facilitation Centres | enforcement and legal-
       expense assistance up to Rs. 1.50 LAKH from the Development
       Commissioner (HANDICRAFTS), Ministry of Textiles
         |
 [7] SAFEGUARDING
     documentation (2022 list of 50 iconic Indian textile crafts) |
     transmission support | promotion
     LARGELY THE HERITAGE OWNER'S TERRITORY

 THE EVALUATION GAP, OFFICIALLY RECORDED:
   "There is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of
    GI-tagged product."  (parliamentary reply, 7 August 2026)
```
