## Map 5 - the argument map for a craft-policy answer

```text
   CLAIM: "Indian crafts are in difficulty."
                    |
       REJECT AS UNMARKABLE UNTIL SPECIFIED
                    |
   RUN THE FIVE-QUESTION DIAGNOSTIC
                    |
   1. Is the MATERIAL available and affordable?
        NO -> ecological/regulatory problem
              -> substitution | sustainable sourcing | (if protected)
                 documentation and museum conservation, NOT revival
              -> evidence: ivory carving
   2. Is the PREPARATORY labour paid?
        NO -> intra-household distribution problem
              -> no product-level instrument reaches it
              -> counter-case: KHADI defines the product BY the stage
   3. Is the SKILL being transmitted?
        NO -> ask WHY. Usually income against apprenticeship length.
              -> evidence: 2-8 weeks per brocade saree; several days'
                 polishing per Aranmula mirror
              -> check KNOWLEDGE STORAGE: kin / community / cluster /
                 record. Kin-held = irreversible.
   4. Can the PRODUCER REACH A BUYER without surrendering design
      and margin?
        NO -> organisational problem
              -> cooperative | direct retail | procurement | exhibition
              -> present measures: 25 GI Mahotsavs; 164 One District
                 One GI programmes; fairs, pavilions, retail outlets
   5. Is the NAME being used by NON-MAKERS?
        YES -> and ONLY here -> GEOGRAPHICAL INDICATION
              -> plus enforcement assistance, because an unenforceable
                 right is nominal
                    |
   WRITE THE VERDICT AS: "the [n]th link is failing because [reason];
   the instrument that reaches that link is [x]; the instrument most
   often proposed reaches link 6 only."
```

## Map 6 - master comparison of the six recurring distinctions

| # | The pair | What actually separates them | The case that settles it |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Weave** and **surface decoration** | the moment the pattern is decided - during or after weaving | Banaras brocade against Chikankari |
| 2 | **Cloth resist** and **yarn resist** | whether the resist sits on fabric or on thread | bandhani against Patola |
| 3 | **Painted cloth** and **painting style** | whether the support is cloth and the chemistry is mordant-and-dye-bath | Kalamkari against any paper or wall tradition |
| 4 | **Felt, flat weave, knotted, pile** | was the fibre spun, and is there a raised surface | namda, durrie, kaleen, khabdan |
| 5 | **Documentation, certification, registration** | describes, tests, or confers an exclusive right to a name | the 2022 textile list, a product-safety standard, the GI Act |
| 6 | **Region-held and community-held technique** | whether the technique-holding unit coincides with an administrative boundary | the mekhela chador, woven in two states by different communities |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Scope, vocabulary and the six-field record

- Handicrafts are **an amalgamation of all things crafted by hand**, serving as **both a means of livelihood and cultural heritage**, especially for **tribal and rural communities**.
- Every craft claim decomposes into **material, technique, community and region, function and market, status, age-class**. Value is a seventh column and carries no marks.
- **Embroidery**: creating **raised designs** using **threads of gold, silver, silk or cotton** on cloth with **sewing needles**. **Applique**: **patchwork of embroidered colourful fabric sewn together into a single piece**.
- **Warp** runs the cloth's length under tension; **weft** crosses it. **Supplementary weft** is an extra weft carried across only where the pattern falls. **Mordant** fixes dye to fibre; **resist** prevents dye reaching the material. **Lost-wax casting** melts out a wax model and replaces it with metal. **Inlay** sets one material into a groove cut in another; **engraving** cuts a line and adds nothing; **repousse** raises relief by pressing from behind.
- **Karkhana** = supervised workshop; **putting-out (dadni)** = merchant-advanced household production.
- **Geographical Indication**: a **sign that an item has originated from a particular region only**, ensuring that **only registered authorised users** may use the product name.

## The material classification, used as an answer spine

Glassware · cloth handicrafts · ivory carving · silver crafts · clay and pottery work · bronze crafts · other metal crafts · leather products · wooden work · various types of toys · stoneware · floor designs. A material spine exposes **dependence**, groups **technique families** and resists **one-state exclusivity**; a state list does none of these.

## The chain, and where the instruments sit

Raw material → preparation → skill → producing community → market access → legal recognition → safeguarding. A **Geographical Indication touches link 6 only**. The margin attaches to control of **credit, design and market information**, not to control of skill. The **five-question diagnostic** replaces the decline narrative: material available? preparation paid? skill transmitted? buyer reachable without losing design and margin? name misused?

## Age-classes and the dating discipline

- Four classes: **excavated artefact**, **court or classical craft**, **living folk and tribal tradition**, **contemporary design**. They are four concurrent **kinds of evidence**, not four periods.
- Six clocks: **material, technique, institution, patronage, practice, status**. State the number, then its noun.
- Hedges are load-bearing: sericulture is **suggested**; Indus weaving and dyeing was **perhaps** established; Kasuti is **possibly** Chalukyan.

## Fibre and the textile sequence

- The sequence: **ginning, spinning, weaving**. Twist is what spinning adds; no twist, no warp, no cloth.
- **Cotton**: early cultivation and textiles in the Indus Valley; fabrics mentioned in the **Rigveda**; absorbent and mordant-friendly, hence the ground of painted and printed traditions.
- **Silk**: sericulture and **wild silk** *suggested* at **Harappa and Chanhudaro**, **3rd millennium BC**. **Muga = Assam, golden**. **Eri = commonly produced in Meghalaya**, where the **Garo, Khasi and Jaintia** are skilled weavers using **both silk and cotton**. The **mekhela chador identifies no state**, being woven in both.
- **Composites**: **Moirang Phee** (Manipur, silk and cotton); **Maheswari** (Madhya Pradesh, cotton-and-silk yarns, golden zari, **reversible borders**).
- **Wool and pashm**: **Kani shawl** from delicate **pashm** from the **underbelly of wild Tibetan** and related goats; **Thigma**, wool tie and dye of **Ladakh**, mainly **Nubra**.
- **Khadi**: from **Khaddar**, **hand spun cotton fabric** of India and Bangladesh; rugged texture; warm in winter, cool in summer; **All India Spinners' Association 1925**, after the Non-Cooperation Movement; Gandhian symbol of **self-reliance, self-sufficiency and resistance against British textiles**; **KVIC, 1956**, promoting it as a mark of **identity, empowerment and transformation**.

## Weaves

- **Jamdani**: **opaque designs on a transparent base**; recorded for **Bengal**, as **Awadh Jamdani** at **Varanasi**, and as **Uppada Jamdani** at **Uppada, Andhra Pradesh**.
- **Banaras Brocade**: **silver and golden zari**, **floral motifs**, generally **silk**, **two to eight weeks** per saree.
- **Tanchoi**: came **via trade**, inspired by **Chinese weaving**, **perfected at Varanasi**, **resembling miniature paintings**.
- **Pochampalli**: **Bhoodan Pochampally, Telangana**; **silk and cotton** sarees; **ikat style of dyeing**; degree not stated.
- **Patan Patola**: **Patan, Gujarat**; **rich handloom silk** sarees; resist-dyed in **both warp and weft**.
- **Baluchari**: **Murshidabad, West Bengal**; sarees that **depict ancient stories**.
- **Himroo**: **Aurangabad, Maharashtra**; medieval **royal dress and shawls**; still popular.
- **Pata** (Bastar, Chhattisgarh) · **Mashru** (Gujarat, handwoven mix) · **Bohra cap** (Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh, crochet from the core outwards) · **Patku** (Gujarat, tie-dyed) · **Khes** (Panipat, Haryana, double-woven, chequered) · **Puan** (Mizoram, needlework stripe or arrow, worn by both sexes, woven primarily by **women**) · **Phanek** (Manipur, wraparound skirt).

## Resist

- **Bandhani / Bandhej**: **repeated knotting and dyeing**; **Rajasthan, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu**.
- **Laharia**: **ripples or wave-like patterns**; **Jaipur and Jodhpur**.
- **Ikat**: **resist dyeing** applied to **yarn**; major centres **Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh**.
- **Telia Rumal**: resist-dyed, name glossed as **oily**; historic home **Chirala, Andhra Pradesh**; the **registered** indication is **Puttapaka Telia Rumal**, application **no. 599**, **Puttapaka, Nalgonda district, Telangana**, with **no year asserted**. **Paagadu Bandhu (Chitki)**: Andhra Pradesh, bindings applied to the fabric.
- **Batik**: **molten wax** then **cold wax** dyeing; **multicoloured sarees and dupattas**; **Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal**; **originated in Indonesia**.
- Physical tell: yarn resist gives **feathered edges and a matching reverse face**; cloth resist gives **crisp dots, rings or diagonal bands**; printing matches on one face only.

## Painted and printed cloth

- **Kalamkari**: **hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes with deep colours**, **commonly practised in Andhra Pradesh**; the wider record adds hand-drawn **or block-printed** cotton with **mordant and resist** processes at Andhra Pradesh and Telangana centres. Individual centre-styles are **not** supplied here.
- **Ajrakh printing**: **Gujarat**, a **block-printed textile**.
- The mordant mechanism: the artisan applies the **mordant** in the shape of the design; the **dye bath** supplies the colour; **different mordants under one dye give different colours**. Hence a cotton ground, a long water-intensive process, planning in treatments rather than colours, and colour **inside** the fibre.

## Embroidery

- **Chikankari**: **Lucknow**; **white thread**; grounds **cotton, mulmul ka cotton, polyester, voile**.
- **Kashida**: **Kashmir**; generally on **Cashmere shawls**.
- **Phulkari**: **making flowers**; **darning**; practised in **Punjab, Haryana and Delhi**, registered for **Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan**. **Bagh**: **Punjab**; **silk threads** on **green cloth**.
- **Nakshi Kantha**: **West Bengal**; thread drawn from **used cloth**; reuse is intrinsic. **Sujni**: **Bihar**, associated with **Muzaffarpur**.
- **Kasuti**: **Karnataka**; **cross stitch**; **geometrical motifs**; **possibly** Chalukyan-era.
- **Shisha**: **Kutch, Gujarat**; **tiny mirrors** fixed with **herringbone and satin stitch**.
- **Gota** (Rajasthan, gold thread, applique-like) · **Karchobi** (Rajasthan, flat stitches over **cotton stuffing**) · **Khandela and Jaipur** (zari-work places).
- **Chamba Rumal**: **Himachal Pradesh**; embroidered **handkerchief**; **Pahari** influence; **Krishna themes and nature**; **wedding gifts**; royal patronage.
- **Manipuri muga-thread embroidery**: dark colours on **phanek borders**, for weddings and auspicious occasions.
- **Applique / Pipli work**: **Pipli village, Odisha**; **used to make lamps**.

## Shawls, rugs and floor coverings

- **Kani** (registered, Jammu and Kashmir, **pashm**, woven) · **Tangaliya** (registered, Gujarat, **dotted hand-woven**) · **Kachchh shawl** (registered, Gujarat, **mainly woven by the Vankar** of Kutch) · **Puthukkuli** (shawl of the **Toda** of the **Nilgiris, Tamil Nadu**).
- **Namda felted rugs** - **Gujarat**; felting skips spinning and weaving entirely.
- **Navalgund Durrie** - **Karnataka**; **Panja Durrie** - **Punjab, Rajasthan**, named from the claw-like beating tool; **Musallah Rug** - **Andhra Pradesh**.
- **Kaleen: knotted carpets** - **Jammu and Kashmir**; **knot density** measures fineness, labour and price. **Khabdan: pile carpets** - **Jammu and Kashmir**, listed separately.

## North-East handloom

Framing: **each tribe has an expertise in weaving**; techniques are **ginning, spinning and weaving**.

| State | The record |
|---|---|
| **Assam** | **golden Muga silk**; sarees, **mekhela chadors**, other garments |
| **Meghalaya** | **Garo, Khasi, Jaintia** skilled weavers; **eri silk commonly produced**; **shawls, sarongs, mekhela chadors**; **both silk and cotton** |
| **Manipur** | **Moirang Phee** (silk and cotton); **Phanek** (wraparound skirt, vibrant colours, traditional motifs) |
| **Nagaland** | **each tribe's unique patterns and motifs reflect cultural identity**; vibrant colours, intricate designs, **natural dyes** |
| **Arunachal Pradesh** | **Adi, Apatani, Nyishi** for intricate weaves; **Monpa** for vibrant colours; jackets, skirts, shawls, dresses |
| **Mizoram** | **women primarily responsible** for weaving; the **Puan**, cloth with **needlework in the shape of a stripe or an arrow**, worn by **both men and women** |
| **Tripura** | **vertical and horizontal stripes with dispersed embroidery** |

## Documentation, certification and registration

- **2022**: a list of **50 exclusive and iconic heritage textile crafts of India**, published as **Handmade for the 21st Century: Safeguarding Traditional Indian Textile**, addressing a stated **lack of proper inventory and documentation** in South Asia; it lists **histories, process of production, popularity and preservation**. Four entries printed here: **Khes** (Panipat, Haryana), **Chamba Rumal** (Himachal Pradesh), **Thigma** (Ladakh, Nubra), **Awadh Jamdani** (Varanasi).
- **Voluntary product-safety certification**: run by an **international association of independent textile research and testing institutes**; tests articles for **harmful substances** with limits graded by **intended use and degree of skin contact**; **periodically renewed**; **voluntary, private, silent on origin**, not an intellectual-property right.
- The one-line test: documentation answers *what is this and how is it made*; certification answers *does this sample meet a published specification*; registration answers *who may lawfully use this name*.

## Clay, glass and stone

- **Pottery centres**: **Khurja** (Bulandshahr, UP, colourful household ware) · **Nizamabad Black** (Azamgarh, UP, dark and patterned) · **Blue Pottery** (Jaipur) · **Pokhran** (Rajasthan) · **Dalgate** (Jammu and Kashmir) · **Karigari** (Tamil Nadu). Identified by **finish, palette and use**, not shape. Ware-sequence archaeology routes to the sculpture-and-pottery owner.
- **Tribal storage technology, Gujarat**: **Moshti**, a **bamboo** grain container of the **Gamit, Vasava and Dholiya Patel**; **Porasi**, plastered with **mud and cow dung** against **spillage and pilferage**, of the **Nayaka** of **Godhara and Panchmahal**; **Kothi**, a **room** with a **large door** for pouring in and a **small bottom outlet**, in **Kutch**, especially among the **Rabari**.
- **Glass antiquity**: **Mahabharata** reference; **Painted Grey Ware, Ganges Valley, 1000 BC**; **Shatapatha Brahmana**'s **kanch/kaca**; industry at **Brahmapuri and Kolhapur, 2nd c BC-2nd c AD**, **lenticular beads**; **Vyasayogacharita** on early spectacles; **Maski** (Karnataka, Chalcolithic); remains at **Ahar, Hastinapur, Ahichchhatra, Eran, Ujjain, Firozabad**. **Mughal**: **Sheesh Mahal**, **hukkas**, **ittardans**, engraved glasses. **Present**: bangles (Hyderabad's **churi ka jodas**), chandeliers (**Firozabad**), toys (**Saharanpur**), **Tikuli beads** (**Patna**). Glass is the only **fully reworkable** material here.
- **Stone**: Mauryan facades; Ajanta, Ellora, Khajuraho, Sanchi, Bharhut; **Masrur** rock-cut temples, **Kangra district**, **early 8th century AD**; Mughal shift to **marble** with **Pietra Dura inlay**; **Taj Mahal** and **I'tmad-ud-Daulah's tomb** in white marble. **Marble centres**: **Makrana / Sang-e-Marmar** (Rajasthan), **Sange-rathek** (Jhansi), **Agra**.

## Metal and silver

- **Bronze** = **copper + tin**, the earliest non-ferrous metals used; anchor is the **Dancing Girl, Mohenjo-daro, c. 2500 BC**. **Matsya Purana** on **casting methods**; **Rasaratnakara** on **metal purity and zinc distillation**.
- **Bronze centres**: **Uttar Pradesh takes precedence** - **Etawah, Sitapur, Varanasi, Moradabad**; **tamrapatra, kanchantal, panchpatra**. **Tamil Nadu**: statues inspired by **Pallava, Chola, Pandya, Nayaka** art.
- **Lost wax**: model in wax, encased, melted out, metal poured, **mould broken** - so **every piece is unique** and **failure is total**. **Bastar Dhokra** (registered) is used for **brass ornaments**.
- **Inlay and engraving family**, separated by what the pattern is made of: **Bidri** = **silver** in **blackened zinc-and-copper alloy**, **Bidar, Karnataka**; **Koftgiri** = **light metal on a dark surface**, Jaipur and Alwar; **Tarkashi** = **copper or brass wire in chiselled grooves**, Rajasthan; **Marori** = **black lac** in an etched void, Rajasthan; **Nakkashi / Baarik Kaam** = a **cut line**, Moradabad; **repousse** = **displaced metal**.
- **Pembarthi** (registered) = **sheet brass** for **chariots and temples**, **Pembarthi, Telangana**. **Pahaldar lamps** = copper and brass. **Badla** = a **zinc** pot of the **Marwar** region.
- **Aranmula Kannadi** (registered, Kerala) = **handmade metal-alloy mirror**, **polished for several days**, **alloy composition held within the families involved**.
- **Silver**: **filigree**; Odisha's **painri** and **paijam** anklets; knitted silver **gunchi**.

## Wood, bamboo, cane, toys, leather, paper and fibre

- **Kashmir**: largest wooden-furniture centre, flourishing **since the 11th century**; **walnut and deodar**; houses, **houseboats**; **acche-dar, khatamband, azli-pinjra**. **Khatamband** = wooden pieces **joined**. **Gujarat**: latticework. **Himachal Pradesh**: **Bharmour and Chhatrahi** - **naghbel, kutheriphool, jali, dori**.
- **Timbers**: **sandalwood** (Karnataka) · **raktachandan** (Andhra Pradesh, carved dolls) · **teakwood** (Kerala) · **kumisyng** (Nagaland) · **rohida** (Rajasthan).
- **Named wood crafts**: **Nirmal** (registered, Telangana, varnished) · **Kashtakari** (Goa) · **Santalum** (Karnataka) · **Khatamband** (Jammu and Kashmir).
- **Bamboo** = the **hollow, noded culm** of a woody grass: cross-cut, **split into slivers**, woven, lashed. Owned case: the **Moshti** grain container of southern Gujarat. **Cane (rattan)** = the **solid stem of a climbing palm**: soaked or steamed, **bent into frames**, **peeled into binding strips**, woven into seat panels. **No named cane craft is held; the gap is declared.** Rule: straight members and split weaving point to bamboo, curved frames and bound joints to cane.
- **Toys**: Harappan **toy carts** in copper, bronze, terracotta at **Alamgirpur, Ambkheri, Kalibangan, Lothal**; early historic at **Atranjikhera, Nagarjunakonda, Sambhar, Sonapur, Ujjain, Brahmpuri**; historic materials clay, paper, **papier-mache**, **lacquered wood**, **Tirupati dolls** from **redwood**; **Rajasthan** cloth dolls, **Assam** pith dolls, **Mysore and Channapatna** lacquered toys, **Kondapalli** **Ambari Hathi** from **ponki** wood. **Channapatna Toys** (registered, Karnataka) from **ivory wood** - a **timber**. **Kondapalli Bommalu** (registered, Andhra Pradesh) - soft wood, **moisture removed**, **parts carved separately then joined**.
- **Leather**: tanning from **3000 BC**, first **tigers and deer**, later **camels**. **Rajasthan** largest market, **camel leather** bags; **Mojaris** at Jaipur and Jodhpur; **Kanpur** tanned products; **Kolhapuri chappals** in Maharashtra; **Chennai and Kolkata** bags and footwear; **Punjabi juttis** in **applique**; **Manoti / Usta** art at **Bikaner** with **camel skin**, from Persian **Ustad**. Footwear: **Konglan** (West Bengal, thick leather soles) · **Paabu** (Ladakh, colourful knee-high) · **Kolhapuri chappal** (registered, **Karnataka AND Maharashtra**) · **Katki** (Odisha).
- **Paper and fibre**: **Kashmir papier mache** from **paper pulp with copper sulphate and rice flour**; **Tirukanur papier mache** (registered, Puducherry); **Gambhira masks** (West Bengal, theme **Goddess Kali**; the associated performance at **Malda** during **Chaitra Sankranti**, masks of **neem and fig** wood); **Chhau mask** (registered, West Bengal, **mythological characters**; the dance has **Saraikela, Mayurbhanj and Purulia** styles, and **Mayurbhanj performers wear no masks**); **Ganjifa cards**; **Rambaans** natural fibre craft.

## Folk and tribal traditions beyond the object

- **Chapchar Kut** - **Mizoram**, **March**, **after completion of the locals' most arduous task of jhum operation**.
- **Zo Kutpui** - **Mizoram**; intends to **unify Mizo tribes living in different parts of the world**.
- **Khongjom Parba** - **Manipur**; a **ballad**; theme the **Battle of Khongjom (1891)**. **1891 dates the battle, not the ballad.**
- **Thang-Ta** - **Manipur**; **thang = sword, ta = spear**; a **mock fight sequence**; recorded as influenced by Manipuri dance alongside **Sankirtana**.
- **Rangma** - **Nagaland**, the **war dance**. **Paika** - **Jharkhand and southern Odisha**, wooden **spears and shields**; **Paika means battle**.
- **Karma Naach** - the **Karma** festival; **many tribes of Eastern India, especially the Chota Nagpur Plateau**; a circle dance.
- Disciplines: name the **anchor**, not the meaning; do not date a tradition from its subject; do not homogenise; use the **present tense**; diagnose before asserting decline.

## Floor designs, gender and transmission

- **Floor designs**: drawn during **religious or auspicious family occasions**, **freehand from the centre in concentric geometric patterns**, in **chalk, lime, powdered marble or rice-lime mixtures**. The limiting case - no buyer, no durable object, no market - and therefore largely beyond documentation, certification and registration alike.
- Explicit gender records: **Mizoram** - **women primarily responsible for weaving**; **Manipur** - **phaneks worn by women** at weddings; elsewhere the record names **communities**, not genders.
- Structural reasoning: a stage with **no separate product** has **no separate market** and therefore **no independent price**. **Khadi is the counter-case**, being defined by the **spinning** stage.
- **Knowledge storage, ranked by fragility**: **kin** (irrecoverable - the Aranmula alloy) → **community** (partly recoverable) → **cluster** (recoverable while it lives) → **written record** (to the extent it is complete). Documentation and secrecy are in genuine tension.

## The Geographical Indication

- **Definition**: a **sign** that an item has **originated from a particular region only**; **only registered authorised users** may use the **product name**. **Eligibility**: **produced, processed or prepared** in a specific region.
- **Act**: **Geographical Indications of Goods (Registration & Protection) Act, 1999**; **in force 2003**. **Registry**: **Chennai**, under **DPIIT**, **Ministry of Commerce and Industry**. **Goods only**. **10 years, renewable**. **34 classes**. Prescribed application fee under the Act's rules.
- **Seven prohibited grounds**: deception or confusion · contrary to law · scandalous or obscene · likely to hurt religious susceptibilities · otherwise disentitled to protection in a court · **generic** or lapsed in the country of origin · **literally true but falsely representing another origin**.
- **International**: **Paris Convention**; **WTO TRIPS**. **WIPO's three methods**: ***sui generis*** · **certification and collective marks** · **measures focusing on business practices**. **India uses the *sui generis* route.**
- **Named cases**: **Darjeeling Tea**, India's **first**, **2004-05**; **Kangra Tea**, separately registered as a protected indication in the **European Union**; **Nilambur Teak**, Kerala, **Malappuram** district, **oldest teak plantation in the world**, **world's first Teak Museum**.
- **What it does**: converts a place-linked name from a **common resource** into an **excludable asset held collectively by producers of the region**.
- **What it does not do**: certify quality · secure income, conditions, material or transmission · travel abroad automatically · stop design copying by a producer who avoids the name · last indefinitely · fit a community-held or kin-held technique cleanly.

## The present institutional layer

- Source: a **Rajya Sabha unstarred question on the export of GI-tag registered products, answered 7 August 2026** by the **Minister of State, Ministry of Commerce and Industry**.
- **Registry handholding** through registration, including deficiencies and compliance with the **1999 Act and its Rules**.
- **Application fee reduced by 80 per cent** by **notification dated 3 November 2025**, to make protection more affordable for **artisans, farmers, producer organisations and traditional communities**.
- **MSME Innovative Scheme, IPR component**: **100 per cent reimbursement** of actual registration expenditure, ceiling **Rs. 2 lakh per registered GI**, covering professional and government fees, application preparation, prosecution, hearings, **GI logo design** and laboratory testing; support through **Intellectual Property Facilitation Centres**.
- **25 GI Mahotsavs** across States; **164 programmes sanctioned under One District One GI**; participation facilitated in international exhibitions; promotion through fairs, pavilions, media, transport-hub branding and dedicated retail outlets.
- **Office of the Development Commissioner (Handicrafts), Ministry of Textiles**: assistance of **up to Rs. 1.50 lakh** towards **enforcement of registered GIs** and **reimbursement of legal expenses**.
- **No dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged products** - the citable statement that licenses a refusal to quote an export figure.

## Answer construction

- **Four controls**, run on every sentence and again on the finished script: **attribution** (name the operation and the community; supply nothing withheld), **chronology** (state the number, then its noun), **geography** (use the record's specificity; reproduce multiplicity; classify the word as place, operation or object type), **terminology** (weave against surface; yarn against cloth resist; single against double ikat; documentation against certification against registration).
- **Directive map**: nine demand types, each with what it really tests and what not to write.
- **Mark scaling**: **10** - thesis, precise definition, two named cases, one limitation, verdict. **15** - add the chain, three or four cases, the full mechanism, a livelihood limit. **20** - add the historical production layer, ecology, gender and appropriation, international protection with a named case, and the institutional route.
- **Four theses**: narrow instrument · chain · continuity-with-change · knowledge storage.
- **Four prohibited narratives**: one-state exclusivity · fabricated symbolism · fixed tribal authenticity · the generic dying craft. Plus the **unsupported registration year**.
- The verdict is never "it worked" or "it failed"; it is **which link the instrument reaches, and which remains unattended**.

## Bounded-research declaration

This session was generated under the bounded-research rule. The substantive base is the complete core owner, the complete advanced owner and one core OCR art and culture source. External work was confined to three declared purposes.

**(a) PYQ verification and routing.** The routing ledgers and the locally held official papers named in the manifest above, the Preliminary 2018 paper being **Set C** and the Preliminary 2026 paper **Set A**. The **provisional 2026 Set-A key was not consulted**. The reproducing source prints its own answer keys beneath each of its reproduced question lists; those keys were **encountered during extraction and were not carried into this session**. No official Preliminary key for 2018 or 2015 is held in this repository.

**(b) One bounded material-fact pass.** The list of items was fixed before searching and is reproduced here in full, with the outcome of each.

| Item | Why it was on the list | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| The region and, where available, the community attribution for **Puthukkuli**, **Sujni** and **Uppada Jamdani** | all three are named in a directly owned 2018 Preliminary item, and the core OCR source prints only the question, not the attributions; their omission would leave an owned item wholly unsupported | **Resolved.** Puthukkuli is the shawl of the **Toda** community of the **Nilgiris, Tamil Nadu**; Sujni is an embroidery tradition of **Bihar**, associated with **Muzaffarpur**; Uppada Jamdani is a saree tradition of **Uppada, coastal Andhra Pradesh**, within the Jamdani technique family. Recorded in Lessons 9, 8 and 5 respectively, as **region and community only**. **No registration year, process description or motif vocabulary is asserted for any of them**, because the substantive base does not supply them and the bounded pass did not establish them from a dated registry source |
| The institutional character of the **Oeko-Tex** textile product-safety certification named in the directly owned 2026 Preliminary item | the core owner records this as an explicit bounded gap - no local source carries the issuing body's nature, its scope or its standards - and the owned item cannot be approached at all without it | **Resolved at the institutional level only.** It is a **voluntary, privately administered** certification run by an **international association of independent textile research and testing institutes**; its principal standard tests textile articles for **harmful substances**, with permitted limits graded by **intended use and degree of skin contact**, and certificates subject to **periodic renewal**. Recorded in Lesson 11, where it is described institutionally and left unnamed so that the teaching carries no answer inference; the scheme is named only in the neutral reproduced demand description and in the declared gaps. **No limit value, product-class detail beyond the skin-contact principle, fee, certificate duration, Indian producer's certification status or commercial-effect evidence is asserted** |
| The **registered geographical scope of Phulkari** | the core record's registered-goods row for Phulkari is partially legible in the held copy, showing "Punjab, Haryana" followed by a broken continuation line, while the source's separate prose passage records the practice in Punjab, Haryana and Delhi; quoting either alone would misstate the instrument | **Resolved.** The row's continuation reads **Rajasthan**, and the official register records the registered indication's geographical scope as **Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan**. Both records are now printed side by side in Lesson 8 and harmonised across the file: the **practice** passage gives Punjab, Haryana and Delhi; the **registration** covers Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan. **No registration year is asserted**, because no dated registry extract was obtained |
| The **registered form of Telia Rumal** | the core record marks a Telia Rumal entry as registered and places it at Chirala, Andhra Pradesh; attaching a registration to that town would be a status error of exactly the kind this topic teaches against | **Resolved as a separation.** The registered indication is **Puttapaka Telia Rumal**, under **application number 599**, for **Puttapaka village, Nalgonda district, Telangana**. The historic association with **Chirala, Andhra Pradesh** is retained as a statement about where the cloth has been made; **the registration is not attached to Chirala** anywhere in this session. **No registration year is asserted**: reported certificate dates differ between accounts and no dated registry extract was obtained, and the conflict is recorded as a discrepancy |
| The **State of the Pembarthi metal craft** | the core record's brass table gives the craft and its function but no State column, leaving a registered craft without a region attribution | **Resolved.** Pembarthi metal craft is registered for **Pembarthi, in Telangana**. The State is now carried in every occurrence, including the region-attribution control in Lesson 19. No registration year is asserted |
| The **town to name for Bidri** | the core record prints "Bidri village of Karnataka", a form that does not match the familiar town name and leaves a learner without a usable place | **Resolved.** The craft is standardly associated with **Bidar, Karnataka**, and Bidar is now the town given to the learner. The printed variant is retained and recorded as a discrepancy rather than deleted |
| A **named cane or rattan craft** owned by this session's substantive base | the topic's scope promises cane alongside bamboo, and a promised material with no content would be a coverage gap | **Not resolved, and declared.** The core owner, the advanced owner and the core OCR art source carry **no cane or rattan craft, centre, community or product**. Lesson 14 therefore teaches the **material and process boundary** between bamboo and cane in full and states the missing example explicitly. No cane craft is imported from any outside list |
| Whether a **current, dated, cumulative national total of registered geographical indications** is available from a dated registry extract or an identified departmental parliamentary reply, as the core owner's date-control rule requires | the core owner records a total dated July 2025 and forbids any later total that is not so sourced; an undated figure in teaching would breach that control | **Not resolved, by design.** The parliamentary reply obtained answers the five-year question by referring to a published annexure listing geographical indications registered **during the last five financial years**. A five-year registration list is **not** a cumulative national total, and this session therefore asserts **no current national total** and places no total in the teaching at all. The core owner's dated July 2025 figure remains the only dated total held, and it is not restated as a present figure |
| Whether the **application fee** printed in the core sources remains current | Lesson 17's statutory block would otherwise carry a superseded rupee figure | **Resolved as a change, not as a new figure.** The same parliamentary reply records that, by **notification dated 3 November 2025**, the fee payable for filing applications and related proceedings was **reduced by 80 per cent**. This session therefore states that the Act's rules prescribe an application fee and that it was reduced by 80 per cent from that date, and **prints no current rupee amount**, because the reply states a percentage rather than a resulting figure |
| Reported **export values** for GI-tagged goods | an export figure would have made the institutional lesson concrete | **Resolved in the negative, and the negative is used.** The answering ministry states that **there is no dedicated database exclusively for exports of GI-tagged product**. No export figure appears anywhere in this session, and Lesson 18 uses the absence itself as a finding |

**(c) One bounded current linkage.** Exactly one is claimed, file-wide, in **Lesson 18**: the Rajya Sabha unstarred question on the export of GI-tag registered products, answered on **7 August 2026** by the Minister of State in the Ministry of Commerce and Industry. Every other pre-teach checklist in this session records the absence of a genuine linkage truthfully. The 2022 textile-documentation record treated in Lesson 11 is used as a **dated historical fact from the core source**, not as a current linkage.

## Discrepancies recorded rather than resolved

| # | Discrepancy | How this session handles it |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | **Banaras brocade** is placed inside the source's **embroidery** section and is described there as **zari embroidery work**, while the same source elsewhere treats brocade as a woven structure and describes Tanchoi brocade as a weaving technique involving one or more wefts | Lesson 8 prints the placement, states the tension explicitly, and adopts the safe examination position that Banaras brocade is a **supplementary-weft woven** tradition. The conflict is recorded, not resolved |
| 2 | **Bidri** is located in the core record at "Bidri village of Karnataka", a place-name form that differs from the familiar name of the Karnataka town associated with the craft | Lesson 13 gives the learner the standard association - **Bidar, Karnataka** - and prints the record's variant beside it. The **State is consistent** across both entries; the village form is recorded, not deleted |
| 3 | **Telia Rumal**: the core record marks the entry as registered and places it at **Chirala, Andhra Pradesh**, while the register protects **Puttapaka Telia Rumal** of **Puttapaka, Nalgonda district, Telangana** | Lesson 6 separates the two explicitly - historic association against registered indication - and the session attaches the registration to Puttapaka only. **Reported certificate dates differ between accounts**, so **no registration year is asserted** anywhere |
| 4 | **Phulkari** appears with two different geographical statements in the same source: the prose gives **Punjab, Haryana and Delhi** as the practice, while the registered-goods row - partially legible in the held copy - gives **Punjab, Haryana and Rajasthan**, which the official register confirms | Lesson 8 prints both, names what each is a statement about, and the rest of the file quotes whichever is relevant to the question being asked. Neither is presented as correcting the other |
| 5 | The **phanek** is described in one passage as a **wraparound skirt worn by Manipuri women** and in another as a **traditional shawl** whose borders carry muga-thread embroidery | Lessons 4, 8 and 10 print both descriptions side by side and record the difference; no argument in this session depends on which is preferred |
| 6 | **Kalamkari** is defined in the core source as **hand painting on fabrics using vegetable dyes**, while the core owner describes the tradition as **hand-drawn or block-printed** cotton with **mordant and resist** processes across Andhra Pradesh and Telangana centres | Lesson 7 leads with the source's definition, records the wider formulation as covering a hand-drawn and a block-printed branch, and declines to name individual centre-styles, which no held source prints |
| 7 | The **Chhau mask** is recorded as a registered craft of **West Bengal**, while the **Chhau dance** is recorded with **three styles across Jharkhand, Odisha and West Bengal**, one of which - Mayurbhanj - uses **no masks at all** | Lesson 14 prints both records, keeps the object and the performance distinct, and states the mismatch in extent rather than collapsing it |
| 8 | The **Khongjom Parba** ballad appears as **Parba** in the source's folk-music table and in one reproduced question list, and as **Parva** in another reproduction of the same item; **Thang-Ta** appears with varying capitalisation and hyphenation | The PYQ section and Lesson 15 record the variants and state that they are recorded, not resolved |
| 9 | The **application fee** printed in the core sources predates a notified reduction of 80 per cent | Lesson 17 states that the Act's rules prescribe a fee; Lesson 18 records the reduction with its notification date; no current rupee amount is printed anywhere |
| 10 | The core sources carry a **dated total of registered goods as of July 2025** and a **leading-State figure as of April 2025**, while the current reply refers only to a five-year registration annexure | This session prints **no total and no leading-State figure in the teaching at all**, records the reason here, and treats every changing total as requiring its own date and source |
| 11 | Several table entries in the held copy of the core source carry **truncated description columns**, so that a craft's name, place and registration status are legible while its full descriptive text is not | Wherever this occurs, this session records the **name, place and status** and does not reconstruct the missing description. Four standard word-completions were nonetheless necessary to make an entry usable and are declared here: the Kutch weaving community as **Vankar**; the second State in the panja durrie entry as **Rajasthan**; the Ladakh area in the Thigma entry as **Nubra**; and the Channapatna timber as **ivory wood**. No other description in this session is completed from a partial line |
| 12 | **Kolhapuri chappal** is recorded with **Karnataka and Maharashtra** in one row, while a separate passage associates the product principally with Maharashtra | Lesson 14 prints the two-state row as the governing record, notes the second passage, and uses the entry as the session's standing refutation of one-state exclusivity |

## Declared gaps - material a candidate may expect that this session does not supply

1. **No process description, motif vocabulary or registration year for Sujni embroidery.** The region and its associated area are recorded; nothing further is supplied or inferred.
2. **No dated process account or registration year for the Puthukkuli shawl.** The community and region are recorded; nothing further.
3. **No fibre specification, motif vocabulary or registration year for Uppada Jamdani.** The centre and the technique-family placement are recorded; nothing further.
4. **No named centre-styles within the Kalamkari tradition.** The held sources do not print them, and they are not supplied from elsewhere.
5. **No named cane or rattan craft, centre, community or product.** The owners used in this session carry none. Lesson 14 teaches the bamboo-against-cane material and process boundary and declares this gap rather than importing an example.
6. **No limit values, product classes, fees, certificate durations or Indian producer certification statuses** for the **Oeko-Tex** textile product-safety scheme named in the owned 2026 item and treated institutionally in Lesson 11. Only its institutional character is recorded.
7. **No current cumulative national total of registered geographical indications**, and no leading-State figure. A five-year registration annexure is not a cumulative total, and no dated registry extract was obtained.
8. **No export value, artisan count, employment figure, income figure or production volume** anywhere in this session. The answering ministry records that no dedicated export database for the category exists, and no other such figure is held.
9. **No registration year for any craft other than Darjeeling Tea's recorded 2004-05** - including Phulkari, Puttapaka Telia Rumal, Pembarthi and Bidri, for none of which a dated registry extract was obtained.
10. **No composition date for the Khongjom Parba ballad.** Only the dated battle that is its theme is recorded.
11. **No record of the medieval head-dress** named in one reproduced objective item, and **no record of the three traditions and communities** named in another; both gaps are declared in the PYQ section and the second item is reproduced in full for that reason.
12. **No full list of the fifty documented textile crafts.** Four entries are printed in the held copy; the remainder are not supplied.
13. **No descriptive detail for Ganjifa cards or Rambaans natural-fibre craft** beyond their appearance among other famous Indian handicrafts.
14. **No pottery ware-sequence, firing-phase or typological chronology**; routed to the sculpture-and-pottery owner.
15. **No architectural, iconographic or art-historical treatment** of any monument named in the stone section; routed to the architecture and sculpture owners.
16. **No performance grammar** for Chhau, Gambhira, Thang-Ta, Rangma, Paika or Karma Naach; routed to the performance owners.
17. **No causal economic argument** about colonial deindustrialisation, and **no sociological proposition** about diversity and marginality; both routed to their primary owners with the contribution this topic makes stated explicitly.

## Source-use statement

The core owner supplied this topic's ownership boundaries, the essential vocabulary, the material classification, the craft-ecology chain, the regional craft-textile distinction table, the complete North-East handloom evidence bank with its eri-silk and community records, the directive-and-demand map, the thesis options, the mark-scaled structures, the trap set and the factual-risk and dynamic-status controls. The advanced owner supplied the craft-ecology chain as an analytical device, the narrow-instrument thesis, the comparative instrument framework, the medieval production layer with its karkhanas and putting-out relations, the continuity-through-market-change formulation, the attribution and chronology trap set, the subject boundaries and its own open caution on the changing registered-goods total. The core OCR art and culture source supplied the paragraph-level record for every material class - glassware, cloth handicrafts, ivory, silver, clay and pottery, bronze, other metals, leather, wooden work, toys, stoneware and floor designs - together with the tie-and-dye, embroidery, weaving, shawl, saree, durrie, pottery, brass, footwear, timber, toy and other-handicraft tables, the Khadi and tribal-storage boxes, the 2022 textile-documentation passage, the complete geographical-indications chapter, and the folk-tradition passages carrying the three traditions routed to this topic. The PYQ ledgers and the locally held official papers supplied ownership, exact structure and key status. The single current linkage supplied one dated official record of the present institutional layer, including the statement that a particular category of export data does not exist.

No artifact under the permanently excluded package directory was read, searched, cited or relied upon at any stage of this generation, and no learner-v2 artifact was used. Qdrant was not required and did not block or delay this session.
